Project development objectives Ref; PAD B.2, Technical Annex 3 
The objectives of the project (APL-1) are to (i) improve sewerage services in the M-R River Basin and 
other parts of the Province and City of Buenos Aires by expanding transport and treatment capacity; (ii) 
support a reduction of industrial discharges to the M-R River, through the provision of industrial 
conversion grants to small and medium enterprises; (iii) promote improved decision-making for 
environmentally-sustainable land use and drainage planning, and to pilot urban drainage and land use 
investments, in the M-R River Basin; and (iv) strengthen ACUMAR’s institutional framework for 
ongoing and sustainable clean-up of the M-R River Basin. 
Project Description (Ref. PAD B.3.a, Technical Annex 4) 
The proposed project has four components grouped under two broad macro-components: 
Macro-component 1: Sanitation 
(i) Sanitation: Investments in sanitation infrastructure including the Left Bank collector, the Baja 
Costanera bypass collector, the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant, inflow and effluent pumping 
stations at the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant, the Riachuelo outfall, the Industrial collector, and 
the Right Bank collector. 
Independent technical supervision of the works listed above; data collection, analyses and modeling in 
support of engineering designs; and capacity development activities in support of the preparation of the 
engineering designs under this component. 
Macro-component 2: Support for Industrial Pollution Abatement, Environmental Territorial 
Management, and Institutional Strengthening 
(ii) Industrial Pollution Abatement: Technical assistance to improve environmental monitoring and 
enforcement of environmental targets for selected industrial activities, as well as financing of cleaner 
production investments to enhance and support environmental compliance among polluting small and 
medium size enterprises; 
(iii) Environmental Territorial Management: Technical assistance for environmental land use planning 
and financing for pilot urban and drainage works; and 
*.

<<PAGE 8>>
(iv) Institutional Strengthening and Project Management: Financing for technical studies to support 
project implementation (components 1, 2 and 3); a communication strategy to inform key stakeholders; 
project management activities; and support for the institutional strengthening of ACUMAR.

assessment acceptable to the Bank for the Right Bank collector, or a suitable alternative approved 
by the Bank, and for the industrial collector (including its pumping station), and thereafter prepare 
and implement an environmental management plan for these civil works. 
(iii) Resettlement is not expected under APL1. Considering the complex nature of works under 
Component 1 and that specific subprojects under Components 2 and 3 are not known (matching 
grants for SMEs and infrastructure investments, respectively), in case resettlement becomes 
necessary, the Borrower through SAyDS shall, or shall cause AySA to, prepare a resettlement plan 
acceptable to the Bank consistent with the Resettlement Policy Framework, as the case may be; and 
thereafter carry out the pertinent resettlement plan in accordance with its terms. 
(iv) The Borrower, through SAyDS, shall: (i) cause AySA to carry out component 1 of the Project in 
accordance with the provisions of the Project Environmental Management Plan, the Ay SA 
Environmental Management Plan, and the Operational Manual; (ii) carry out components 2 and 3 of 
the Project in accordance with the provisions of the Project Environmental Management Plan, the 
Environmental Management Framework for component 2 of the Project, the Environmental 
Management Framework for component 3 of the Project, and the Operational Manual; and (iii) carry 
component 4 of the Project in accordance with the provisions of the Operational Manual. 
The Borrower, through SAyDS, shall cause AYSA to prepare and furnish to the Bank an 
environmental management plan for the Berazategui sewage treatment plant not later than six (6) 
months after the Effective Date. 
(vi) The Borrower, through SAyDS, shall cause AySA to, prior to the commencement of the main works 
under component 1 (Left Bank collector, the Baja Costanera bypass collector, the Riachuelo 
preliminary treatment plant, inflow and effluent pumping stations at the Riachuelo preliminary 
treatment plant, the Riachuelo outfall, the Industrial collector, and the Right Bank collector), hire 
one or more firms (with qualifications and experience, and under terms of reference, all acceptable 
to the Bank) to supervise construction.

A. STRATEGIC CONTEXT AND RATIONALE 
Country and sector issues 
1. The Matanza-Riachuelo (M-R), a tributary of the Rio de la Plata (La Plata River), is the 
most contaminated river basin in Argentina’ and the most visible environmental issue in the 
country. Over the past hundred years, the M-R River Basin has been used as a sewage sink for 
the entire city of Buenos Aires. Pollution levels have increased steadily with urbanization in the 
metropolitan area of Buenos Aires and accompanying industrial growth within the basin. It is 
estimated that more than 4,000 industrial facilities are located in the lower and middle sections of 
the basin. Many of these industries discharge untreated effluents into the drainage system or 
directly into the M-R River. In addition to high levels of organic pollution, these discharges 
contribute toxic contaminants such as heavy metals from petrochemical industries, tanneries, and 
meat processing facilities. The acute environmental and social degradation of the M-R River 
Basin is the result of limited public infrastructure investment, poor environmental management, 
and lack of adequate urban and industrial planning. This situation has created serious health 
risks, especially for the highly vulnerable social groups who have demanded government action 
to address these deteriorating conditions. The Fernandez de Kirchner administration has publicly 
committed to making concrete progress in the clean-up of the basin. 
2. The basin is home to Argentina’s largest concentrations of urban poor. Of the 
approximately 3.5 million inhabitants in the basin, 1.2 million live below the poverty line. An 
estimated 10 percent of the total population in the M-R River Basin lives in informal settlements, 
often in flood-prone areas and/or near open garbage dumps. The poorest populations living 
alongside the river are in constant contact with numerous contaminants ranging from untreated 
organic waste to toxic industrial chemicals. The M-R River also floods frequently because of 
high flows in the rainy season and high water levels in La Plata River. This flooding spreads 
highly polluted waters into informal settlements, exposing the inhabitants to further 
contamination. 
3. Currently, the Buenos Aires Metropolitan Area’s sewage (domestic and some industrial) 
is collected and transported away from the city to the Berazategui short outfall on the La Plata 
River2 downstream from the city of Buenos Aires and outside the city limits (see figure 1) in the 
province of Buenos Aires by way of three main sewage pipes (cloacas troncales mdximas, or 
CTM). This conveyance system initially used to discharge directly to the M-R River. However, 
in the 1950s, the system was extended to Berazategui, crossing the M-R River and finally 
discharging to the La Plata River. Frequently, insufficient capacity or failures in the system 
generate an overflow of the CTMs leading to direct discharges to the M-R River. For these 
reasons, significant expansion of wastewater collection and treatment for final disposal into La 
Plata River is needed. 
’ The Matanza is the upper part of the river, and the Riachuelo the lower. The name changes when the Matanza 
reaches the City of Buenos Aires. The M-R River Basin discharges into La Plata River near the well known La Boca 
(“Mouth of the River”) neighborhood. 
*La Plata River is known to be the widest estuary in the world; the river’s average flow is 17,000 m3/second and its 
average width is 50 km. 
1 
<<PAGE 11>>
4. Preliminary data suggest that the industrial sector is the largest source of organic 
contamination3. In addition, industries are also a significant source of toxic pollutants, including 
heavy metals. While environmental standards, as well as maximum permissible limits for 
effluent and emission discharges exist on paper4, evidence shows that compliance is minimal as a 
consequence of unclear responsibilities across government jurisdictions, weak enforcement 
systems, and inadequate monitoring and control capacity in terms of human resources and 
technical expertise. 
Figure 1. Schematic presentation of the Existing Buenos Aires sewerage system 
5. Data on service coverage in the M-R River Basin’ indicate that 35 percent of the 
population does not have access-to safe drinking water and 65 percent are not connected to the 
sewerage system.6 The situation is much more extreme in the poorer municipalities such as 
Esteban Echeverria, where, according to the last census in 2001, about 87 percent of the residents 
lacked access to sewerage and 63 percent lacked access to piped drinking water.7 Furthermore, 
65 percent of the water supply for the service area of the Argentina Water and Sanitation utility 
(Agua y Saneamientos Argentinos S.A., or AySA) is drawn from two intakes in the La Plata 
River, 1-2 km off the coast at Bernal and Palermo. In time, the Bernal intake could face

pollution load, the Government has emphasized the need for additional data collection and modeling to validate 
these results.

sewage networks, surface waters, and soil absorption; as well as parameters for air quality relating to emissions. 
Estimates are based on the percentage of the population of the 14 municipalities within the Basin boundaries. Total 
population of the 14 municipalities is 4.8 million while the total estimated population within the Basin is 3.5 million. 
Buenos Aires is served by two water utilities: Agua y Saneamientos Argentinos S.A (AySA) and ABSA.

<<PAGE 12>>
contamination risks from discharges of the M-R River and the existing Berazategui outfall into 
La Plata River (see Annex lo), raising significant health and environmental concerns that affect 
the entire population of the basin. 
6. In recent decades, the Government of Argentina (GOA) has launched important initiatives 
to address flooding and reduce pollution levels in the M-R River Basin, but implementation has 
continually been postponed for legal, economic, or political reasons. In the mid- 1990~~ the 
Government completed a comprehensive M-R River Basin Environmental Management Plan 
(Plan de Gestibn Ambiental y de Manejo de la Cuenca Hidrica Matanza Riachuelo, PGA) and 
received a US$250 million loan from the Inter American Development Bank (IADB) through the 
Environmental Recovery of the Matanza-Riachuelo project to help finance its implementation. 
However, 12 years later, the IADB loan has disbursed only US$lO million and is only now 
committing another US$90 million for secondary networks and a treatment plant expansion 
(AySA’s Sudoeste treatment plant). The GOA concluded that the lack of a clear mandate and 
accountability for action combined with an inadequate institutional and legal framework to 
coordinate the involvement of the relevant jurisdictions was a major obstacle to implementing 
the M-R PGA. Therefore, in spite of these early efforts, the social and environmental degradation 
of the M-R River Basin has worsened over the past decade. 
7. Given worsening conditions in the M-R River Basin, national governments have made its 
clean-up a high priority over the past several years. At the same time, a high-profile lawsuit’ was 
filed by people affected by environmental degradation against the national government, Buenos 
Aires province, the Government of the City of Buenos Aires, and 44 industries in 2004. In 2006, 
the Supreme Court issued a ruling calling on the Government to put in place a concrete action 
plan to improve environmental conditions in the basin. As a result, the Integrated Cleanup Plan 
for the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin (Plan Integral de Saneamiento de la Cuenca Matanza- 
Riachuelo, PISA) was designed and agreed by all applicable jurisdictions and AySA. While the 
PISA builds on the earlier PGA, its scope is significantly wider (see table 1 below). A river basin 
authority (ACUMAR) was created by national law to implement this plan. In July 2008, the 
Supreme Court issued its final ruling establishing general criteria to ensure that the PISA is 
executed and setting a timeline for its main milestones.

8. The new government strategy. The new approach adopted by the GOA to solve the 
pollution issues in the M-R River Basin is different from previous clean-up attempts and is based 
on two concepts: (i) the establishment of a River Basin Authority that brings together all major 
actors in the basin and (ii) the progressive elimination of identified point source discharges of 
municipal and industrial wastes into the M-R River. 
(i) Establishment of a River Basin Authority. The federal government concluded that one of the 
main reasons for the limited impact of previous clean-up attempts for the basin was the lack of 
an institutional framework to provide a consistent policy approach, interjurisdictional 
coordination9, and an enforcement plan. As a result, Law 26168 created a new river basin 
authority, Autoridad de Cuenca Matanza-Riachuelo (ACUMAR), which was designated to (i) 
implement a comprehensive action plan (Plan Integral de Saneamiento, or PISA) including 
structural and non-structural measures, (ii) coordinate and harmonize activities, and (iii) control 
and monitor environmental compliance. By law, the Secretary of SAyDS is the president of 
ACUMAR and ACUMAR's governing body also includes representatives from the city and 
province of Buenos Aires. (For a detailed description of ACUMAR, see Annex 11B). 
(ii) Progressive elimination of identified point source discharges into the M-R River. The low 
flow characteristics of this urban river make it unsuitable for receiving residential or industrial 
discharges. Thus, a policy on the progressive elimination of point source discharges is being 
adopted for domestic and industrial effluents in both the lower and middle-upper basin which are 
inconsistent with the uses and objectives established by ACUMAR for the M-R River. The 
The basin is covered by the jurisdictions of 14 municipalities and the City of Buenos Aires. 
4 
<<PAGE 14>>
change to an approach of progressive elimination of point source discharges is fully justified 
given the low self-purification capacity of the M-R River and the severity of its pollution. 
9. As part of its action plan to implement PISA, as mandated by the Supreme Court, the 
GOA has requested Bank support for parts of its long-term plan (PISA) through the proposed 
Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project. The proposed operation goes 
beyond a narrowly-focused sanitation infrastructure project by taking a more comprehensive 
urban environmental recovery approach, through components aimed at gradually upgrading 
environmental conditions as well as urban infrastructure in the basin. Integrated industrial and 
urban infrastructure with sufficient capacity is needed to alleviate pollution in the entire M-R 
River Basin and to adequately respond to the concomitant social and environmental problems. 
10. 
basic components grouped under two broad macro-components: 
The proposed Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project includes four 
Macro-component 1 : Sanitation 
(i) Investments in sanitation infrastructure including the Left Bank collector, the Baja 
Costanera bypass collector, the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant, inflow and effluent 
pumping stations at the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant, the Riachuelo outfall, the 
Industrial collector, and the Right Bank collector. 
Independent technical supervision of the works listed above; data collection, analyses and 
modeling in support of engineering designs; and capacity development activities in support of 
the preparation of the engineering designs under this component. 
Macro-component 2: Support for Industrial Pollution Abatement, Environmental Territorial 
Management, and Institutional Strengthening 
(ii) Technical assistance to improve environmental monitoring and enforcement of 
environmental targets for industrial activities, as well as financing of cleaner production 
investments to enhance and support environmental compliance among polluting small and 
medium size enterprises; 
(iii) Technical assistance for land use and urban planning and financing for pilot urban and 
drainage investments; and 
(iv) Financing for technical studies to support project implementation; a communication strategy 
to inform key stakeholders; project management activities; and support for the institutional 
strengthening of ACUMAR. 
2. Rationale for Bank involvement 
11. The Matanza-Riachuelo river basin clean-up effort is large and complex, not only 
because of its scale but also because of the institutional coordination and capacity required for 
achieving its long-term objectives. The World Bank is well-positioned to assist in this type of 
project because of its convening power and capability to actively engage experienced specialists 
from its environmental, water, urban, and social units, recruit independent international 
expertise, and leverage additional resources (e.g., trust funds) to support project preparation. 
5 
<<PAGE 15>>
12. In this capacity, the Bank has been collaborating with the GOA in defining the concept for 
the project. In November 2006, at the request of SAyDS, the Bank supported an international 
workshop to review the 1995 M-R PGA and evaluate independent technical alternatives for the 
river-basin clean-up. The workshop recommended the following: (i) redesigning the pollution 
management strategy in the M-R River Basin, taking into account the basin’s dynamic 
interaction with the metropolitan area of Buenos Aires and La Plata River, (ii) revising the water 
quality objective proposed in the PGA and adopting an approach of progressive elimination of 
point source discharges for the M-R River, (iii) reviewing the priorities of investments in both 
the PGA and the city of Buenos Aires’ pollution management program, and (iv) aggressively 
addressing the social problems of the poor in the M-R River Basin. 
13. The long-term investment program envisaged under the proposed project is consistent 
with the Bank’s strategy to establish a long-term partnership with the GOA focused on 
environmentally sustainable infrastructure. The Bank, which is already financing a major flood 
control project in the City of Buenos Aires and a multi-sectoral infrastructure project in the 
Province of Buenos Aires, is also preparing a new coordinated operation for the sustainable 
conversion of the industrial sector and is supporting an ongoing solid waste management project. 
The latter two projects may finance additional investments in the basin area. From an 
institutional point of view, the GOA believes that support provided by a multilateral institution 
like the Bank to the new basin authority, ACUMAR, and to the Secretariat of the Environment 
(SAyDS) will provide the necessary financing and continuity to help carry out the ambitious 
program proposed for the clean-up of the M-R River Basin. 
14. Based on its knowledge and experience in dealing with large and complex contracts, the 
Bank can also contribute substantively to the planning and oversight of the highly challenging 
procurement plan (including pre-qualification and detailed technical specifications) designed for 
this operation. 
3. Higher-level objectives to which the project contributes 
15. The overall and specific objectives of this project are consistent with the Country 
Assistance Strategy (CAS) approved in June 2006, which seeks to reduce the extent and severity 
of poverty in Argentina by focusing on (i) the promotion of sustained economic growth with 
equity, (ii) social inclusion, and (iii) improved governance by strengthening institutions and 
increasing accountability and transparency. 
16. The project is strongly aligned with all three CAS objectives, by (i) addressing a major 
environmental issue with substantial impact on poverty reduction (thereby contributing to the 
social inclusion pillar); and (ii) promoting a broad partnership between national and subnational 
governments, private sector firms and civil society as well the multi-jurisdictional basin agency, 
ACUMAR, (thereby contributing to the governance pillar). The two-stage APL program is 
expected to contribute to specific CAS outcomes, notably (a) expanding sewage coverage in 
areas of high sanitary and environmental vulnerability; (b) reducing flood risk in selected urban 
areas; and (c) deepening the emerging investment partnership in favor of institutional 
strengthening in selected areas of public sector management. The poorest and most vulnerable 
population will benefit from a better environment and improved coverage of public services, 
which in turn will lead to better health conditions. Looking forward, the proposed project is also 
6 
<<PAGE 16>>
consistent with the proposed 20 10-201 2 Country Partnership Strategy for Argentina and the 
Bank’s increasing emphasis on partnering with Argentine authorities on transformational 
operations that bring the Bank’s experience and long-term commitment to bear in helping to 
solve complex problems. 
B. PROJECT DESCRIPTION 
17. The government’s medium to long term objective under the PISA is the progressive 
elimination of point source discharges to the M-R River, which would result in water quality 
improvements in a 15 - 20 year timeframe. The proposed project is a building block towards this 
goal by supporting the following integrated activities: (i) the construction of a collector along the 
left bank of the M-R River (Colector Murgen Izquierdo, or CMI), diversion of the wastewater 
collected by this collector to the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant and discharging its 
effluent to the La Plata River via an effective sub-aquatic outfall, (ii) the construction of a 
collector from the upper M-R River Basin along the right bank of the M-R River (Colector 
Murgen Derechu, or CMD) which will convey approximately 1.5 m3/s of wastewater from the 
upper basin as well as wastewater from the Sudoeste, El Jaguel, and Laferrere drainage basins in 
the southwest of the Buenos Aires Metropolitan area to the Third Trunk Sewage Pipe (Tercera 
Cloaca Troncal Maxima), (iii) technical assistance for designing an industrial control program 
reducing industrial (both organic and toxic) discharges to the M-R River by connecting these 
industries to the AySA sewerage network after suitable treatment of toxic and organic effluents 
in line with existing standards, inter alia by means of an industrial collector, and (iv) design and 
implementation of a comprehensive inter-jurisdictional land use plan including the provision of 
basic infrastructure services. 
1. Lending instrument 
18. The proposed operation will be structured as an Adaptable Program Loan (APL). The 
choice of an APL allows for flexibility in the implementation schedule within and between 
components to (i) enable adjustments in the pace of implementation depending on the evolution 
of the fiscal framework, (ii) take into consideration different preparation requirements and 
implementation times among different components of the project, and (iii) build the required 
institutional capacity to implement the project and strengthen coordination mechanisms among 
the different institutions involved in project execution. 
19. Project amount and duration. The project comprises two overlapping phases to be 
executed over the 2009-2019 period, each phase to be implemented over 6 years. The first phase 
(APL-1) will extend from 2009 to 2015 for a total loan amount of up to US$840 million. The 
second phase (APL-2), for a total loan amount of $1,160 million, is expected to begin after 
achievement of defined targets, overlapping in time with APL-1 as new interventions are ready 
to be implemented. 
2. Program objectives and phases 
20. The overall development objective of the proposed APL program supports the 
Government’s Integrated Basin Cleanup while simultaneously improving sanitary conditions 
along the banks of La Plata River and providing a long-term and cost-effective solution for safe 
7 
<<PAGE 17>>
disposal of wastewater from the Buenos Aires Metropolitan Area (AySA's concession area). The 
two stage APL program that contributes to this objective and the allocation of works and 
activities under each APL has been specifically designed to ensure that APL-1 can be free- 
standing, with no stranded assets at the end of the first stage.

3. Project development objective and key indicators 
24. The project (APL-1) development objectives contribute to the overall program 
development objective by (i) improving sewerage services in the M-R River Basin and other 
parts of the Province and City of Buenos Aires by expanding transport and treatment capacity; 
(ii) supporting a reduction of industrial discharges to the M-R River, through the provision of 
industrial conversion grants to small and medium enterprises; (iii) promoting improved decision- 
making for environmentally-sustainable land use and drainage planning, and piloting urban 
drainage and land use investments, in the M-R River Basin; and (iv) strengthening ACUMAR’s

4. Project components 
25. The project will contribute to the environmental recovery of the M-R River Basin 
through coordinated action under two macro-components: the first comprises a sanitation 
component, while the second includes components on industrial pollution abatement, 
environmental territorial management, and technical assistance and project management. A 
detailed project description is provided in Annex 4. 
Macro-component 1: Sanitation 
Component 1: Sanitation (US$ 1,309 million total, US$694 million in loan proceeds) 
26. Over the course of APL-1 and APL-2, this component aims to progressively collect, 
convey, treat, and dispose of wastewater from the M-R River Basin along with wastewater from 
the AySA concession area. Sewage works included in this component are essential to improving 
the coverage and quality of sanitation services in the M-R River Basin and the entire AySA 
service area, to enhancing water quality, and to expanding the potential uses of the water bodies 
The existing drainage masterplan is known in Spanish as the Plan Hidrico for the M-R River Basin. 
10 
<<PAGE 20>>
in the M-R River Basin and southern coastal strip of La Plata River. The infrastructure system 
selected is based on the principle of intercepting most of the domestic and industrial wastewater 
in the AySA concession area (except wastewater that is diverted to the North and Hurlingham 
treatment plants, which discharge their effluents to the Reconquista River), as well as some flows 
from the M-R River Basin outside AySA's concession area. This entire wastewater flow will be 
diverted to La Plata River through two new sub-aquatic outfalls, (the Riachuelo and Berazategui 
outfalls) after preliminary treatment. Specific sub-components financed under this project are 
detailed further below. Figure 2 schematically represents the proposed infrastructure works. 
Figure 2: Schematic Presentation of Component 1 
27. Construction/extension of collectors and complementary work. Under APL- 1, waste 
water collection and conveyance works will include (i) the Left Bank collector, with a total 
length of approximately 12 ktl.1, in the lower part of the basin (the diameter varies between 0.8 m 
and 2.9 m); (ii) the Baja Costanera bypass collector with a length of 5 km and a diameter of 4.5 
m; (iii) the Right Bank collector or a suitable alternative to convey the wastewater from the 
middle upper basin away from discharging into the M-R River; and (iv) an industrial waste 
conveyance pipe (collector) and pumping station on the right bank of the M-R River. Under 
APL-2, the Baja Costanera expansion collector (Radio Antiguo section and Capital Federal- 
Vicente Lopez section) as well as primary and secondary sewerage networks in Lomas de 
Zamora, Lanus, A. Brown, and other municipalities would be added. 
28. Treatment plants andpumping stations. Prior to its discharge through each outfall to the 
La Plata River, the wastewater will be subjected to preliminary treatment in two plants namely 
the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant located on the right bank of the M-R River at its 
confluence with La Plata River at Dock Sud, and the Berazategui preliminary treatment plant at 
Berazategui on the banks of La Plata River near the location of the current Berazategui outfall. 
The construction of the Berazategui preliminary treatment plant has already been contracted and 
is being financed by the Government using its own funds complemented by a loan from Brazil's 
11 
<<PAGE 21>>
BNDES. The Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant, on the other hand, will be financed with 
loan proceeds under APL- 1. 
29. Construction of new sub-aquatic outfalls. This project will also finance the construction 
of two new sub-aquatic outfalls over the two APL phases. The Riachuelo outfall, financed during 
APL-1 , will be located in the Dock Sud area. This outfall will measure 3.8 meters in internal 
diameter, and is expected to be 11.5 km long, of which the final 2.3 km are the diffuser section. 
The Berazategui outfall, expected to be financed under APL-2, will be located near the existing 
short outfall, measuring 3.8 meters in internal diameter and 7.5 km in length, of which the final 
3.5 km will be the diffuser section12. 
30. Construction of primary and secondary sewage networks (APL-2). Included only under 
APL-2, is the construction of primary and secondary sewerage networks in the municipalities of 
the M-R River Basin which currently lack coverage and are home to some of the Basin’s poorest 
people (for instance in Lomas de Zamora, Lanus, A. Brown, and other neighborhoods, but not in 
the city of Buenos Aires, which already has full coverage). 
3 1. These infrastructure works, as well as other sewerage expansion investments executed by 
AySA under its master plan, will directly benefit approximately 2.4 million people in the 14 
municipalities of the M-R River Basin, and about 1.2 million who live along the banks of La 
Plata River. Indirect beneficiaries include about 10 million people who will benefit from an 
improved and secure collection, conveyance and wastewater disposal system. 
32. In addition to infrastructure works, APL-1 will also provide financing for (i) independent 
technical supervision of key works; (ii) data collection, analyses and modeling in support of the 
engineering designs for these works; and (iii) some capacity development activities for AySA in 
support of the preparation of the engineering designs of the works. 
Macro-Component 2: Support for Industrial Pollution Abatement, Environmental 
Territorial Management and Institutional Strengthening 
Component 2: Industrial Pollution Abatement (US$ 75.4 million total, US$ 60.3 million in 
loan proceeds) 
33. Based on initial estimates, there are approximately 4100 industries in the M-R River 
Basin area that contribute a significant portion of the total organic load discharged to the M-R 
River in addition to virtually all toxic discharges. Of these, approximately 1600 are connected to 
the AySA network, while the remaining 2500 currently discharge effluents directly to the M-R 
River. ACUMAR will first focus on the 50 main industrial polluters that contribute 
approximately 95 percent of the total industrial organic load and 170 industries with the highest 
toxic loads that current discharge to the M-R River, using some of the technical assistance made 
available through this component. As part of the Government’s strategy, plans for industrial 
restructuring (Planes de Reconversion Industrial, or PRIs), including indicators, targets, and 
deadlines will be developed and signed by ACUMAR with these industries.

Component 3: Environmental Territorial Management (US$8 1.9 million total, US$65.5

36. This component's main objective is to improve public policies on environmental 
territorial management within the M-R River Basin. Towards this end, the component seeks to 
improve territorial planning and develop pilots of integrated investments in basic infrastructure 
services and flood control to improve urban living conditions in selected low-income areas of the 
basin.

River Basin to develop a regional planning system for the M-R Basin, and developing and 
implementing a geographic information system (GIS) to support ACUMAR's planning and 
decision-making capacity; (ii) Institutional development for flood control, supporting technical 
assistance to design and implement a flood control system, update of the hydrological master 
plan, improve the hydro-meteorological alert system and support municipal capacity

Component 4: Institutional Strengthening and Project Management (US$18.2 million total, 
US$18.2 million in loan proceeds) 
38. In line with the development objectives, a separate TA component has been prepared to 
support the design and implementation of measures required in both the short and long term to 
establish the necessary institutional capacity for the new basin authority. 
39. The component has been divided into five sub-components: (i) Institutional 
Strengthening of ACUMAR to adapt its current organizational structure to better respond to its 
institutional responsibilities. A new structure will be designed through a participatory process 
and using proven methodologies and strategies. This subcomponent will support the necessary 
activities such as workshops, consultants, and infrastructure investments to support the new 
institutional structure; (ii) a Communication Strategy to inform key actors and stakeholder 
groups in the basin about the project in a transparent manner; to strengthen the credibility of and 
support for the project; and to contribute to the overall consultation and participation framework 
of the project; (iii) Establishment of a public information office in ACUMAR supporting the 
necessary investments for the proper functioning of a public information office such as human 
resources, office equipment, infrastructure and logistic tools; (iv) Technical and monitoring 
studies encompassing both additional studies required during the course of project 
implementation and the provision of expert support (including a panel of recognized independent 
experts in the fields of environment, engineering, biodiversity, modeling etc.) to provide periodic 
advice in conjunction with project implementation. Studies will include additional data collection 
and analytical work relating to impact and water quality monitoring in the M-R River and La 
Plata River, as well as the control of high groundwater tables in the basin area. This 
subcomponent may also finance other studies required for the clean-up of the M-R River Basin 
as agreed with the Bank. Finally, (v) Project management, supporting human resources, physical 
space and equipment for UCOFI and the proposed UEP within SAyDS to strengthen purchasing, 
contracting and procurement processes as well as technical management of the project.

42. The World Bank has acquired considerable experience in river basin clean-up projects, 
industrial pollution abatement and control projects, and a wide variety of urban rehabilitation 
projects. The following lessons learned from these projects, undertaken in Argentina and in other 
countries, have been reflected in the design of the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable 
Development Project: 
43. Need for sound institutional coordination and strong political will. The lack of an 
adequate institutional and legal framework to forge agreement among different government 
jurisdictions and assign accountability has clearly been a major obstacle to implementing a 
clean-up program for the M-R River Basin. While national governments have made clean-up a 
priority over the past several years, the high-profile Supreme Court lawsuit and ruling and 
recently passed legislation resulting in the creation of ACUMAR, have reinforced the framework 
to address the basin’s challenges. Although the creation of ACUMAR represents significant 
progress over previous attempts to solve the M-R pollution problem, the agency is still in the 
process of consolidation and requires institutional strengthening. GOA efforts are also backed by 
strong political will: President Fernbdez de Kirchner publicly committed on her second day in 
office to make concrete progress in the basin within her 4-year mandate. Neither the 
interjurisdictional’ basin authority nor the Supreme Court ruling that forces the Government to 
implement actions to clean-up the M-R River were in place at the time the last major attempt was 
launched to clean-up the M-R River Basin under IADB’s “Environmental Recovery of the 
Mutanza-Riachuelo” project (1 997). With the Supreme Court ruling, ACUMAR is politically 
and legally accountable. 
l5 The basin is covered by the jurisdictions of 14 municipalities and the City of Buenos Aires. 
15 
<<PAGE 25>>
44. Lessons learned j?om past environmental operations involving utilities. Past operations 
have often ignored the additional financial burden that expanded sewage collection and treatment 
systems place on utilities. This project explicitly recognizes the importance of cost recovery - in 
terms of covering the operations and maintenance costs resulting from new sewage infrastructure 
-- and takes several concrete steps towards addressing it. While Law 26.221 provides a 
regulatory framework for AySA’s operations, the utility has been operating without a concession 
contract since 2006, for example. Against this backdrop, the Government has committed to 
entering into a concession agreement with AySA before the Bank’s loan is declared effective. 
Furthermore, this project also “piggy-backs” on a recently-approved IADB operation which 
includes conditions for AySA to increase tariffs to break even on O&M costs in 4 years. While 
these are important steps, the project team recognizes that AySA’s financial position will remain 
vulnerable to any interruption of public financing as long as tariffs don’t cover at least O&M 
costs. More generally, it is expected that a concession contract will help to enhance incentives to 
maintain AySA’s relatively good operating efficiency. A detailed discussion of AySA’s financial 
position and performance is provided in Annex 11B. 
45. Adopting an integrated basin-wide approach. Experience with pollution control projects 
in river basins in Bangladesh, China, and elsewhere has shown the importance of adopting a 
basin-wide approach, with integrated components that fit together “naturally” and do not place 
an undue burden on borrowers and implementing agencies. This experience also highlights 
treating institutional and environmental objectives associated with integrated water quality 
management and urban upgrading projects as long-term program goals rather than short to 
medium-term project objectives. These projects have also pointed to the need for adequate 
monitoring of pollution sources through the creation of databases and for linking data to 
investment planning for wastewater treatment. 
46. Understanding obstacles to local capacity building. Implementation experience from 
previous projects in Argentina, particularly in partnership with SAyDS , has shown the following 
main weaknesses: (i) lack of technical capacity, (ii) lack of awareness of project processes, and 
(iii) frequent changes in leadership and personnel. This situation has led to enormous delays in 
project implementation. Project design has sought to ensure efficient implementation by using 
AySA to implement component 1 (which accounts for approximately 88% of the APL-1 
program) and SAyDS’ experienced UCOFI unit (which has a good track record with 
internationally financed contracts) to manage the procurement, financial management and 
administrative aspects for components 2, 3 and 4. 
47. Need for public participation and communication. The Bank’s experience with pollution- 
control and sewerage infrastructure projects clearly demonstrates the ,need for a strong public 
participation and communication component to address public concerns related to project 
components. The proposed operation has already carried out consultation workshops to analyze 
the terms of reference for and draft of the Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA). ACUMAR 
has also agreed to establish and maintain more frequent dialogue with NGOs and other 
stakeholders through its Social Participation Commission. A dedicated communication strategy 
is being financed through the proposed project. Moreover, the Supreme Court’s ruling in the 
Mendoza Case, specifically designates 5 NGO’s/civil society organizations as part of a 
“collective body” coordinated by the Ombudsman to monitor compliance by national and 
subnational governments as well as ACUMAR with the Court’s decision. The project has 
16 
<<PAGE 26>>
maintained a dialogue with all of these entities during project preparation and is expected to 
deepen this dialogue further during implementation. 
6. Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection 
48, Various alternatives were considered during project design and preparation. The 
alternatives considered and the reasons for the rejection of those not adopted are discussed 
below. 
49. Lending instrument alternatives. Lending instruments considered for this project were 
the Sector Investment Loan (SIL) and the Adaptable Program Loan (APL). Given the size of the 
project and its complexity, with multi-sectoral and multi-jurisdictional aspects, a longer-term 
commitment will be required from both the Government and the Bank. Under these 
circumstances, an APL affords greater flexibility to prepare and lay the groundwork for future 
investments. Because it establishes goals and triggers to move from one phase to the next, an 
APL is considered more appropriate than a SIL or a sequence of individual projects. For 
example, components 2 and 3 require several studies, additional analytical work, and 
institutional strengthening for appropriate investment decisions. These background studies will 
lay the groundwork for investments in subsequent phases. For component 1, which focuses on 
major infrastructure works, an APL design will permit the sequencing of works to be carried out 
in an orderly manner. 
50. Technical alternatives. A variety of technical alternatives for the level of wastewater 
treatment, the length of outfalls, and the number and location of the wastewater treatment plants 
were considered for the sanitation component of the proposed project. Annex 11A describes the 
alternatives and the reasons for rejection of those that were not selected. 
5 1. An issue of major importance for the project is the selection of preliminary treatment of 
wastewater coupled with discharge of the effluent to La Plata River through a long, effective 
outfall. The decision to adopt a low level of treatment rather than a higher level has been 
challenged by several NGOs. However, Buenos Aires and other riparian municipalities can be 
protected from the negative impacts of pre-treated effluent discharge to La Plata River by an 
effective outfall, making use of La Plata River’s large oxygenation capacity. This conclusion was 
derived from mathematical modeling (see Annex 11A). 
52. Choosing a higher level of treatment (secondary) and a shorter outfall while effective in 
removing organic matter would be unaffordable considering that disinfection to eliminate 
pathogens would still be required. This option would be much more expensive in terms of initial 
investment as well as operation and maintenance costs. The additional costs associated with 
secondary treatment would not provide additional benefits. Therefore, the alternative of 
secondary treatment in conjunction with an effective outfall was not selected because it is 
equivalent, in terms of risks to humans and the environment, to the selected alternative 
(preliminary treatment and an effective outfall), but much more expensive. These conclusions 
were reached with the support of international technical experts on subjects including outfall 
impacts on water bodies, hydrological modeling and integrated basin management. After the 
beginning of operations of the treatment and outfall system, comprehensive water quality 
monitoring will be undertaken in the La Plata River. If the water quality monitored is found to 
17 
<<PAGE 27>>
deviate from what was foreseen by the modeling, and presents risks, additional treatment units 
will be installed in the treatment plant to mitigate these risks. Sufficient area within the site of the 
treatment plant has been secured to accommodate any additional treatment units that might be 
needed in the future. 
53. For the industrial pollution abatement component (component 2), two alternatives were 
considered. The first was a high level of treatment for all industrial wastes and discharge of all 
effluents except those containing toxic matter into the M-R River. The second was a lower level 
of treatment of all industrial wastes and discharge of all effluents, except those containing toxic 
matter, to the municipal sewerage network for further processing with domestic wastewater in 
line with existing AySA standards. The first alternative was rejected because mathematical 
modeling showed that even after a high level of treatment, the discharge of industrial effluents 
into the M-R River would still render it anaerobic. The second alternative was found to be the 
only way to bring about aerobic conditions in the river and recover its water quality. 
54. Phasing of investments. As currently designed, APL- 1 includes the Right Bank Collector 
(CMD), a key piece of sewerage infrastructure that would receive and transport wastewater 
collected in the coverage area of the Southwest, Jaguel, Fiorito and Laferrere treatment plants as 
well as wastewater generated in the municipalities of the upper M-R River Basin located outside 
the Ay SA concession area. Initial water quality modeling during project preparation has 
identified the withdrawal of these discharges to the M-R River as a key enabler of improved 
water quality. Given the size of the proposed investments (estimated at about US$275 million 
(without contingencies) based on preliminary designs), AySA will conduct a pre-feasibility study 
of alternatives to the CMD. AySA is considering, for example, whether side stream elevated pool 
aeration (SEPA) stations, being used in Chicago, Illinois, could be used -- possibly in 
conjunction with higher levels of treatment at existing and future treatment plants - to meet 
water quality objectives established by ACUMAR. Water quality modeling of various 
alternatives is already underway in conjunction with ACUMAR. Should a better alternative 
emerge in terms of economic, environment and social aspects, the latter would need to be 
submitted to SAyDS and ACUMAR for approval before it would be presented to the Bank for 
review. A covenant has been added to the Loan Agreement requiring that the study of 
alternatives to the CMD be completed by March 3 1 , 20 10. Any alternative to construction of the 
CMD would need to be approved by the Bank and may need to be reflected in an amendment to 
the Loan Agreement. Given this uncertainty, the Bank and the Borrower initially included the 
CMD under APL-2. Subsequently, however, the Bank and the Borrower agreed to advance the 
CMD to APL-1 to provide the Government with an instrument for accelerated basin clean-up in 
the short and medium-term. Including the CMD in APL-1 also has the important advantage of 
concentrating all major trunk infrastructure works needed to remove contaminant flows from the 
M-R River Basin in a single phase over the next 6 years. On the other hand, construction of the 
CMD during APL-1 increases the size of the project’s first phase to nearly US$1.5 billion, 
meaning that the Bank’s US$840 million loan would, on average, finance only about 56% of 
APL- 1 activities. 
55. Institutional alternatives for project implementation. Several institutional arrangements 
for project implementation were considered. The first was to vest ACUMAR, the recently- 
created basin agency with direct implementation responsibility for the entire project. This 
alternative was rejected because ACUMAR lacks the capacity to construct sewage collectors, 
18 
<<PAGE 28>>
networks, and treatment plants under component 1. These works are the natural responsibility of 
AySA, the entity in charge of water and sanitation service provision in Buenos Aires. 
56. A second alternative considered was to split responsibilities by vesting the 
implementation responsibility for component 1 exclusively in Ay SA and the implementation 
responsibility for components 2, 3 and 4 exclusively in ACUMAR. Given that ACUMAR’s 
organizational structure is still being finalized and ACUMAR will only gradually staff-up against 
its new organigram, the Government has opted for a more cautious approach whereby 
components 2, 3 and 4 are implemented by SAyDS with support from ACUMAR while 
ACUMAR reviews the technical consistency of any changes to the works proposed under 
component 1 that may impact PISA objectives. 
57. Alternative financing mechanisms for small and medium size polluting industries. 
Component 2, on industrial pollution abatement, includes financing SMEs that are among the 
most polluting in the M-R River Basin, to support their investments in cleaner production 
measures. During preparation, the team considered providing commercial lending or matching 
grants for these SME investments. Given their weak financial capacity and limited access to 
commercial credit, it was decided to extend grants to SMEs on a matching basis, in order to help 
them improve environmental compliance (as spelled out in their PRIs). Large enterprises can 
access credit either through local commercial loans or through loans for the private sector from 
multi-lateral organizations (e.g. International Finance Corporation or Interamerican Investment 
Corporation). This financing mechanism is also consistent with the financial mechanism being 
developed for the Sustainable Industrial Development Project, a proposed complementary Bank- 
financed project in Argentina. 
C. IMPLEMENTATION 
1. Partnership arrangements (if applicable) 
58. The Bank preparation team for the proposed project has coordinated with IADB’s 
Programa de Agua Potable y Saneamiento del Area Metropolitana y el Conourbano Bonaerense 
project preparation team. The purpose of this collaboration was to share information and avoid 
duplication of work, especially in tasks related to AySA’s financial assessment and the analysis 
of common socio-economic information in similar areas. It is expected that this partnership will 
continue. 
2. Institutional and implementation arrangements 
59. The Argentine Republic will be the Borrower for the proposed loan. SAyDS will be 
responsible for overall project coordination and will directly implement macro-component 2 
together with ACUMAR (as the inter-jurisdictional entity). While ACUMAR has been created as 
an inter-jurisdictional entity with overall responsibility for implementing the PISA, the 
Government recognizes that ACUMAR currently has limited implementation capacity which 
will be reinforced and developed over the course of APL-1. The responsibilities for each entity 
are described in greater detail in Annex 6. Regarding macro-component 1, AySA will be the 
executing entity while ACUMAR, will ensure the consistency of project activities with the PISA 
in line with its legal mandate. 
19 
<<PAGE 29>>
60. Institutions. ACUMAR: The governing body of ACUMAR has 8 members. It is headed 
by the Secretary of the SAyDS and comprises three other representatives from the national 
government,’6 as well as four representatives from local governments -- two from the Province 
and two from the City of Buenos Aires. Through various resolutions, ACUMAR has been legally 
empowered to effectively coordinate the different agencies and manage and enforce regulations 
and norms relating to integrated basin management and the implementation of PISA. Details are 
summarized in Annex 11B. ACUMAR has the authority to enforce a wide range of preventive 
actions against polluters. Since its creation in 2006, it has used this authority widely. Through 
July 10, 2008, ACUMAR had issued 702 preventive actions (“medidas preventivas”) to prevent 
pollution, of which 110 have resulted in the temporary closure of polluting firms. Nonetheless, 
ACUMAR has not yet imposed preventive actions that would result in the definitive and total 
elimination of discharges or the closure of firms. 
61. AySA: In March 2006, AySA was created as a private company -- with the Government 
as the controlling shareholder -- to take over the service area previously served by Aguas 
Argentinas Sociedad Anonima (AASA), a private company controlled by the French group Suez 
and the Spanish group Aguas de Barcelona. The owners of AASA also included a minority group 
of shareholders comprising Bank of Galicia, Vivendi, the International Finance Corporation 
(IFC) and Anglian Water. After tariffs were frozen in the wake of the 2002 economic crisis 
under the Economic Emergency Law, the GOA and AASA were unable to reach agreement on a 
renegotiated concession agreement. In 2006, AASA’s concession contract was rescinded by the 
Government which alleged that AASA was not in compliance with its investment program. Suez 
and other foreign shareholders are currently suing the GOA before ICSID for economic 
compensation. The case is still pending resolution. 
62. AySA is currently subject to controls mandated both for private companies and for public 
utilities and will be in substantial compliance with the operator eligibility criteria set out in the 
PAD for the Buenos Aires Infrastructure Sustainable Development Project once an appropriate 
concession contract is signed between the Government and AySA (see Annex 11B for details). 
The regulatory framework delegates primary oversight authority of the concession to the 
Subsecretaria de Recursos Hidricos (SSRH) - which exercises authority both as majority owner 
and primary regulator of AySA alongside the Regulatory Entity for Water and Sanitation 
(ERAS). The SSRH is part of the Ministry of Federal Planning, Public Investment and Services 
(MPFIPS) and plays a key role in the governance of AySA. 
63. AySA’s capital structure is not typical of a fully-integrated utility. Its minimal level of 
debt and fixed assets are more akin to those of an operating company than a government-owned 
water utility with responsibility for financing all (or at least a portion of) its fixed assets. Details 
of AySA’s capital structure are presented in Annex 11B. 
64. Implementation arrangements. The SAyDS has informed the Bank, that it is moving to a 
new management model for internationally financed projects under which procurement, financial 
management and general administrative functions are centralized in a single (existing) unit

Planificaci6n Federal, Inversi6n Publica y Servicios, or MPFIPS), and a representative of the Ministry of the 
Economy. 
20 
<<PAGE 30>>
(UCOFI) while the technical leadership’ for each operation is vested in separate project 
implementation units (UEP). In line with this strategy, SAyDS will exercise its overall project 
coordination role for the present project through two units: UCOFI (Unidad de Coordinacidn 
para la Gestidn Tbcnica y Financiera Internacional) and UEP (Unidad de Ejecucibn del 
Proyecto). UCOFI has experience with the management of projects financed by international 
financial institutions. UCOFI will . manage the project’s US Dollar denominated designated 
account as well as a Peso-denominated project account from which it will make all payments for 
goods, works, and services procured under components 2, 3 and 4. With technical support from 
UEP and ACUMAR (which will help produce terms of reference and technical specifications 
and support bidproposal evaluation and contract supervision) UCOFI will also carry out all 
procurement for these components. For component 1 , UCOFI will transfer funds to AySA which 
will contract all goods, works and consulting services and make all payments to consultants and 
contractors using its regular technical units. These units include AySA’s procurement and legal 
departments (for the preparation of bidding documents) and AySA’s planning department for 
technical aspects. The environmental, social relations and communications departments of AySA 
will also participate in project implementation. Details of the implementation responsibilities of 
SAyDS, ACUMAR and AySA can be found in Annex 6. 
3. Monitoring and evaluation of outcomes/results 
65. This project strengthens the monitoring and evaluation capacity of the newly created river 
basin authority, ACUMAR. During APL- 1 , financing allocated for component 2 includes 
technical assistance to support the development of an industrial pollution control and monitoring 
system including a GIs-based industrial pollution database. This database will include 
monitoring results of parameters relating to water consumption, as well as pollution loads from 
various industries in the basin. In addition, the project will also finance pollution control 
infrastructure, equipment, and testing in certified laboratories. Under component 3 , technical 
assistance will support the development of a GIS database to aid planning processes; and to 
strengthen the meteorological and hydrological forecasting capacity in the M-R River Basin. 
Annex 4 presents details on these sub-components. 
66. The recent Supreme Court ruling against the central government, Buenos Aires Province, 
the Government of the City of Buenos Aires, and 44 industries requires ACUMAR to adopt an 
internationally recognized system of indicators to assess the fulfillment of the PISA. While civil 
society participation in ACUMAR is provided under article 4 of Law 26.168 through a Social 
Participation Commission, the Supreme Court ruling provides explicit additional arrangements 
for civil society oversight of all basin clean-up measures, including the designation of a 
monitoring body (“Cuerpo Colegiado ” or “collective body ”) headed by the national 
ombudsman, and composed of the five non-governmental organizations (NGOs) participating in 
the judicial case as “terceros interesados” (i.e., interested third parties). A Federal Court in 
Quilmes has been designated to monitor the Government’s compliance with the terms of the 
ruling. 
67. As mentioned previously, key program indicators have been developed for the Bank loan 
and will be monitored by AySA and ACUMAR with the help of local universities through loan 
financing at baseline and during project implementation to evaluate progress. The specific targets 
21 
<<PAGE 31>>
for results monitoring are detailed in Annex 3, and include information on data collection and 
reporting. 
4. Sustainability 
68. While the GOA has made solving the M-R River Basin’s environmental problems a high 
priority, the Supreme Court ruling requiring the central government, Buenos Aires province and 
the city of Buenos Aires to clean up the M-R River and its basin makes rehabilitation a matter of 
national (not merely government) policy. In addition, the Supreme Court ruling has also 
heightened the profile of ACUMAR which has been designated the lead agency responsible for 
clean-up of the M-R basin. Finally, given that the M-R River has been a source of national 
debate for several decades, and the recent Supreme Court ruling, future governments (both 
national and provincial) will almost certainly continue to consider the clean-up of the Basin a 
high priority. Even so, it is clear that implementation of PISA will require substantial political 
and financial commitment by the national government, at levels that may be difficult to sustain 
given global economic uncertainty. Moreover, the Supreme Court’s ruling notwithstanding, 
ACUMAR remains dependent on national government financing and personnel. 
69. The GOA will assure the adequate operation and maintenance of the main infrastructure 
investments under the sanitation component of the proposed project. The GOA will own the new 
assets, and AySA will be the infrastructure operator (See Box 1). 
Box 1. International cases of government financing for water and sewerage services 
Government financing for wastewater treatment and disposal investments through subsidies is common practice in 
developed and developing countries. The main reasons for supporting this practice include the following: (i) 
wastewater treatment and disposal is treated as a quasi-public good because of the wider public health benefits, (ii) 
the level of investment in the infrastructure involved is so high that it generally cannot be filly paid for by medium 
or low income municipalities; and (iii) the avoided environmental externalities cannot be directly charged to users. 
Examples of subsidized investment are found in both developed and developing countries, including the USA where 
the federal government provided 75% of the investment costs for wastewater treatment and disposal until the 
beginning of the 1990s. Between 1972 and 1989 US$56 billion in federal construction grants were provided to 
municipalities for secondary treatment. The federal government of Mexico provides subsidies for wastewater 
treatment and disposal amounting to about 50% of the investment through various federal programs. The subsidy 
can go up to 100% in the poorest municipalities. The government of Colombia has an innovative subsidy program 
which has provided some US$150 million in investment subsidies during the past four years for investments in 
water and sanitation as part of the 2002-2006 National Development Plan, most of this for sewerage networks and 
wastewater treatment plants. The government plans to expand this program and allocate an additional US$500 
million to it over the next 4 years. EU programs for new member states include up to 100% subsidies for 
investments in wastewater treatment facilities. 
In some cases investment subsidies are provided by governments paying for each cubic meter treated. Examples 
include (i) France, where it is a common practice to provide an “operator grant” to municipalities with sewage 
treatment plants, with subsidies that generally amount to 50% and provide an incentive to reduce contamination. If 
standards are not reached, the subsidy is not disbursed; and (ii) the German model, where all municipalities pay a 
sewage discharge fee to the state, with a 50% discount if discharge standards are reached. 
70. In the wake of the economic and social crisis of 2002, one of the most severe losses of 
income and downturns in living standards on record, the GOA decided to tackle the imbalance 
between tariffs and the population’s payment capacity by freezing the tariffs of all public 
22 
<<PAGE 32>>
services (electricity, gas, water, and transport). Since then water tariffs have been frozen in 
Buenos Aires. In real terms they have declined substantially. Nonetheless, starting in 2008, the 
GOA has begun to increase public transport, electricity and gas tariffs. While AySA’s tariffs 
have not increased, the Province of Buenos Aires recently appr~ved’~ a significant tariff increase 
for ABSA, another major public water and sanitation concessionaire operating just outside 
AySA’s concession area. 
71. In spite of this public service tariff policy, AySA was able to finance its O&M costs 
directly from tariffs until 2007. For 2008, however, AySA’s O&M costs were on track to exceed 
tariff revenues by about 45 percent” (see Annex 11B). The GOA, as the majority owner of 
AySA, is making budgetary transfers” necessary for the normal operation of the company, as 
has been the practice since AySA’s creation. In September 2008, Argentina’s National Congress 
approved a AR$1250 million (approximately US$4 12 million) capital improvement budget for 
AySA, including transfers covering operating costs for CY2008. In addition to covering this 
O&M gap, the GOA through AySA and the Buenos Aires provincial government has increased 
investment levels during the last 2 years to improve service and expand coverage. Moreover, 
clause 4.1 1 of IADB’s loan agreement (Program de Agua Potable y Saneamiento del Area 
Metropolitana y el Conurbano Bonaerense) with Argentina for its November 2008 loan for 
AySA (which commits the Government to achieving operation, maintenance and administration 
cost recovery of AySA by year 4 of IADB’s operation) is deemed to improve the sustainability 
of AySA. The government’s commitment to AySA’s financial sustainability is further 
underscored in the Development Policy Letter (DPL) (see Annex 11E) provided in conjunction 
with the proposed operation. 
72. For industrial pollution abatement, clean-up investments will include those financed by 
the private sector within a framework of government-sanctioned economic incentives and 
penalties. The project proposes a US$41 million fund that will provide financing to a number of 
SMEs for pollution control and cleaner production investments. This financing would be on a 
matching grant basis, as opposed to a targeted or revolving credit fund. The financial cost of 
future compliance would be assumed by the private sector. 
73. SAyDS and ACUMAR will implement works and activities such as the infrastructure 
investments under the environmental territorial management component of the proposed project. 
For long-term sustainability, these investments rely on strengthening ACUMAR as the lead 
agency for the basin. Operation and maintenance functions will be transferred to the relevant 
jurisdictions, i.e., the relevant municipality for micro drainage investments and the province for 
macro-drainage investments.

<<PAGE 33>>
5. Critical risks and possible controversial aspects 
74. The proposed program is a highly complex operation with very substantial risks aiming at 
significant positive environmental and social impacts. The main risks involve: (i) delays in key 
investments essential to basin clean-up as the global economic downturn leads to slower growth 
in Argentina and competing priorities reduce the availability of government counterpart funds. 
This could lead to postponing those investments under the PISA (such as the Right Bank 
Collector) whose benefits would be felt only in the medium term. It could also complicate the 
willingness to enforce industrial clean-up in industries located in the M-R basin; (ii) special 
interest capture of the investment program proposed under the project; (iii) the weakness of 
ACUMAR as a new Basin Authority and (iv) that ACUMAR, subnational governments or 
private firms in the basin could use their own funds to carry out actions broadly in line with basin 
clean-up under the PISA without full consideration of social and environmental impacts. Table 3 
shows the risks involved in the proposed project and measures to mitigate these risks.

APPRAISAL SUMMARY 
1. Economic and financial analyses 
75. The Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project (APL- 1 and APL-2) is 
estimated to render a net economic benefit and, in particular, to favor unserved population 
groups. Detailed financial, economic, and distributive analyses carried out during preparation, 
complemented by sensitivity and risk assessments, are presented in Annex 9. 
29 
<<PAGE 39>>
Economic Analysis 
0 Cost Benefit Net present value (NPV) = US$0.5 billion 
o Cost Effectiveness 
o Other 
Economic rate of return (ERR) = 22% 
76. The economic analysis for this project was carried out for the following activities: (i) 
sewerage expansion, (ii) improvement of water quality in the M-R River only, and (iii) 
improvement of water quality in both the M-R and La Plata Rivers. Economic benefits were 
estimated based on a combination of revealed and stated preference approaches. The revealed 
preference approach, which uses market information indirectly, was used to estimate (i) the 
benefits of sewerage expansion through avoided costs and hedonic pricing methods and (ii) the 
benefits of water quality improvement in the M-R River through hedonic pricing methods. The 
contingent valuation method (a stated preference approach), which is based on asking 
households how much they are willing to pay for an environmental quality improvement, was 
used to estimate the benefits of clean-up of the M-R River and of both the M-R and La Plata 
Rivers. Special attention was paid to ensure that these benefits were not double counted. The 
alternative combinations of methods that were used to estimate the total benefits of the project 
are detailed in Annex 9. 
77. A contingent valuation survey was designed to evaluate the benefits expected from water 
quality modeling by ACUMAR, Le., the elimination of unpleasant odors along the entire length 
of the M-R River, the possibility of using the M-R and La Plata Rivers for recreation without 
direct contact with water, and protection of the water intakes for the public water supply system. 
Results of this survey showed that the willingness-to-pay (WTP) for cleaning up both rivers was 
US$3.12 per household per month, compared with US$2.63 per household per month for the M- 
R River only. Also, as expected, the average WTP for households closer to the M-R River was 
significantly higher (US$3.65 per household per month) than that for households farther (over 20 
blocks) away (US$l.44 per household per month). Similar figures for the clean-up of both rivers 
were US$3.68 per household per month for houses nearer the river and US$2.40 per household 
per month for houses farther away. 
78. The hedonic pricing approach was used to estimate benefits relating to (i) sewerage 
connections and (ii) clean-up of the M-R River, using a series of attributes of properties and their 
location. For sewerage connections, results of the 2008 Real Estate Survey of 1,000 properties 
(empty lots, apartments, and houses) conducted as part of this economic evaluation revealed 
statistically significant higher average prices of houses (3 1 percent increase), apartments (24 
percent increase), and empty lots (8.5 percent increase) with a sewerage connection. For cleaning 
up the M-R River, which would eliminate the river’s bad odor, there is an important difference 
between the average prices of properties based on odor, and the presence of odor is strongly 
correlated with proximity to the river. Descriptive statistics from the survey data showed that 
property prices fall as proximity to the M-R River increases. Distance was used as a proxy for 
the presence of odor after controlling for other variables that are correlated with distance in the 
hedonic price function. The resulting average 9 percent increase-which varies from 3 to 18 
percent depending on the type of property and distance from the river-was applied to the 
properties within 20 blocks of the M-R River to estimate the total benefits of removing the 
river’s odor. 
30 
<<PAGE 40>>
79. The avoided cost method was used to estimate the benefits relating to (i) the sewerage 
connection and (ii) the protection of water intakes. Avoided cost estimates associated with 
sewerage connections were based on current costs of different on-site sewage disposal systems. 
At present, 65 percent of households located in the M-R River Basin do not have access to a 
sewerage connection and use other solutions for sewage disposal: septic tanks (30 percent), holes 
with no septic chamber (17 percent), and other solutions (18 percent). The avoided costs 
associated with these private sewerage solutions range from US$47 to US$292 per household per 
year based on the data collected as part of this economic analysis. Avoided costs resulting from 
the protection of the Bernal water intake were provided by AySA. 
80. Health benefits. The expansion of sewerage is expected to result in important health 
benefits, primarily from a reduction in diarrheal mortality among children under 5 and diarrheal 
morbidity of the total population. The cost-of-illness approach was used to estimate health 
benefits resulting from the provision of sewerage and the protection of drinking water quality at 
intake sites as an alternative for quantifying benefits under the avoided cost method. The annual 
benefits resulting from the reduction of diarrheal mortality in children under 5 and diarrheal 
morbidity in the total population are estimated at US$29 per person per year from sewerage 
expansion and US$6 per person per year from the protection of water at the Bernal water intake. 
While the health benefits from sewerage expansion and water intake protection are substantial 
for society as a whole, it should be noted that for the water utility (AySA), the with-project 
scenario will result in avoided costs from water intake protection. The health benefits were used 
to calculate total benefits in one of the alternatives presented in detail in Annex 9. 
81. Among the different combinations of approaches used to calculate the total economic 
benefit of the project, the one selected for this project included (i) hedonic pricing for sewerage 
connections, (ii) hedonic pricing for households less than 20 blocks from M-R River, and (iii) 
contingent valuation for households in Greater Buenos Aires more than 20 blocks from the M-R 
River. The value of estimated health benefits was not included in the selected approach. The 
selected approach was considered the most robust as it uses hedonic pricing data that more fully 
capture the benefits of sewerage provision and the elimination of odor than the alternative 
revealed preference methods (avoided costs and cost of illness). The results obtained using this 
combination of valuation methods show a net benefit of US$0.5 billion and an ERR of 22 
percent. 
82. 
probability of having positive returns.
