Financial Analysis of the Project 
NPV = -US$480 million 
83. The financial viability of the program (APL- 1 +APL-2) was assessed from the perspective 
of the entity implementing the project (AySA). It is based on the actual prices that AySA will 
pay and receive for the project over a period of 30 years. Only O&M costs were taken into 
consideration and a 100 percent subsidy on investment was included in the evaluation, as a grant 
from the Federal Government. Revenues from tariff were considered constant in real terms over 
30 years for the analysis. Taxes were also included. 
31 
<<PAGE 41>>
84. The results show a net loss of the program of US$480 million. The financial analysis of 
the program differs from the financial analysis of the entity (see Annex 11B) that operates the 
project, i.e. AySA; and hence the conclusions reached for the financial viability of the program 
do not necessarily apply to the financial viability of the company. The main differences between 
these two analyses are: (i) the financial evaluation of the program only calculates the incremental 
financial benefits and costs generated by each activity of the program (sewerage component and 
clean-up of the M-R and de La Plata Rivers); (ii) the financial evaluation of the program 
addresses only the cash flow generated by the program, without including other services 
provided by the company, for instance water supply. 
2. Technical 
85. Taking into account the existing sewerage system, which routes wastewater to the 
Berazategui outfall, and the geographical setup of the M-R River Basin in relation to the service 
area of AySA, the proposed project provides a combined solution for the treatment and disposal 
of the wastewater of the M-R River Basin and the entire concession area of AySA, which has a 
total population of about 10 million. Moreover, the project takes into consideration the liquid 
effluents of the approximately 4,000 industries located in the M-R River Basin. 
86. Mathematical models for simulating the water quality of the M-R River system and the 
coastal area of La Plata River near Buenos Aires were developed during project preparation and 
were used for the technical assessment of the alternatives and their comparison. The main 
finding of the modeling analysis is that the M-R River is unable to absorb virtually any 
wastewater and effluent discharges because of its limited auto-purification capacity, especially in 
the dry season. Consequently, a policy of progressive elimination of point source discharges is 
needed, aimed at achieving with time the elimination of all identifiable wastewater discharges to 
the river, consistent with the uses and objectives defined by ACUMAR. With this goal in mind, 
the defined alternatives were compared using technical, economic, environmental, and social 
criteria. As a result of this comparison, the most favorable alternative was selected and its stages 
of execution determined. 
87. The most suitable alternative, which from a combined standpoint offers lowest cost, 
technical advantages, simple operation and maintenance, the greatest positive environmental 
impacts, the lowest adverse impacts, and minimum negative social impacts, was found to be the 
distribution of the total collected wastewater flow to two preliminary treatment plants -- one 
located on the southern bank of the M-R River at its confluence with La Plata River and the other 
in Berazategui -- and the discharge of the effluents of these treatment plants to La Plata River 
through two long and effective sub-aquatic outfalls. 
88. These conclusions are the result of mathematical modeling of the impact of the analyzed 
alternatives on the water quality of La Plata River. They are also compatible with the 
recommendation of the World Health Organization that, from the public health 
standpoint of human exposure to wastewater, additional treatment beyond preliminary treatment 
2o WHO. 2003. Guidelines for Safe Recreational Water Environments. Volume 12, Coastal and Fresh Waters. 
Geneva, p. 80. Available at http://www.who,int/water sanitation healthhathindsnve l/en/. 
32 
<<PAGE 42>>
prior to discharge to the receiving body through an effective outfall does not reduce health risks. 
That is, the health risk associated with a system composed of preliminary treatment followed by 
an effective outfall is identical to that associated with a system composed of secondary treatment 
followed by an effective outfall. The WHO report refers to risks related to human exposure 
through bathing, which is not exactly the risk in the case of discharge to La Plata River. 
However, because the main risk of effluent discharge in the La Plata River system comes from 
pathogenic organisms, the exposure risk referred to in the WHO report is a good proxy for the 
risk relating to La Plata River. 
89. The comparison of the industrial pollution abatement alternatives demonstrated that the 
alternative of providing an appropriate level of treatment of all industrial wastes and discharging 
the effluents, except those containing toxic matter, into the municipal sewerage network for 
further processing with the domestic wastewater is the most suitable alternative. This alternative 
was coupled with the alternative selected for the sanitation component in the sense that the 
simulation model confirmed that the organic matter loads -including those of industrial origin - 
discharged to La Plata River through the two outfalls, can be absorbed by the river and will not 
generate water quality problems. Similarly, the conveyance and treatment systems were 
dimensioned to include all the industrial waste flows generated in the M-R River Basin. 
90. In terms of execution stages, the first stage of the sanitation component of the project will 
include (i) the main collection system, consisting of the Left Bank collector and the Baja 
Costanera bypass collector, (ii) the Right Bank collector (or a suitable alternative for the same 
function), (iii) the industrial waste collector to Berazategui, (iv) the Riachuelo preliminary 
treatment plant including the inflow and effluent pumping stations, and (v) the Riachuelo sub- 
aquatic outfall. The second stage of the sanitation component will include (i) the Baja Costanera 
alleviation collector, (ii) the Berazategui preliminary treatment plant (being constructed by the 
Government), (iii) the Berazategui effluent pumping station, (iv) the Berazategui sub-aquatic 
outfall, and (v) additional primary and secondary sewerage networks in the M-R River Basin. 
91, The system of preliminary treatment followed by an effective outfall is widely used in 
coastal cities around the world. About 140 such systems are in operation in Latin America, 40 of 
them in Chile. Montevideo, Uruguay, uses the same system, which also discharges its effluent to 
La Plata River through one existing outfall with a second one being planned. 
92. A major part of the project consists of construction of the large pipes, including the 
outfall pipes, in diameters between 0.8 m and 4.5 my using tunneling methods of construction. 
Most of the pipes will be constructed using segment-lined tunnels. The smaller-diameter pipes 
may be constructed using the pipe jacking method. Corrosion-resistant polymer concrete is a 
good option for the pipe material. Internally lined reinforced concrete or fiber-reinforced 
polymer pipes are also corrosion resistant and may be used. As stated in the section on analysis 
of technical alternatives, the selected alternative will discharge the effluent at a great distance 
from the shore so that it cannot reach the area used as a water supply source. This has been 
confirmed by mathematical simulation of the various alternatives (see details in Annex 11A). In 
addition, prior to the final design of the sub-aquatic outfalls, extensive hydrometeorological data 
collection will be undertaken and used for more detailed modeling. This detailed modeling will 
serve as the basis for the design of the outfalls and the diffusers. 
33 
<<PAGE 43>>
3. Fiduciary 
93. An assessment of the Financial Management (FM) arrangements for the proposed project 
was carried out in accordance with OPBP 10.02 and applicable policies and guidelines.” Results 
of the FM assessment are presented in Annex 7. The proposed financial management 
arrangements meet Bank requirements. UCOFI’s FM staff performance in previous Bank- 
financed projects22 and a review of AySA financial management arrangements in place indicate a 
positive attitude by management toward financial control. However, there is a moderate element 
of risk due to the large amount of lending involved in the proposed operation. To avoid any 
additional complexity and reduce associated risk, the simplest flow of funds mechanism for 
Component 1 has been proposed, as follows: 
(i) The SAyDS, through the Coordination Unit for International Technical and Financial 
Management (Unidad de Coordinacidn para la Gestidn Te‘cnica y Financiera Internacional), 
or UCOFI will have overall responsibility for FM aspects of the project, comprising 
accounting and financial reporting, disbursements and documentation of expenditures to the 
Bank, and external auditing arrangements. UCOFI has administrative capacity and 
experienced staff skilled in WB-financed activities; 
(ii) Flow of funds for Component 1. UCOFI will submit withdrawal applications to the Bank 
and handle all disbursement issues. Loan proceeds will be transferred from the Bank to the 
Designated Account (DA) in dollars managed by UCOFI to be opened in Banco de la Nacion 
Argentina (BNA). UCOFI will transfer loan proceeds to a “special account” in pesos opened 
by AySA in a bank acceptable to the Bank, from which AySA will make payments for 
eligible expenditures under Component 1. In order to monitor the uses of Loan funds, this 
bank account should not receive funds from other sources. In addition, transfers from UCOFI 
will be made solely on the basis of eligible expenditures incurred (certifications of works) or 
advances supported by expected expenditure reports prepared by AySA on the basis of 
signed contracts and planned certifications of works as determined by the Bank; 
(iii) There will be no transfers of loan funds either to municipalities or to other subnational 
government agencies. 
94. Procurement for this project will be carried out in accordance with the World Bank‘s 
Guidelines: Procurement under IBRD and IDA credits, May 2004, as revised October 2006 (the 
Procurement Guidelines) and Guidelines: the Selection and Employment of Consultants by the 
World Bank Borrowers, May 2004, as revised October 2006 (the Consultant Guidelines). Details 
are recorded in Annex 8. In August 2008, the Bank carried out an assessment of the capacity of 
the two agencies involved in project procurement, UCOFI and AySA to implement procurement 
actions for the project and prepared a detailed action plan to address all risks identified, as 
presented in Annex 8. The overall procurement risk has been rated as High for both 
implementation agencies. The proposed risk mitigation strategy for the project includes the 
following main actions: (i) agreement has been reached that additional experts be hired by AySA 
and UCOFI to strengthen local procurement teams; (ii) AySA is preparing a detailed plan and

works will be carried out using specialized consulting firms; and (viii) carrying out joint FM- 
Procurement supervision missions twice a year. 
4. Social 
95. The M-R River Basin is home to Argentina’s largest concentrations of urban poor. The 
poorest populations live in low income settlements alongside the river, without adequate and safe 
water supply, sanitation, and garbage collection and are in constant contact with numerous 
contaminants ranging from untreated organic waste to toxic industrial chemicals. Frequent 
flooding of the M-R River spreads highly polluted waters into informal settlements, exposing the 
inhabitants to further contamination. High rates of health problems are the result of the poor 
environmental conditions of the basin. 
96. The improvement in the quality of the river targeted by the proposed project will directly 
benefit the 3.5 million inhabitants in the basin, in particular the 1.2 million inhabitants below the 
poverty line. The main benefit will be the improvement of environmental and health conditions, 
through the introduction of sewage collectors which in turn, will enable water and sanitation 
expansion, especially to low income settlements. 
97. The identified main adverse impacts of the project include the disruptions that sanitation 
construction would cause, proper disposal of solid waste generated by both treatment plants, and 
the possible reduction of jobs due to closure of industries or changes in technology. Given that 
most of the works financed by APL-1 will be done through tunneling, no displacement is 
expected. However, as a precaution, the project has developed a Resettlement Policy Framework 
in the event of any unexpected resettlement. 
98. In order to identify the project’s positive social impacts and avoid or mitigate the 
potential negative impacts, two social assessments were carried out during project preparation. 
These two assessments are complementary: one focuses on the specific potential impacts 
associated with the execution of the large sanitation infrastructure works, and the other focuses 
on the impacts of the comprehensive set of actions proposed by the project. The conclusions and 
recommendations emerging from these assessments were taken into account in the project design 
in order to improve the social benefits generated by the project. 
35 
<<PAGE 45>>
99. The methodology adopted by these assessments ensured a meaningful consultation 
process, in which the broad range of stakeholders associated with the project fully participated in 
detailed discussions about project activities. The adopted methodology included focus groups, 
workshops, field visits, interviews and several meetings at the request of the stakeholders 
involved. Municipal and provincial governments, labor unions, private sector entrepreneurs, 
academia, community-based groups, and NGOs were among the stakeholders consulted. 
100. The main finding of the SAs was that all stakeholders were in favor of the project, 
considering the present scenario whereby given by the intervention of the Supreme Court and 
with the assistance by the World Bank, the environmental improvement of the M-R River Basin 
becomes achievable. None of the interviewed stakeholders opposed the overall project. However, 
critical opinions about various aspects of the project were expressed, such as inadequate 
information sharing and consultation, concerns over industry closures, higher tariffs, etc. (See 
Annex 1 1 C for a complete list of description of stakeholder opinions). 
101. As part of the project preparation process, extensive stakeholder consultation has been 
undertaken using formal as well as informal methodologies (see Annex 10 for more details). The 
project EIAs have been made available through the ACUMAR and AySA websites as well as 
through the Bank’s Infoshop. Information on the project has been made publically available 
through public disclosure events and workshops. Informal focus group discussions have been 
conducted with NGOs, local governments, affected people and beneficiaries at various points 
during preparation and appraisal. Some important events included: surveys and focus groups 
during the “willingness to pay” study for the economic analysis (see Annex 9), a workshop for 
the consultation of the terms of reference of the EIAs conducted on 10 July 2008, a workshop for 
the dissemination of the results of the EIAs conducted on 7 November 2008, AySA meetings 
with 11 NGOs prior to the EIA results workshop, World Bank meetings and exchanges of letters 
with several NGOs active in the basin, and meetings with stakeholders conducted by the 2 
consultant teams which carried out the Social Assessment studies of the project (see Annex 
1 IC). 
102. In addition to the project specific consultation and disclosure efforts, it is important to 
recognize that both AySA and ACUMAR have social outreach units within their organizational 
structure. These teams have been established to be in constant contact with local communities, 
and are used to facilitate communication tools. ACUMAR, for example, has established a 
network of approximately 300 community and environmental NGOs that are part of its broader 
communications strategy (see Annex llD) about the issues facing the M-R River Basin. This 
network provides an opportunity for information disclosure not just on the project but on a wide 
array of development issues facing the basin. 
103. Through the consultation process, stakeholders have provided feedback and expressed 
their concerns on a range of topics including the TOR and scope of the EIA studies; the scope of 
the project including the levels of treatment selected and alternatives considered; the risks 
associated with pollution discharges to La Plata River; the sequencing of project works and their 
adequacy to address the scale of the problems faced by in the M-R basin; the licensing and 
clearance procedures that will apply to the project works; government capacity to implement 
their responsibilities under the project and how disruptions to daily life will be handled during 
the construction phase. For a more complete discussion of issues raised see Annex 1 lC, Social 
36 
<<PAGE 46>>
Assessment and Public Consultation. As a result of the consultations, some important aspects of 
project design were modified. In particular, based on stakeholders feedback about the need to 
remove contaminant flow from the upper middle basin and right bank to the M-R River, the 
construction of the Right Bank Collector was advanced from APL-2 to APL-1 to provide the 
Government with an instrument for accelerated basin clean-up in the short and medium term. 
104. ACUMAR will develop a Social Management Plan to integrate and coordinate the 
approaches suggested in the two social assessments and the project’s communication strategy for 
addressing identified social impacts, risks and opportunities under the project. ACUMAR and 
Ay SA will confirm the institutional arrangements for coordination of the Social Management 
Plan to address: (i) the resettlement framework; (ii) the management and mitigation of impacts 
during the construction phase of Component 1 ; (iii) the management and mitigation of impacts 
during the operation phase of Component 1; (iv) the management and mitigation of impacts 
associated with industrial conversion of Component 2; (v) monitoring; and (vi) stakeholder 
consultation, communication and disclosure processes. 
105. In particular, Communication Management Plan (see description of component 4 in 
Annex 4 as well as Annex 11D) has been designed with ACUMAR, in order to inform key actors 
and stakeholder groups in the basin about the project in a transparent manner, strengthen the 
credibility of and support for the project, and contribute to the overall consultation and 
participation framework for the project. The Communication Management Plan includes the 
creation of a Communication Unit in ACUMAR, which would be responsible for (i) providing 
adequate and timely information prior to consultation events; (ii) maintaining documentation on 
consultation processes and decisions taken; and (iii) providing feedback to concerned 
stakeholders. Staff in the new unit will coordinate with those in AySA’s Communication Unit. 
This unit has already started to improve the communication of the project within the company by 
harmonizing project information among central and regional offices, and externally, by holding 
10 workshops with NGOs which had highlighted the shortage of project information. 
106. In response to the institutional weaknesses of ACUMAR’s Social Participation 
Commission and lack of opportunities for stakeholder participation, component 4 will focus on 
their institutional strengthening. Close coordination between the Social Participation Component, 
Environmental Unit and Communications Unit will be key to ensuring that engagement with key 
stakeholders throughout the implementation of the environmental management plan and 
consultations on social issues remain a two-way dialogue. 
107. Population resettlement; No resettlement is expected as a result of the project 
components financed under APL- 1. The technology adopted to execute the sanitation works, 
which includes underground tunnels and construction of shafts to access the underground tunnels 
only on land owned/leased by AySA, as well as the design of the sewage collector routes has 
been conceived to minimize adverse impacts and avoid resettlement, and no displacement is 
expected regarding industrial and urban improvements. However, given the complexity of the 
works, and since the physical interventions of components 2 and 3 of the project have not yet 
been fully determined, a Resettlement Policy Framework has been prepared for the project as a 
precaution. As pilot and initial phase interventions for components 2 and 3 are selected, 
minimizing resettlement will be one of the selection criteria. 
37 
<<PAGE 47>>
108. As described earlier, the Government has advanced the construction of the Berazategui 
preliminary treatment plant (BPTP). The World Bank team was recently informed (March 2009) 
that the construction site required resettlement of 18 families that had illegally occupied the 
BPTP site and erected precarious sheet-metal dwellings. In October 2008, families were 
relocated to new concrete houses approximately 2-3 km from the BPTP site. The Bank considers 
that the relocation of families from the BPTP site does not require application of OP 4.12 since 
construction of the BPTP is neither directly related to the project (APL-1) nor necessary to 
achieve its objectives. This conclusion notwithstanding, the Bank team conducted a preliminary 
assessment of the resettlement program and has found no evidence to indicate worsened living 
conditions. The Bank will continue to monitor living conditions going forward. 
109. Mechanisms for mediation, conflict resolution, and redress. There are several alternative 
and robust mechanisms at various levels available to citizens who wish to seek information or 
complain if they feel the project does not adequately address their needs or concerns. At the 
lowest level, there are project consultation mechanisms through the Social Participation 
Commission (created within ACUMAR for channeling comments and proposals from civil 
society organizations to ACUMAR's management), as well as submissions to the specific 
administration unit or institution (ACUMAR, SAyDS, or AySA). At the next level, external 
mechanisms include the presentation of claims to a Collective Body ("Cuerpo Colegiado) led by 
the National Ombudsman. Alternatively, people can directly file "ampuro" claims (restraining 
orders) before local, provincial or national courts, which may eventually be redirected to the 
Federal Judge of Quilmes. Claims related to AySA's conduct can be submitted to the company 
using different ways of communication (telephone, office visits or email); and in the case of non- 
response, users can file a claim with the Regulatory Entity for Water and Sanitation (Entidad 
Reguladora de Agua y Saneamiento, or ERAS) (see Annex 1 1 C). 
5. Environmental 
1 10. Environmental benefits. The principal aim of the project is to improve the environmental 
conditions of the M-R River basin by reducing levels of contamination from various domestic 
and industrial discharges. Specifically, the sanitation component is designed to eliminate sewage 
discharges to the M-R River through the provision of sewage collection, treatment, and disposal 
infrastructure. The industrial pollution abatement component will help improve environmental 
compliance and monitoring and will finance investments in cleaner production technologies for 
SMEs in order to reduce toxic industrial pollutants discharged to the M-R River. The 
environmental territorial management component is designed to address urban land use and flood 
management problems. Finally, the technical assistance and project management component will 
finance additional studies and overall project management activities to improve the efficiency 
and effectiveness of the main counterpart agencies. 
11 1. Overall, the environmental benefits of the project are substantial and are expected to 
outweigh environmental risks. Expected benefits include: (i) the improved quality of life for the 
3.5 million inhabitants of the M-R River Basin through reduced exposure to domestic and 
industrial pollution, (ii) improved sewerage and drainage services in the M-R River Basin and 
other parts of Buenos Aires by increasing the capacity of the sewerage network enabling AySA 
to expand service to 1.7 million people in the basin (from 35 percent to about 75 percent), 
especially in poor marginal areas, (iii) improved environmental quality through investments in 
38 
<<PAGE 48>>
pollution control for rivers and tributaries crossing the urban area and for La Plata River, and (iv) 
improved urban living conditions associated with territorial development and reduced flooding in 
the M-R River Basin. 
112. Environmental Risks of Component 1. Through the EIA process, a number of potential 
environmental risks were identified for the construction. and the operational phases of 
Component 1. During the construction phase, the main environmental risks relate to the 
management and control of the construction processes involved in the large civil works. These 
risks include: (i) impacts typically associated with large-scale construction projects (e.g., dust 
and noise control), (ii) management of hazardous materials, (iii) control of erosion and 
sedimentation, (iv) disposal of tunnel spoil and large earth movement, (v) re-suspension of 
potentially hazardous sediments during river dredging, and (vi) management of work sites as 
well as worker and public health and safety concerns. 
113. During the operational phase of the project, the potential environmental risks of 
component 1 include: (i) the human and ecological health risks associated with contamination of 
the Rio de la Plata resulting from the operation of the treatment plants and associated sub-aquatic 
outfalls (ii) nuisance noise and odor associated with the operation of the treatment plants and 
pumping stations and (iii) disposal of the bio-solid waste generated by the treatment plants. The 
benefits and risks have been described and evaluated in the project’s EIA and supporting 
technical analyses (see Section 6 below). A summary of the potential environmental impacts, 
risks, and mitigation measures is presented in Annex 10 of the PAD. 
114. Environmental Risks of Components 2 and 3. Environmental risks associated with 
components 2 and 3 are less significant than component 1 and are expected to be offset by 
positive. benefits. Potential adverse impacts could be associated with the construction of civil 
works for flood control and drainage under component 3 but these works are expected to be 
small scale with limited potential for negative impacts. Under component 2 there may also be 
risks associated with legacy environmental issues at industrial sites that may be beneficiaries of 
the project and in the demolition and disposal of outdated industrial processing and 
manufacturing equipment and stockpiles of hazardous materials. 
1 15. Because most of the specific works investments to be made under these components have 
yet to be identified it is not possible to ascertain with absolute certainty the nature or extent of 
the risks. Thus, for both of these components an Environmental Management Framework (EMF) 
has been developed which describes, among other issues (i) the eligibility criteria for works to be 
funded under the project and (ii) procedures for .screening and scoping potential issues for all 
proposed investments and, where appropriate, developing management and mitigation measures. 
6. Safeguard policies 
116. The proposed project is categorized as an Environmental risk category “A” based on the 
expected long-term impacts of the large-scale sanitation civil works investments under 
Component 1. Works to be carried out under Components 2 and 3 are expected to have impacts 
that are mostly positive and where adverse impacts may occur they are expected to be small- 
scale, temporary, local in nature, and can be addressed with known mitigation measures. Table 5 
summarizes the applicable Bank safeguard policies.

1 17. Environmental Assessment (OP/BP/GP 4.01). Environmental assessments have been 
prepared for the project at two levels of analysis. At the overall project and river basin level, an 
Integrated Environmental Assessment has been prepared which describes the regional 
environmental issues facing the M-R River Basin, the baseline environmental situation, the 
applicable legal and institutional framework guiding the management of the basin, the principal 
management strategies considered, and alternatives evaluated for different basin management 
approaches. In addition, the Integrated Environmental Assessment (IEA) provides a 
comprehensive description of the overall project components, their main environmental risks and 
benefits, and the measures to be employed during project implementation to ensure management 
of adverse environmental risks. 
1 18. A seven-volume Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) for Component 1 describes 
AySA’s strategic corporate vision, the Integrated Plan developed for its concession area, baseline 
environmental conditions in the project’s area of influence, and the applicable legal and 
institutional framework guiding Ay SA’S sanitation works. The EIA report from AySA considers 
realistic alternatives to each proposed work and evaluates the proposed works in the context of 
AySA’s five year investment plan (Plan Director). A comprehensive EMP has been developed, 
spelling out institutional responsibilities for implementation of mitigation actions as well as 
supervision and monitoring of compliance with agreed mitigation measures. For example, all 
construction contractors will be required to follow site-specific management plans agreed on and 
incorporated into all construction contracts. 
119. A panel of four independent experts24 was hired by the Bank to review the draft EIA 
reports and to provide comments and recommendations to ACUMAR and AySA in order to 
improve the quality of their EIA reports. The draft reports of the panel of experts were shared

Where needed, an EMP will be developed for sub-projects that will address specific issues 
identified during screening. The frameworks also describe the procedures and expectations with 
respect to disclosure of information and consultation with affected communities. 
122. EIA documentation has been made available for public review and comment. During 
project implementation, ACUMAR and AySA will carry out a strategic communications plan for 
the project that will define the methods for public disclosure of all relevant documents, including 
monitoring reports during implementation. 
123. Natural Habitats (OP/BP 4.04). Within the project’s area of influence there are five 
known terrestrial ecological reserves and no existing aquatic reserves. Based on studies 
conducted by the GEF funded project “Proyecto de Proteccidn Ambiental del Rio de la Plata y 
su Frente Maritimo” (commonly known as FREPLATA), priority areas for protection of aquatic 
biodiversity have been identified but these areas are far from the immediate impact zone and not 
likely to be affected by the project. 
124. Because the project aims to contribute to the significant improvement of water quality 
conditions in the M-R River Basin and in the coastal areas of the Rio de la Plata, it is expected to 
have significant positive impacts on the natural riverine habitats found in the project area. An 
area of the receiving waters in La Plata River will be degraded as a result of the pollution 
discharge from the wastewater treatment plant via the outfall. Models developed for the outfalls 
indicate, however, that the discharge plume is expected to be fully contained within a relatively 
small area of the receiving waters. The pollution is not expected to affect downstream coastal 
areas designated as conservation areas.

answered questions from Uruguayan representatives. The Argentine government has now 
received a reply from CARP that it does not express an objection to the proposed project. 26

Annex 1: Country and Program Background 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project 
The Matanza-Riachuelo Basin 
1. The Matanza-Riachuelo Basin lies in Buenos Aires Province and City and runs from the 
southwest to the northeast, discharging to Rio de la Plata (La Plata River). Usage rights to the 
water in this estuarine river are shared between Argentina and Uruguay. La Plata River 
discharges to the Atlantic Ocean. The Matanza-Riachuelo River is known as the Matanza in the 
upper part of the basin and the Riachuelo in the lower part. The river’s flow fluctuates between a 
minimum average of 2.9 m3/ second and a maximum average flow of 1,000 m3/second. Its mean 
slope is 0.04 percent, characteristic of this very flat region. The basin area is 2,240 km2 and 
covers 14 municipalities and part of the City of Buenos Aires, with a total population of about 
3.5 million. The Matanza-Riachuelo (M-R) and Reconquista river basins are the main river 
systems in the Metropolitan Area of Buenos Aires (Figure 1.1). 
Figure 1.1. Interaction between different hydrological systems in the M-R River Basin 
Notes: CRR = Reconquista River Basin, CAU = Urban Creek Basin, 
CM-R = Matanza-Riachuelo River Basin, CAS = South Creek Basin, FC 1 = First 
Coastal Strip of La Plata River, and FC2 = Second Coastal Strip of La Plata River 
2. The groundwater system in the basin comprises two aquifers. The shallow aquifer is 
called Pampean0 or Epipuelche and is extremely vulnerable to human activities. The deeper and 
less vulnerable aquifer is called Puelche. The Epipuelche aquifer has suffered severe 
environmental damage, and the Puelche is used for productive activities and human water 
consumption. 
3. The natural hydrological system has been altered by urban infrastructure including flood 
protection, drinking water networks, and sewerage systems, which import wastewater from the 
City of Buenos Aires into the basin. The M-R River’s flow is also influenced by La Plata River. 
44 
<<PAGE 54>>
These conditions, combined with the basin’s topography, urbanization, groundwater system, and 
urban infrastructure, make the lower part of the basin very vulnerable to flooding. 
4. The Matanza-Riachuelo River Basin should be seen as a territorial unit whose main 
development features result from the interaction of a typology of urban sprawl over a sensitive 
environmental area. Although various occupational patterns are found in the basin, in general the 
basin suffers from high levels of environmental degradation, especially in unregulated low- 
income urban areas. 
5. Three different zones are clearly identified in the basin: (i) the lower basin, which is 
highly urbanized, (ii) the middle basin, which presents a combination of consolidated urban areas 
and peri-urban areas, and (iii) the upper basin, a rural area where environmental conditions are 
still adequate. 
6. The lower basin. This is a medium-high-density urban area, with approximately 70 
percent of the basin’s 3.5 million inhabitants. It includes the southern part of the City of Buenos 
Aires and the municipalities of Avellaneda, Lanus, and Lomas de Zamora. Despite the generally 
high urban consolidation levels found in this part of the basin, there are still large vacant sections 
of land, zones where population density exceeds norms, and unregulated settlements. Land use is 
highly mixed, including residences, warehouses, and industries. The dominance of residential 
use increases in the upper portion of the lower basin, although this dynamic varies by area. 
Compared to the rest of the metropolitan area, this portion of the basin contains a large number 
of industries. The number of abandoned and dilapidated industrial buildings is striking, as are the 
numbers of illegal dumpsites and unregulated-low-income settlements along the riverbanks. The 
highest water contamination loads, resulting from raw domestic and industrial effluents, are 
found in this part of the basin. Nevertheless, good quality and planned residential settlements are 
also found here. 
7. The middle basin. The urban density in this area varies from medium to low. The 
municipalities in this section of the basin are Almirante Brown, Esteban Echeverria, Ezeiza, La 
Matanza, and Merlo. Land use is predominantly residential, with the exception of commercial 
activities along the main transportation corridors and small and medium industries. Urban 
consolidated areas cover part of this portion of the basin, and urban sprawl is evident where low- 
density areas dominate and many large vacant land polygons are still found. There are also clubs 
and large green areas that offer recreational opportunities for the metropolitan population, as well 
as important logistical assets such as railroad warehouses and the largest food wholesale market 
in Buenos Aires. Illegal solid waste dumpsites are a major problem in the area, and the number 
of unregulated low-income settlements has increased significantly. 
8. Upper basin. This area is predominantly rural, with some small and low-density urban 
centers. The municipalities in the area are Caiiuelas, San Vicente, General Las Heras, and 
Marcos Paz. 
B. Current Status of Water Quality 
9. The Matanza-Riachuelo River has been used historically as the main drainage system of 
part of the City of Buenos Aires and some of its surrounding municipalities. The rapid increase 
45 
<<PAGE 55>>
in population and accompanying regional economic development has significantly worsened the 
natural ecological environment of the basin. Currently the main environmental problems 
contributing to the deterioration of water quality are point and non-point source urban and 
industrial discharges into the river and groundwater system, as well as the uneven infrastructure 
development of potable water and sewerage networks, resulting in a net increase of wastewater 
inflows into the basin. 
10. Figures 1.2 and 1.3 show the biological oxygen demand (BOD) concentration and 
dissolved oxygen (DO) concentration respectively, in the M-R River throughout its length. The 
figures show two situations with the low and medium flows that the river experiences more than 
70 percent of the time. The figures show the high level of organic matter (measured in terms of 
BOD), one of the major causes of environmental degradation. This high level of organic matter, 
combined with low flow velocities, produces very low values of dissolved oxygen, resulting in 
an anoxic state, odors, methane emissions, unsightliness, limited use, and significant health risks.

demand (BOD) concentrations along the M-R River.

Causes of Water and Environmental Degradation 
12. Institutional framework. The overlapping institutional responsibilities in this complex 
area have worsened the environmental conditions in the basin. A new Matanza-Riachuelo Basin 
Authority (Autoridad de Cuenca Matanza-Riachuelo, or ACUMAR) was created in 2006 with a 
broad mandate and enforcement authority to address the coordination issues in the basin. 
ACUMAR’s board of directors includes representatives from the national, provincial, and 
municipal governments and is chaired by the Secretariat of the Environment and Sustainable 
Development (Secretaria de Ambiente y Desarrollo Sustentable, or SAyDS). 
13. The institutions with some jurisdiction over the basin area include 11 institutions at the 
national level, 4 at the provincial level, 7 at the municipal level, and 14 municipalities and 4 
institutions with a concession area larger than the City of Buenos Aires (but one that does not 
cover the whole basin). 
14. Sewage management. The current sewerage system in the Argentina Water and 
Sanitation (Agua y Saneamientos Argentinos S. A. , or AySA) concession area is divided into four 
operational watershed systems: Norte, Sudoeste, el Jaguel, and Wilde-Berazategui. Each of these 
areas has a treatment plant. The average operational flow of the Norte watershed is 0.67 m3/s, 
that of the Sudoeste is 1.95 m3/s, that of El Jaguel is 0.15 m3/s, and that of the Berazategui is 23 
m3/second. Most of the wastewater from the City of Buenos Aires is conveyed to the Berazategui 
District in the southwest and discharged to La Plata River through a sub-aquatic outfall. 
15. Based on this situation, three main sewers were constructed, which convey an average 
flow of around 18 m3/second of wastewater to the existing outfall located in Berazategui. The 
Berazategui outfall-3.8 meters in diameter and 2.5 km in length-discharges the wastewater 
into La Plata River. The plan to construct a fourth main sewer to Berazategui, a preliminary 
treatment plant in Berazategui, and a longer outfall at this site with capacity to discharge the 
entire wastewater flow of the Buenos Aires metropolitan area, was never completed. 
16. This system does not allow any flexibility or increase in network coverage with 
reasonable operating security. The existing metropolitan area wastewater evacuation system is 
clearly inadequate, as a significant portion of raw wastewater is currently discharged into the 
rivers and tributaries that cross the metropolitan area (principally to the M-R and Reconquista 
47 
<<PAGE 57>>
rivers) or directly in the coastal zone of La Plata River. Consequently, the rivers and tributaries, 
as well as the coastal strip of La Plata River, are highly polluted. Moreover, the length and 
configuration (absence of diffusers) of the existing outfall at Berazategui do not allow adequate 
discharge to La Plata River. 
17. AySA has designed a new plan that will allow it to incorporate the M-R River Basin’s 
environmental objectives, expand its network coverage, and reduce the vulnerability of the 
system. For these purposes, AySA decided to divide the Wilde-Berazategui operational 
watershed system into two (Berazategui and Riachuelo), building ‘a treatment plant and all the 
needed connection structures in this “new” operational watershed system and changing the 
sewage management structure in the basin. 
18. Industrial pollution. The Matanza-Riachuelo is the most contaminated river basin in 
Argentina and one of the country’s most visible environmental problems. Over the past hundred 
years, the M-R River Basin has been used as a sewage sink for the entire City of Buenos Aires. 
Pollution levels have steadily increased with urbanization in the metropolitan area of Buenos 
Aires and industrial growth within the basin. Based on estimations carried out in the M-R River 
Basin, there are more than 4,000 industrial facilities located in the lower and middle sections of 
the basin. Industries are typically located in clusters, but a significant number are also 
interspersed with residential areas (e.g., tanneries in Lanus). As a result, the pollution problem in 
the basin is compounded by the mixing of industrial and domestic wastewater, limited coverage 
of the sewerage network, and the absence of wastewater treatment. In areas without adequate 
sewerage, industrial and domestic wastewater is drained through open channels or infiltrates the 
groundwater. 
19. The industrial growth in the basin has been rapid and includes both large enterprises and 
numerous small and medium enterprises (SMEs). Since 2002, the number of small and medium 
industries in the basin has grown significantly, with important industrial clusters in La Boca (in 
the City of Buenos Aires), Lanus, Avellaneda, and Lomas de Zamora. The main industries 
operating along the banks of the basin are food processing (dairy production and meat 
processing), tanneries, chemical, petrochemical, pulp and paper, textiles, and electroplating and 
metal production. Many of these industries dump their effluents directly into the river because of 
a lack of coordinated action among the various levels of government. In addition, the wide range 
of industrial types and sizes has made effective control more difficult. 
20. Existing capacity and pollution loads. The current capacity of the sewerage system and 
the four existing wastewater treatment plants in AySA’s concession area is far from sufficient to 
handle the existing domestic and industrial wastewater load. The vast majority of industries do 
not have any pre-treatment or process optimization before discharging wastewater into the 
sewerage system or surface water, resulting in significant increases in the organic and toxic loads 
in the existing sewerage network as well as the receiving water bodies. 
21. Initial estimates suggest that the industrial sector contributes almost 68 tons a day of 
organic waste (measured by BOD), which accounts for 73 percent of the total BOD discharged 
into the M-R River Basin. A preliminary analysis was done of industrial contamination in the 
basin by industrial sector and pollutant type (organic as well as toxic substances). The analysis 
found that tanneries in the basin contribute about 26,000 tons of BOD per year, or 43 percent of 
48 
<<PAGE 58>>
the total discharge into the basin, while meat processing and dairy industries together contribute 
about 10,300 tons of BOD annually, or 17 percent of the total. Levels of toxic effluents are also 
high, particularly for tanneries, which discharge 34 tons per year of chromium VI (1 00 percent of 
total discharge to the basin) as well as 20,000 tondyear of sulfates (100 percent of the total 
discharge to the basin). Toxic discharge from the metallurgical industry is also significant and 
includes chromium, zinc, and cyanide. The government has emphasized the need for additional 
data collection and modeling to validate these results. 
Municipality 
Almirante Brown 
22. Industrial effluents have different physical, biological, and chemical characteristics and 
flows, depending largely on the process and technology used. As a result, it is not possible to 
treat all industrial effluents in a single treatment plant as can be done for domestic wastewater, 
unless a group of homogenous industries is located in close proximity, as in some areas of 
Avellaneda and Lanus.

23. Compliance. Several laws and regulations in Argentina address issues relating to 
industrial pollution, with the Federal Government primarily responsible for environmental policy 
and regional governments for control and enforcement. Environmental standards, as well as 
maximum permissible limits for effluent and emission discharges exist on paper2*, but evidence 
shows that compliance is minimal as a consequence of unclear responsibilities across 
government jurisdictions, weak enforcement systems, and inadequate monitoring and control 
capacity in terms of human resources and technical expertise. The absence of a corporate culture 
of environmental responsibility, even within larger enterprises, compounds the problem. 
Caiiuelas 
Esteban Echeverria

of Argentina, have been precarious since the 2001 economic crisis. Levels of coverage are very 
low in absolute terms and particularly in relative terms when compared with the national 
average, considering the importance of the area in economic terms (gross domestic product, or 
GDP). Table 1.2 shows the sewerage service coverage in each municipality in the basin.

25. Conventional sanitation services in the area have not expanded at the same rate as the 
population. Residents with no access to piped water have traditionally tended to obtain their 
water through wells, generally extracting directly from the Puelche. Low-income residents 
normally obtain water from the upper water table (Epipuelche) because it is closest to the surface 
and therefore less expensive to exploit, even though it is highly contaminated. These low-income 
groups also tend to concentrate in flood-prone areas, where the water table is very high.

26. In areas with no sewerage, the most common sanitation systems are septic tanks and pits. 
Their construction is frequently precarious, resulting in contamination of the upper water table. 
As cleaning and emptying these tanks costs approximately ARS5O per service, there is an 
additional incentive for illegal dumping in storm sewers (if available), streets, surface water 
bodies, or groundwater through infiltration.

3,265,129 
27. Flood control. The area of interest is particularly vulnerable to urban and rural flooding. 
Increased runoff, clogged sewers, rising water tables, and the basin’s location in a very flat and 
low-lying area increase the vulnerability. 
28. Flooding affects the poorer segments of the population in particular. The lack of urban 
planning regulation or its enforcement has led to uncontrolled settlement of predominantly low- 
income dwellers in the more flood-prone locations. There are large unmet demands for drainage 
infrastructure, as urban centers have expanded without updating their storm water facilities. 
Government Stratem 
29. The idea of large-scale investments to improve the M-R River Basin is not new. In fact, 
governments for decades have discussed the importance of clean-up, and in the mid-l990s, the 
Government completed a comprehensive M-R Environmental Management Plan (PGA) and took 
a US$250 million Inter American Development Bank (IADB) loan to help finance 
50 
<<PAGE 60>>
implementation. However, 12 years later, the IADB loan had only disbursed US$lO million, and 
the Government is only now committing another US$90 million for urgent clean-up activities. 
30. In response to these problems, a high-profile lawsuit2’ was filed against the national 
government, the Province of Buenos Aires, the City of Buenos Aires, and 44 industries in 2003 
by people affected by environmental degradation. In 2006, the Supreme Court issued a ruling 
calling on the Government to put in place a concrete action plan to improve environmental 
conditions in the basin. As a result, the Integrated Cleanup Plan for the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin 
(Plan Integral de Saneamiento de la Cuenca Matanza-Riachuelo, PISA) was designed, and 
ACUMAR was created by national law to implement this plan. In July 2008, the Supreme Court 
issued its final ruling establishing general criteria to ensure that the PISA is executed, setting a 
timeline for its main milestones. 
31. The PISA’s objectives are (i) improvement of the quality of life, (ii) improvement of the 
quality of the air, water and soil, and (iii) prevention of future damage. The PISA represents an 
integrated approach to address the problems of the basin. The plan’s main components are listed 
below. 
(i) Institutional strengthening. The plan highlights the need for more coordinated action 
among all institutional actors in the basin and states the need for a clearer regulatory and 
control framework. 
(ii) Cleanup and .regional planning. The plan seeks proper management of all effluents 
from outside and within the basin and comprises actions on sewerage systems, industrial 
control, and treatment and disposal of wastewater. 
(iii) Social interventions. PISA addresses the needs of the more than 2 million people living 
in a situation of high environmental risk in an area where the poverty map, the flooding 
map, and high environmental impact industries map overlap. The social component 
comprises interventions in regional planning, including land use and urban planning. 
32. The Supreme Court has also taken a more proactive role in the area of environmental 
compliance. The Supreme Court case and growing public pressure has forced SAyDS to update 
its policy framework and improve its dialogue and negotiation capacity with industries to ensure 
that their effluents, emissions, and waste management practices comply with environmental 
regulations and permissible limits for effluent discharges to sewage networks, within a specified 
timeframe. As a result of this process, Plans for Industrial Restructuring (Planes de Reconversion 
Industrial, or PRIs)~’ are being developed and signed, committing enterprises to reach mutually 
agreed discharge levels by certain dates and the regulatory agency to exempt these companies 
from non-compliance penalties before the agreed deadline. 
33. The new strategy contemplated in the PISA tackles the main sources of pollution in the 
M-R River: (i) industrial discharges into the M-R River Basin, (ii) secondary treatment plant 
*’ “Mendoza Case”, Beatriz Silva and others against the National government and others. 
30 The environmental standards set under the PRIs are customized to individual enterprises and based on the capacity 
of the enterprises to comply with environmental standards and permissible limits for discharging effluents into 
AySA’s sewage network as articulated in Resolutions 1/2007 and 2/2008. 
51 
<<PAGE 61>>
effluents in the M-R River Basin, and (iii) non-point sources of domestic wastewater from 
urbanized areas without sewerage coverage. 
34. Based on modeling simulations, ACUMAR developed different scenarios for managing 
wastewater from domestic and industrial origins in the basin in collaboration with the National 
Technological University (Universidad Tecnoldgicu Nucional, or UTN). The simulations 
showed that the recuperation of the water quality in the M-R River Basin from its current anoxic 
state (consumption of oxygen from the degradation of organic waste) to an oxygenated condition 
(without emission of odors and allowing for recreational uses without direct water contact) 
requires the following action: 
90-95 percent reduction in organic loads of industrial brigin with the implementation 
of PRIs and industrial effluents connected to AySA’s sewerage network with 
discharge to the La Plata River; 
(ii) Elimination of the discharge from AySA’s secondary treatment plants into the M-R 
River; 
(iii) Elimination of 80-90 percent of non-point domestic wastewater discharges through 
the provision of sewerage networks in urbanized areas that currently lack these 
services.

35. While these initial results are indicative of the size of the challenges ahead, the 
Government has emphasized the need for additional data collection and modeling to validate 
these results. To this end, funds have been set aside under component 4 of the project.

Annex 4: Detailed Project Description 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project 
Macro-component 1: Sanitation 
Component 1: Sanitation (US$1,309 million total, US$694 million loan) 
1. This component includes the construction and external engineering supervision of 
sanitation infrastructure for the collection, treatment, and disposal of the wastewater of the 
Matanza-Riachuelo (M-R) basin and the AySA concession area. These works help meet two 
objectives including (i) extending and improving the level of coverage and quality of 
sewerage services in the M-R River Basin and in the entire AySA concession area, and (ii) 
contributing to the recovery and preservation of the quality of water bodies in the entire M-R 
River Basin and in the La Plata River. 
2. Through a combination of sanitation works including the expansion and extension of 
collection systems, construction of treatment plants and pumping stations, and construction of 
new sub-aquatic outfalls, this project component aims to intercept most of the domestic and 
industrial wastewater in the AySA concession area3’ as well as the wastewater of the 
municipalities in the upper M-R River Basin, and divert this entire wastewater flow, after 
subjecting it to preliminary treatment, to La Plata River through long and effective sub- 
aquatic outfalls. The progressive elimination of point sources of contamination from the M-R 
River needs to be accompanied by an end to the dumping of municipal solid wastes into the 
river and on its bank. Solid waste management activities in the basin are undertaken by 
ACUMAR as part of the PISA using other financing sources. 
3. 
option was chosen for the following reasons: 
Among the various alternatives considered for the sanitation component, the selected 
It takes into account the proper dimensioning of the wastewater collection system, 
thereby limiting discharges of wastewater and eMuents into inland rivers such as the 
M-R and its tributaries. These receiving bodies, which have limited auto-purification 
capacity, will be protected by progressively diverting all collected wastewater to the 
sewerage network and discharging it to La Plata River (which has ample 
auto-purification capacity) after appropriate treatment. 
It distributes the flow between the two preliminary treatment plants (Riachuelo and 
Berazategui) in order to provide greater flexibility and security in the main 
conveyance and treatment systems. The incorporation of the Riachuelo system makes 
it possible to divide wastewater flows between the two plants and perform treatment 
and effluent discharge independently in each one, thereby decentralizing the existing 
sewerage system and reducing the impact of the effluents discharged to La Plata 
River. 
It intercepts major dry season drainage flows in the area served by AySA’s existing 
sewerage network in order to substantially reduce the pollution of the M-R and La 
Plata Rivers, since during the dry season the drainage system conveys mostly 
wastewater discharged to it through illegal or erratic connections. 
. 
. 
. 
35 Except wastewater that is diverted to the North treatment plant, which discharges its effluent to the 
Reconquista River. 
58 
<<PAGE 68>>
It allows all industries located in the area served by AySA that pre-treat their 
wastewater to the level required by the norms to discharge their treated wastewater to 
the sewerage network, thereby reducing the required level of in situ treatment. 
It increases the sewerage system capacity and allows the entire population located in 
the M-R River Basin to be connected to the sewerage network. 
4. Because of the scale, complexity, and high cost of the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin 
Sustainable Development Project, the sanitation component will be implemented in two 
stages. Completion of sanitation construction works under both APL phases is required to 
attain the objective of cleaning up the M-R River Basin. The first stage of Component 1 
(financed during APL-1) will consist of the following works: a collector on the left bank of 
the M-R River in the lower part of the basin, the Baja Costanera bypass collector, a collector 
on the right bank of the M-R River starting in the upper middle part of the basin and reaching 
its lower part, an industrial wastes collector and pumping station on the right bank of the M-R 
River, the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant, the two Riachuelo pumping stations, and 
the Riachuelo outfall. While the left bank conveyance system (Left Bank collector and the 
Baja Costanera bypass collector) has been front-loaded in the early years of APL-1, the Right 
Bank collector (CMD) is scheduled for construction towards the end of APL-1. Moreover, 
given the size of the investments required, AySA will continue to study technical alternatives 
to the CMD. Any alternative solution would require approval from SAyDS and ACUMAR as 
well as the Bank. 
5. The second stage of Component 1 (mostly financed through APL-2) will consist of 
the following works: the Baja Costanera expansion collector, the Berazategui effluent 
pumping station, the Berazategui outfall, and additional primary and secondary sewerage 
networks in the basin. The Berazategui preliminary treatment plant, which is hnctionally part 
of APL-2 , is being financed and constructed separately by the Government. The investments 
in sewerage networks financed under the project are only part of the total investment required 
for networks, and it is estimated that additional hnds will be mobilized by AySA to ensure 
meeting its coverage targets. The diagram of Component 1 is presented in Fig. 4.1, in which 
works under APL-1 and APL-2 can be identified.

A detailed description of the works under Component 1 is presented below: 
(i) Construction/extension of collectors and complementary works 
6. The main wastewater collection network, and the conveyance and distribution system 
of wastewater to the two preliminary treatment plants are critical parts of the project, and 
alternatives for this system were considered. The selected collection and conveyance system 
consists of constructing the Left Bank collector (Colector Margen Izquierda or CMI) to 
convey the wastewater flows generated in the AySA concession area located in the lower part 
of the basin to the Riachuelo outfall and constructing the Right Bank collector (Colector 
Margen Derecha or CMD) to convey the wastewater generated in the AySA concession area 
in the middle and upper middle parts of the basin, as well as the wastewater generated in the 
upper part of the basin outside the concession area of AySA to the Berazategui outfall. Both 
collectors will be constructed during APL-1 thereby ensuring that the main conveyance 
infrastructure required for the clean-up of the M-R River Basin will be in place in a relatively 
short period of time. The Left Bank collector will be about 12 km long, with diameters 
ranging from 0.8 meters to 2.9 meters and will discharge into the Baja Costanera bypass 
collector - with a 4.5 m diameter and a length of 5 km - which in turn discharges through the 
Riachuelo outfall. The Right Bank collector is expected to be constructed with capacity to 
convey the wastewater of the upper and middle parts of the Basin and discharge it to the third 
cloaca rnkirna, which will convey the wastewater to the Berazategui preliminary treatment 
plant and outfall. The CMD length has been estimated to be about 37 km and its estimated 
diameter varies between 2.3 m in its upper part and 3.5 m in its lower part. The CMD 
collection system will mainly receive and transport wastewater collected in the coverage area 
of the Southwest, Jaguel, Fiorito, and Laferrere treatment plants, as well as wastewater 
60 
<<PAGE 70>>
generated in the municipalities of the upper M-R River Basin located outside the AySA 
concession area. 
7. The conveyance system also includes complementary works financed under APL-1 
such as connections between the two operational watershed systems (Riachuelo and 
Berazategui), and the interception of storm water. Under APL-2, it includes the Baja 
Costanera Expansion collector (Radio Antiguo section), and the Baja Costanera Expansion 
collector (Federal Capital -Vicente L6pez section). These two sections of the Baja Costanera 
Expansion collector constitute only the first stage of this collector. An additional stage will be 
financed by AySA from its own sources. 
8. During the first stage, an industrial wastes conveyance pipe and pumping station will 
be constructed on the right bank of the M-R River to collect industrial effluents from about 
23 industries, thereby diverting them from the M-R River. This system will significantly 
reduce industrial waste discharges into the lower part of the M-R River. 
9. The aforementioned systems will collect the wastewater generated in the AySA 
concession area (excluding those generated in the Reconquista basin) and the wastewater 
generated in the upper basin and will distribute them between the two planned outfalls, so 
that a peak flow of 25 m3/s will be conveyed to the Riachuelo outfall and 33 m3/s will be 
conveyed to the Berazategui outfall. The treatment plants and outfalls will be designed and 
constructed to handle these peak flows. These systems are expected to provide sufficient 
capacity for approximately 20 years. AySA and ACUMAR plan to run, as part of the 
communication plan, campaigns for controlling water demand. In Buenos Aires, the water 
demand per capita is extremely high, reaching average values of over 400 liters/day/capita. 
This high water demand results in high flows of domestic wastewater. It is expected that 
through demand control the per capita water consumption in the city, and, consequently, the 
wastewater production per capita will decline with time so that the rate of increase in the 
generated wastewater flows declines and the systems constructed under the project will 
provide a capacity for much more than 20 years.’Nonetheless, if the demand control strategy 
is less successful than expected over the next two decades, the treatment plants will need to 
be expanded, and a parallel outfall will have to be constructed at the Riachuelo site. 
(ii) Treatment plants and pumping stations 
10. Prior to discharging effluents through each outfall to the La Plata River, the collected 
wastewater will be subjected to preliminary treatment at two plants. The Riachuelo plant, 
which will be financed through this project, is to be located in an area near La Plata River in 
the Dock Sud area. The Berazategui preliminary treatment plant, which is being financed by 
the Government using its own finds complemented by a loan from Brazil’s BNDES, is to be 
located at Berazategui near the inlet of the existing 2.5 km outfall. These preliminary 
treatment plants consist of coarse screens to retain heavy material, followed by milliscreens 
with 6 mm (or smaller) openings for removal of fine material, and then by aerated grit 
chambers to remove sand and floating material such as oil and grease. The Riachuelo 
preliminary treatment plant is designed to receive a peak flow of 25 m3/s of wastewater 
collected from the city of Buenos Aires and the lower part of the M-R River Basin. The 
Berazategui preliminary treatment plant was designed to receive a peak flow of 33.5 m3/s of 
wastewater collected from the right bank area of the M-R River, from the middle and upper 
parts of the M-R River Basin which will be collected by the Right Bank collector at a peak 
flow of about 12 m3/s, and from the upper part of the basin at a peak flow of about 1.5 m3/s, 
61 
<<PAGE 71>>
also collected by the Right Bank collector. The peak conveyance capacity of the Right Bank 
collector will be designed for 13.5 m3/s. 
11. The Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant site will include two pumping stations, a 
lifting station at the entrance to the treatment plant, and a second station to discharge the 
effluent to La Plata River through the outfall. The Berazategui plant site will have only one 
pumping station (the effluent discharge station), as the existing Wilde pumping station will 
pump the raw sewage into the treatment plant. While the efficiency of removing solids and 
organic matter by the preliminary treatment plants appears low, the plants play an important 
role in removing important waste material contained in the raw sewage. This waste material 
includes (i) coarse solids such as sand and grains, which, if not removed, tend to settle rapidly 
and accumulate around the outfall discharge points and are not carried away by the flows in 
the receiving water body, (ii) coarse floatable materials such as plastic bags, which, if not 
removed, float on the surface of the receiving water body, causing aesthetic problems, and 
(iii) oil and grease, which, if not removed, form an impermeable layer on the surface of the 
water, preventing re-aeration and oxygen transfer to the water which could result in oxygen 
depletion in the diffuser zone. 
12. The Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant will generate approximately (i) 132 m3/day 
of solid waste residues, (ii) 29-74 m3/day of sand, and (iii) 48-96 m3/day of oil and grease, 
totaling about 200-300 tons/day (m3/day) of waste. The Berazategui preliminary treatment 
plant, during the first stage of its (full) operation, will generate approximately (i) 158 m3/day 
of solid waste residues, (ii) 35-88 m3/day of sand, and (iii) 57-128 m3/day of oil and grease, 
totaling about 249-374 tons/day (m3/day) of waste. 
13. The quantity of solid waste generated at both treatment plants is expected to increase 
over time with increased wastewater flows. During the initial years of the operation of the 
plants, the quantity of solid waste will vary from 459 to 676 tons/day (m3/day) and will be 
removed by trucks or barges (or a combination of the two) and deposited in a sanitary landfill 
managed by the State Company for the Ecological Coordination of the Metropolitan Area 
(Coordinacidn Ecoldgica Area Metropolitana Sociedad del Estado, or CEAMSE)36 along 
with the rest of the solid waste generated in Buenos Aires. Installations for the recovery of 
sand for reuse and generation of energy or production of biofuels from the oil are being 
considered for inclusion in the project at a later stage, but research is required before taking 
specific related actions. The costs related to the proper management of these solids will be 
included in the O&M costs of AySA and not subject to Bank financing. 
(iii) Construction of new sub-aquatic outfalls 
14. The project will finance the construction of two new sub-aquatic outfalls over the two 
APL phases. The Riachuelo outfall, to be financed during APL-1, is located in the Dock Sud 
area. This outfall measures 3.8 m in internal diameter and is 11.5 km long. The final 2.3 km 
are the diffuser The Berazategui outfall, expected to be financed under APL-2, is 
located near the existing short outfall, measures 3.8 m in internal diameter, and is 7.5 km 
long. The final 3.5 km are the diffuser zone. 
36 CEAMSE is the public company in charge of solid waste management in the City of Buenos Aires and 34 
municipalities in Buenos Aires Province. 
37 Dimensions for the Riachuelo outfall are indicative. AySA is in the process of finalizing bidding stage 
designs. Final designs will be prepared by construction contractor(s). 
62 
<<PAGE 72>>
15. The design peak flow of the Riachuelo outfall is 25 m3/s, and that of the Berazategui 
outfall is 33.5 m /s. Because the Berazategui outfall is designed to convey a peak wastewater 
flow rate of 13.5 m3/s from the middle and upper sections of the M-R River Basin, the entire 
wastewater flow from municipal and industrial sources in the basin will reach the two 
outfalls. This is the principle of the clean-up of the basin and of the recovery of the water 
quality of its inland waters, which will enable the sustainable development of the basin. The 
clean-up will be achieved through progressive elimination of the point sources of 
contamination of the rivers and their discharge to the sewage collection system.

Buenos Aires, which already has full coverage). The investments in sewerage networks 
financed under the project are only part of the total investment required for networks and it is 
expected that additional funds will be mobilized by AySA to ensure its coverage targets are 
met.

18. Technical information. Table 4.2 presents technical information regarding some 
works of Component 1, as well as the expected construction techniques and materials. The 
main conveyance system pipes and the outfall pipes will be installed using tunneling (i.e., 
trenchless methods). Costs of the safe management of dirt fiom tunneling will be 
incorporated in the respective construction contracts. Pipes up to 3 m in diameter may be 
installed by pipe jacking, while larger-diameter pipes will be constructed from pipe segments 
assembled inside the tunnel. For pipe installation by pipe jacking, shafts will be constructed 
every 1-1.5 km, and for pipe installation in tunnels by segments, shafts will be constructed 
every 4-5 km.

Territorial Management and Institutional Strengthening 
APL-2 
Component 2: Industrial Pollution Abatement (US75.4 million total, US$60.3 million 
loan) 
19. The objective of this component is to contribute to the progressive elimination of 
point-source industrial wastewater discharges (organic as well as toxic effluents) many of 
which currently enter the Matanza-Riachuelo (M-R) river. This process is expected to occur 
over the next 15-20 years and is essential to the overall environmental recovery of the river. 
This component of the project represents an additional tool to clean-up the M-R River by 
diverting treated industrial discharges to the Rio de la Plata through an improved, expanded 
and more secure system of sewage management (financed by Component 1).

20. Based on initial estimates, there are approximately 4,100 registered industries in the 
M-R River Basin area that contribute a significant part of the total organic load currently 
discharged to the M-R River, as well as virtually all toxic discharges. Of these, approximately 
1,600 are connected to the AySA network, while the remaining 2,500 currently discharge 
effluents directly into the M-R River. ACUMAR will first focus on the 50 main industrial 
polluters that contribute approximately 95 percent of the total industrial organic load and 170 
industries with the highest toxic loads that currently discharge to the M-R River, using 
technical assistance made available through this component. As part of the Government’s 
strategy, plans for industrial restructuring (Planes de Reconversion Industrial, or PRIs), 
including indicators, targets, and deadlines will be developed and signed with these 
industries. While the Bank will help the Government monitor progress on PRIs signed with 
the largest industrial polluters (see sub-components (i) through (iv) below), it has little 
leverage for enforcing them unless firms are beneficiaries of the matching grants program 
financed under sub-component (v) of the loan. The absence of direct leverage 
notwithstanding, the design and implementation of a program for encouraging corporate 
environmental responsibility under sub-component (iv) is expected to create a public 
environment increasingly favorable to industrial clean-up. 
21. At a broad level, ACUMAR will require industries currently discharging to the M-R 
River to instead connect to the AySA sewage network for ultimate discharge into the Rio de 
64 
<<PAGE 74>>
la Plata after appropriate treatment. Additionally, the Government intends to also require 
identified contaminating industries (agentes contaminantes) to sign Planes de Reconversidn 
Industriales (PRIs) committing them to making necessary investments to reduce effluent 
loads with targets and deadlines. The strategy for industrial  discharge^^^, which will be 
adjusted through this project, includes the following: 
- Those industries that can currently connect to the sewage network will be required to 
reduce their organic load to under 200 mg/l and treat their toxic effluents before 
discharging to the network.39 
- For industries that cannot currently access the AySA network, ACUMAR will require 
that interim solutions (such as developing cleaner production plans in accordance with 
their PRIs) be developed while the construction/extension of the collectors (financed 
under Component 1) is undertaken. Subsequently, these industries will be expected to 
connect and discharge their treated effluents to the sewage network for transport to 
the Rio de la Plata. 
- For industries that may not have access even after the collectors are constructed/ 
extended (because AySA’s concession area does not cover the entire M-R River 
Basin), ACUMAR envisions developing alternative solutions such as using trucks to 
collect treated effluents for discharge at points along AySA’s network or building 
collectors to connect to AySA’s existing sewerage network. 
22. Activities under Component 2 will complement those under Component 1 in 
contributing to the clean-up the M-R River. This component combines technical assistance 
(TA) measures to improve environmental monitoring, control, and enforcement by 
ACUMAR with a matching grants program to support private sector investments in pollution 
control and cleaner production, as well as the construction of specific centralized treatment 
facilities included in the relevant plans for industrial restructuring (Planes de Reconversion 
Industrial, or PRIs). 
23. Technical assistance activities will be oriented to increase the monitoring and 
implementation capacity of the Government through four sub components. Part of the TA 
38 This strategy is in line with the World Bank Group’s Environmental Health and Safety Guidelines relating to 
Wastewater and Ambient Water Quality, which states that “when wastewater treatment is requiredprior to 
discharge, the level oftreatment should be based on: . Whether wastewater is being discharged to a sanitary 
sewer system, or to surface waters; . National and local standards as reflected in permit requirements and 
sewer system capacity to convey and treat wastewater if discharge is to sanitary sewer; Assimilative capacity 
ofthe receiving water for the load of contaminant being discharged wastewater if discharge is to surface water; 
, Intended use ofthe receiving water body (e.g. as a source of drinking water, recreation, irrigation, navigation, 
or other).. .” ACUMAR is in the process of finalizing norms/ regulations relating to uses and quality of the 
receiving water bodies in the short, medium and long terms. Furthermore, water quality modeling has been the 
underlying basis of choosing the technical solution for the M-R project, and considers the assimilative capacity 
of Rio de la Plata. 
39 The admissible limits for organic and toxic substances that need to be reached before industries can discharge 
to a sewage network are in ACUMAR’s Resolutions 112007 and 212008 and match those required by AySA for 
effluent discharges into their sewage networks. This is in line with the World Bank Group’s Environmental 
Health and Safety Guidelines relating to Wastewater and Ambient Water Quality specifically on discharges to 
sanitary sewer systems, which states “Discharges of industrial wastewater, sanitary wastewater, wastewater 
from utility operations or stormwater into public or private wastewater treatment systems should meet the 
pretreatment and monitoring requirements ofthe sewer treatment system into which it discharges.“ ; as well as 
the EHS Guideline on Water and Sanitation, which states “Treatment or pre-treatment to neutralize or remove 
toxic chemicals should ideally take place at the industrial facility itseK prior to discharge ofthe efluent to the 
sewer or water body...”.
