will be targeted to the establishment of a registration system for industries located in the 
basin (see subcomponent 1). The information from this registry will serve as a basis for the 
management of PRIs and their implementation by ACUMAR. 
24. The initial phase of the matching grants program will be launched during APL-1, to 
support private sector investments in pollution control and cleaner production measures. This 
initial phase of the program will target a limited number (20-50) of small and medium 
enterprises (SMEs) which have weaker financial and technical capacity to comply with 
environmental regulations. While the initial phase of the program will only reach a small 
sample of eligible SMEs, it aims to inform the project by (i) learning and sharing how SME- 
specific cleaner production plans are applied; (ii) encouraging associations of SMEs to adopt 
cost-effective solutions for reaching environmental compliance; and (iii) facilitating support 
for SMEs to gain access to commercial credit for cleaner production investments.

(i) Support pollution diagnostics and policy analysis 
26. This sub-component will support pollution diagnostics carried out by 
SAyDUACUMAR using the baseline data in the GIS database (financed under component 3) 
and the application of water quality modeling to design a framework to establish appropriate 
effluent loads for industries located in the M-R River Basin. The database will include 
baseline information on about 20 parameters relating to water consumption and pollution 
loads (organic and toxic) discharged by each of the industries. In addition, this sub- 
component will support an analysis of possible social impacts of industrial conversion within 
the M-R River Basin. 
27. The information from this centralized database, among other inputs, will help in 
evaluating the effluent loads from polluting agents discharged to the M-R, along with 
receptive capacity of the Rio de la Plata to accept these effluent discharges. Based on this 
baseline information, and corroborated by rapid assessments, all industries will be ranked by 
each polluting substance (organic as well as toxic) relative to the total effluents discharged to 
the M-R River basin and La Plata River by all the industries in the basin. The resulting 
information from these activities will serve as inputs for taking decisions on developing 
policies relating to pollution control and prevention. The pollution diagnostic includes an 
66 
<<PAGE 76>>
independent validation exercise of the list of contaminating industries as well as an analysis 
of the possible social impacts of activities relating to industrial pollution abatement within the 
M-R River Basin. 
28. This sub-component will also support work planned by ACUMAR and AySA for the 
inspection and control of water consumption and flow discharge of industries connected to 
AySA’s network with sampling for analysis of industrial effluents. 
(ii) Improvement in the monitoring system for industrial discharges 
29. This sub-component will enhance the system for monitoring the status of industrial 
waste discharge by supporting institutional pollution control infrastructure such as 
independent certified labs and training of their staff, While initially ACUMAR will use 
AySA labs for some water quality tests, later in the project it plans to use commercial labs or 
professional services. The purchase of some equipment may be required. This sub-component 
will also finance the hiring of technical audits for follow-up and verification to ensure the 
compliance of enterprises with their PRIs. 
(iii) Support for adjustments to the strategy for industrial discharges 
30. The diagnostic process in sub-component (i) will help determine current conditions in 
each industry relating to its production, operations, and facilities to control water 
consumption, flow discharge, and sampling to analyze industrial effluents. Based on these 
diagnostics, and within the framework of the general industrial discharge strategy, ACUMAR 
will be able to make adjustments to the strategy by defining (i) the specific targets and 
conditions to be fulfilled by individual enterprises, in compliance with the permissible limits 
for the discharge of pollutants into sewer networks as well as surface water bodies4’; and (ii) 
the volumes and quality of industrial discharges for determining the resulting level of 
treatment of industrial effluents or industrial restructuring required of the individual 
enterprises, and the design of the necessary treatment systems. This adjustment of the overall 
industrial discharge strategy for the M-R basin will be supported through this sub-component. 
(iv) Design and implementation of a program for encouraging corporate environmental 
responsibility 
31. This sub-component supports activities aimed at building a culture of corporate 
environmental responsibility, i.e. through the design and development of an environmental 
disclosure program to help build a culture of corporate environmental responsibility. Such 
disclosure programs have been successful in other countries in changing industrial behavior 
related to environmental compliance. Changes in corporate culture will be promoted through 
the annual publication by ACUMAR of firm compliance with their respective PRIs. 
(v) Support for pollution control and cleaner production investments by SMEs 
32. 
investments in pollution control and cleaner production processes. 
This sub-component aims to provide technical and financial support to SMEs for

The proposed scheme for the execution of this component, including the details of the 
1. Approval of PRIs by ACUMAR (according to ACUMAR's resolution on the subject). 
2. Prior determination of objective criteria for eligibility, selection, and financing 
decided within the framework of the Interjurisdiccional Commission of Industry 
(Comisidn Interjurisdiccional de Industria, CIjI) and contained in the regulation. 
3. Presentation of financing requests. 
4. Based on pre-determined objective criteria, SAyDS determines the SMEs to be 
selected and the amounts to finance, and signs agreements (CRIs) with the 
beneficiaries. 
5. Disbursements on the part of SAyDS in accordance with the timelines defined in the 
agreements. 
6. SAyDS will monitor financial execution under the grant agreements (CRIs) while 
compliance with action plans contained in the PRIs will be monitored by ACUMAR. 
35. Enterprises that are eligible for participation in the matching grants program should (i) 
be categorized as an SME4'; (ii) have a signed PRI; and (iii) lack access to commercial credit. 
Matching grants will be allocated to eligible enterprises, prioritizing on basis of the relative 
pollution load contributed by the enterprise. Firms signing CRIs with SAyDS will therefore 
constitute a subset of firms signing PRIs with ACUMAR. Detailed eligibility criteria for 
assistance to SMEs, as well as the specific disbursement modalities will be clearly defined 
and disclosed in the project's Operational Manual to ensure transparency of resource 
allocation. Moreover, the specific level of matching grants required from SMEs will be 
determined on an industry (sector) basis using best practice lessons, with special 
dispensations for micro  enterprise^.^^ These matching grants will not substitute for 
commercial credit, as they focus specifically on environmental investments for SMEs for 
whom bank financing is not available. The launch of this matching grants program will occur 
when SMEs in the basin have met the eligibility criteria, which includes having signed PRIs.

Component 3. Environmental Territorial Management (US$81.9 million total, USS65.5 
million loan proceeds) 
36. The main objective of the Environmental Territorial Management component is to 
improve public policies and programs for environmental territorial management in the M-R 
River Basin and steer the basin’s development trends in a more sustainable direction. This 
will be achieved through a two-fold approach of (i) providing technical assistance to improve 
a broad range of planning aspects associated with land use at the regional level and (ii) 
executing pilot investments in selected areas of the basin to improve the quality of life. 
37. Preparation strategy and pilot interventions. During the first year of implementation, 
the project will strengthen ACUMAR’s capacity to implement Component 3. Subsequently, 
key technical information will be updated to support decision making to select the priority 
area for pilot investments financed by the component in consultation with the basin’s 
stakeholders. 
38. Institutional arrangements. The nature of the activities proposed under this 
component requires decisive participation by provincial and municipal governments. This 
will be ensured through the participatory mechanisms that are part of ACUMAR’s structure 
and through specific implementation arrangements proposed for the component. 
39. Legal Framework. ACUMAR has the legal power to desigddevelop environmental 
territorial management programs, and to take the necessary measures to implement the PISA. 
The content of this component is aligned with the scope of the PISA and will contribute to its 
implementation. SAyDSlACUMAR will be responsible for the execution of this component. 
Additionally, in order to comply with Argentina’s legal framework, and prior to the carrying 
out of any pilot investment, a separate agreement will be signed between the Borrower 
(through SAyDS), ACUMAR and the different jurisdictional entities which have primary 
responsibility to carry out said investments (e.g. AySA, PBA and municipalities), whereby 
the relevant entity will provide its consent to permit SAyDS/ACUMAR to carry out and 
finance the pertinent pilot investment, and to accept the obligation to operate and maintain 
the pilot investment thereafter.

Table 4.4 : Activities and costs for Component 3: Environmental Territorial 
Management 
(i) Institutional Development for Environmental Territorial Planning 
4 1. This sub-component will finance technical assistance activities that strengthen 
institutional capacity for environmental territorial planning with a comprehensive 
development perspective for the entire Matanza-Riachuelo River Basin. SAyDS/ACUMAR 
will execute the activities under this sub-component in close collaboration with the Province 
of Buenos Aires and the municipalities. The legal and institutional framework that created 
ACUMAR provides it with a broad coordination role. Therefore, ACUMAR's institutional 
70 
<<PAGE 80>>
structure promotes the participation of the provincial and municipal governments. This sub- 
component includes the following activities: 
42. Develop a regional planning system for the Matanza Riachuelo River Basin. To 
promote more integrated regional, sectoral, and local public policies that integrate water 
resource protection, land use trends, and socio-economic interests, this activity will (i) review 
and update the Territorial Development Study prepared for ACUMAR in October, 2007, 
develop a technical tool for enabling consensus building among basin key actors on priority 
issues and programs to create a more sustainable development model for the basin, (ii) 
finance workshops with key stakeholders on priority issues and programs related to 
sustainable development in the M-R River Basin, and (iii) develop specific studies proposed 
as a result of the agreements reached. Studies carried out on the basis of discussions with 
stakeholders will also identify social impacts of improved territorial planning and suggest 
strategies to mitigate any negative effects. 
43. Develop a GIs to support planning and decision making in the M-R River Basin. 
This activity seeks to support the development and continuous update of a geographic 
information system (GIS) that will incorporate baseline data and information relating to the 
main variables driving territorial development, especially those related to the industries 
located in the basin. The following thematic areas will be covered and correlated: socio- 
economy; urban; physical-territorial; provision of services; land use; legislation; 
environmental conflicts and sources of organic and toxic contamination; and location of 
industries. Under the industrial contamination thematic area, the GIS will include baseline 
information on about 20 parameters relating to water consumption and pollution loads 
(organic and toxic) discharged by each of the industries. The information in this centralized 
database will be instrumental in evaluating the effluent loads from polluting agents 
discharged to the M-R, along with the capacity of the Rio de la Plata to accept these effluents 
discharges. 
(ii) Institutional Development for Flood Control 
44. This sub-component will provide technical assistance to improve and update 
hydrological studies and management tools to control flooding events. Activities will be 
executed by SAyDS/ACUMAR in close collaboration with the provincial and municipal 
governments and are summarized below: 
45. Design and implement a flood control system. Activities will include (i) improving 
and updating the flood control database with detailed information about the urban areas 
affected by flooding (macro and micro drainage) in order to develop a planning and decision- 
making tool to prioritize the most critical areas. Preliminary criteria for supporting this 
prioritization include the number of people affected, poverty levels, the impact of flooding on 
living conditions, and synergy with other relevant investments in the area; (ii) undertaking a 
detailed technical assessment in the sub-basins where the priority areas are located, including 
topographical studies to support planning of pre-feasibility studies, assessing major existing 
infrastructure to identify expected interference and needed institutional coordination to 
construct a drainage system in the area, and developing pre-feasibility studies outlining 
technical solution alternatives and incorporating alternatives in the existing flood control 
investment program. 
46. Update the Hydrological Master Plan (Plan Hidrico) prepared for the Matanza- 
Riachuelo Basin. This activity will focus on the priority sub-basins and alternative technical 
71 
<<PAGE 81>>
solutions, taking into consideration climate-change induced changes in rainfall patterns and 
levels of La Plata River. It will also include studies to update information on hydrological 
sub-systems and determine flooding risk levels and mapping. 
47. Improve the Hydro-Meteorological Alert System. To strengthen meteorological and 
hydrological forecasting in the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin, this sub-component proposes a 
comprehensive approach to optimize the M-R River Basin’s hydro-meteorological 
infrastructure. Upgrades will include new and updated sensors, integrated processing, 
analysis and data display that supports information dissemination to all users, and 
meteorological forecasts useful to decision makers. The objectives are to unify the efforts of 
local authorities and national- level forecasting centers to obtain real-time emergency 
management information, rainfall flooding forecast and warning information, M-R River 
flow and ~udestada~~ forecasts, precipitation quantity forecasts, hail forecasts, and early 
warnings. Based on information from the detection and forecasting stages, the decision to 
issue warnings should be taken in time to allow a proper response by relevant authorities and 
inhabitants to reduce damage through flood preparedness. 
48. Support municipal capacity strengthening in flood management. This activity will 
include (i) organizing contingency plans to prepare for emergencies, as well as 
implementation modalities and simulation exercises involving provincial and municipal 
agencies and local communities, (ii) developing communication plans to provide timely 
information to communities on forecasted flood events, and (iii) preparing environmental 
programs and management plans and disseminating information to design the next steps of 
flood prevention investments. 
(iii) Investments in Basic Infrastructure to Support Territorial Development 
49. This sub-component will finance pilot interventions to improve infrastructure in low- 
income urban areas appropriate for urban expansion. The proposed intervention includes 
technical assistance for developing specific studies and engineering designs, as well as 
investments in basic infrastructure. The basic infrastructure primarily includes: (i) secondary 
networks for water and sanitation and the connection of those to the main existing water and 
sanitation systems; (ii) micro drainage and the section of macro drainage required to operate 
the micro drainage system; (iii) secondary streets; and (iv) recreational areas. 
SAyDS/ACUMAR will be the executing agency, and tripartite agreements will be signed 
between SAyDS, ACUMAR and the different jurisdictions which have primary responsibility 
to carry out these investments. 
50. The approach proposed for the targeted investments will focus on developing 
intervention models for properly providing basic infrastructure and inducing urban expansion 
in low population density areas of the basin. The current situation shows a very low 
population density in the upper section of the basin, and increased expansion (increase in 
population density), already occurring in the middle section of the basin. The lower section of 
the basin already features very high population density. Consistent with this situation and 
with the characteristics of the urban expansion in the middle basin, two intervention models 
are envisaged: 
(i) “Consolidating” urban areas: provision of urban improvement and provision of basic 
infrastructure services in dense and low-income settlements in order to improve the 
43 Sudestadus (literally, “Southeast hits”) are exceptionally high tides in La Plata River from strong 
southeasterly winds. 
72 
<<PAGE 82>>
quality of life in areas that are appropriately located in urban environmental terms. 
Such settlements are common in the lower portion of the middle basin; 
(ii) Promoting urban expansion: provision of urban improvements and basic infrastructure 
services in not-yet-dense low-income settlements in order to improve the quality of life 
of residents, and steer urban expansion in appropriate directions within the basin. Such 
not-yet-dense low-income settlements are common in the peri-urban upper portion of 
the middle basin. 
5 1. By consolidating the targeted areas, this subcomponent aims at promoting 
interventions that will substantially improve living conditions of low-income population 
segments, and simultaneously induce a more intense use of appropriate urban areas through 
actions that integrate urban and environmental aspects. Currently, the offer of appropriate and 
affordable urban sites in the basin is scarce, and the number of unregulated subdivisions of 
land often located in sensitive environmental areas is increasing, thus driving urban 
expansion toward the upper basin, where rural uses were once dominant. This dynamic 
creates enormous pressure for expanding high-cost public infrastructure to distant, sparsely 
occupied areas. It also creates socially and economically segregated communities 
characterized by extremely low living standards. 
52. Nonetheless, as the urban area expands, already subdivided land in less recent 
settlements remains vacant, contributing to the rapid socio-economic and environmental 
degradation that is clearly visible in these areas. In addition, large polygons of land, adjacent 
to these urban areas, are not incorporated in the informal or formal land market. In 
consequence, isolated and sparsely populated urban areas emerge following the urban sprawl. 
53. 
will comply with the following criteria: 
a) Selection is made in agreement with the provincial and municipal governments involved; 
b) More than 50 percent of population lives below the poverty level; 
c) Proposed basic infrastructure and urban improvements are consistent with a local 
integrated urban development plan prepared in consensus with local and regional 
government authorities; 
d) Located relatively near to existing or planned main infrastructure (water, sanitation, 
drainage, roads) to permit connections in the area; 
e) Integration with other programs such as: Plan Federal de Vivienda; PROMEBA; 
progressive social housing programs supported by non-governmental organizations; 
investments in basic services executed by ACUMAR with IADB resources. 
Two (or perhaps three) pilot areas are envisioned for subcomponent investments and 
(iv) Investment in Infrastructure for Flood Control 
54. This sub-component will finance microdrainage systems and the macrodrainage 
works needed to make the selected microdrainage system operational. The areas to receive 
the investments in drainage systems will be selected through a comprehensive socio- 
economic cost-benefit analysis in consultation with provincial and municipal governments. 
Typical areas in need of investments to reduce flooding have low-income settlements located 
in low-lying areas with insufficient municipal drainage services. Only those drainage 
solutions unlikely to be impacted by later updates to the Drainage Master Plan (Plan Hidrico) 
will be eligible. It is anticipated that two low-income settlements encompassing 
approximately 20,000 households will benefit from this component. Once the construction is 
concluded, these systems will be operated by the respective municipal governments.

Component 4. Institutional Strengthening and Project Management (US$18.2 million 
total, US$18.2 million loan) 
56. In line with the development objectives, a separate TA component has been prepared 
to support the design and implementation of measures required in both the short and long 
term to establish the necessary institutional capacity for the new basin authority. This 
capacity would allow ACUMAR to (i) implement the proposed infrastructure works, (ii) 
implement the pollution control and enforcement program, and (iii) support the strategic 
planning capacities of the agencies involved. The component has been divided into five sub- 
components, described below. 
(i) Institutional Strengthening of ACUMAR 
57. ACUMAR has an institutional structure defined under Resolution 5/2007. Using a 
participatory process involving the main stakeholders, this structure will need to be adjusted 
to respond to its institutional needs. The methodology created by Alain Tobolem (Analysis 
System and Institutional Capacity Development) has been proposed. Consideration has also 
been given to using the same institutional strengthening strategy used in the Bank financed 
GEF project Coastal Contamination Prevention and Marine Management that was 
implemented by SAyDS between 2003 and 2007. ACUMAR’s institutional development 
needs will be incorporated into a plan that will be developed over the course of six months, 
where the objectives and activities will be identified to overcome problems and that will 
allow for the assessment and monitoring of progress. 
58. This subcomponent will support three workshops with key decision makers, staff and 
representatives from other institutions within ACUMAR. Representatives from civil society 
that have experience using the Tobolem methodology will also be invited to the workshops. 
A series of interviews with institutional and other civil society stakeholders is envisioned. 
59. As a result of the participatory process, an institutional strengthening plan for 
ACUMAR will be developed and will include key activities, the required institutional 
structure, and defined needs in terms of skilled personnel, office equipment, furniture, human 
resources and other necessary material. ACUMAR is currently supported by SAyDS staff, 
and will be able to able to count on technical support from universities and research institutes 
74 
<<PAGE 84>>
during the institutional strengthening process. This subcomponent will also support the 
necessary investments to provide ACUMAR with proper office space. 
(ii) Communication Strategy 
60. This sub-component will finance actions for the creation of a communication unit and 
implementation of a communications strategy during project implementation. The 
communication strategy is designed to inform key actors and stakeholder groups in the basin 
in a transparent manner about the project; strengthen the credibility and support of the 
project; and contribute to its overall consultation, participation and empowerment Eramework. 
(For further details, see Annex 11D). 
(iii) Establishment of a public information office in ACUMAR 
61, This subcomponent will support the necessary investments such as human resources, 
office equipment, infrastructure and logistic tools, for the proper functioning of a public 
information office within ACUMAR. 
(iv) Technical and monitoring studies 
62. This sub-component includes financing for additional studies required during the 
course of project implementation, and for providing expert support (including a panel of 
recognized independent experts in the fields of environment, engineering, biodiversity, 
modeling etc.) to provide periodic advice in conjunction with project implementation. Studies 
will include additional data collection and analytical work relating to impact and water 
quality monitoring in the M-R River and La Plata River, as well as the control of high 
groundwater tables in the basin area. This subcomponent may also finance other studies 
required for the clean-up of the M-R River Basin as agreed with the Bank. 
(v) Project management 
63. Management of the loan requires human resources, physical space and equipment 
within SAyDS’ UCOFI and proposed UEP units to strengthen purchasing, contracting and 
procurement processes as well as technical project management. The Bank’s procurement 
capacity assessment of UCOFI identified the following needs: (i) engaging a specialist in 
procurement as well as an archives assistant; (ii) acquiring additional equipment (furniture, 
computing equipment, and space for archives); and (iii) improving and adapting physical 
spaces. Other needs will be determined based on the actual work load once the program 
begins. This sub-component will finance (i) consultants to support SAyDSKJCOFI for 
fiduciary activities and technical expertise and SAyDS/UEP for technical project 
management, (ii) UCOFI’s and UEP’s incremental operational expenses; and (iii) office 
equipment/minor remodeling of physical space for UCOFI and UEP as required.

Annex 6: Implementation Arrangements 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project 
Introduction 
1. The National Government will be the Borrower for the proposed loan. SAyDS will be 
responsible for overall project coordination and will directly implement macro-component 2 
together with ACUMAR (as the inter-jurisdictional entity). While ACUMAR has been 
created as an inter-jurisdictional entity with overall responsibility for implementing the PISA, 
the Government recognizes that ACUMAR currently has limited implementation capacity 
which will be reinforced and developed over the course of APL-1. Regarding macro- 
component 1, AySA will be the executing entity while ACUMAR, will ensure the 
consistency of project activities with the PISA in line with its legal mandate. 
2. SAyDS has informed the Bank that it is moving to a new management model for 
internationally financed projects under which procurement, financial management and 
general administrative functions are centralized in a single (existing) unit (UCOFI) while the 
technical leadership for each operation is vested in separate project implementation units 
(UEP). In line with this strategy, SAyDS will exercise its overall project coordination role for 
the present project through two units: UCOFI (Unidad de Coordinacibn para la Gestidn 
Te‘cnica y Financiera Internacional) and UEP (Unidad de Ejecucibn del Proyecto). The 
establishment of UEP will be a condition for loan effectiveness. UCOFI will manage the 
project’s US Dollar denominated designated account as well as a Peso-denominated project 
account from which it will make all payments for goods, works, and services procured under 
components 2,3 and 4. UCOFI will also carry out all procurement for these components with 
technical support from UEP and ACUMAR, which will help produce terms of reference and 
technical specifications, participate in bid/proposal evaluation and support contract 
supervision. Even though UCOFI has experience implementing projects financed by 
international financial institutions, funds have been set aside under component 4 (sub- 
component 5) to strengthen it and UEP further to cope with the demands of the proposed 
project. Strengthening would include contracting of additional staff (particularly a 
procurement specialist and assistant for UCOFI and technical staff as needed for UEP), the 
purchase of additional equipment and furniture and upgrades to UCOFI’s and UEP’s physical 
space. 
3. For component 1, UCOFI will transfer funds to AySA pursuant to the terms of a 
subsidiary agreement between the National Government and AySA on terms acceptable to 
the Bank. The signing of this subsidiary agreement will be a condition of effectiveness of the 
loan. AySA will contract all goods, works and consulting services and make all payments to 
consultants and contractors using its regular technical units. These units include AySA’s 
procurement and legal departments (for the preparation of bidding documents) and AySA’s 
planning department for technical aspects. The environmental, social relations and 
communications departments of AySA will also participate in project implementation. While 
AySA has experience with large works, the concurrent execution of a recently approved 
IADB loan, works financed by other development banks (e.g. BNDES) and the works 
foreseen under component 1 is expected to challenge its implementation capacity. For this 
reason, the Left Bank CollectorBajo Costanero Bypass, Riachuelo preliminary treatment 
plant and Riachuelo Sub-Aquatic Outfall (to be tendered as a single bidding process) will be 
supervised by one or more engineering consulting firms specialized in tunneling, large civil 
works and marine infrastructure. Subsequent works (including the Right Bank Collector (or 
79 
<<PAGE 89>>
alternative) and the Industrial Collector) would also be supervised by independent 
engineering consulting firms hired by AySA. ACUMAR will certify that the package of 
works proposed under component 1 as well as key environmental design parameters are 
consistent with the PISA. 
4. Official communications with the Bank on disbursement issues will be channeled 
through the SAyDSAJCOFI. On procurement matters, AySA will communicate directly with 
the Bank for component 1 while UCOFI handles communications for components 2, 3 and 4. 
5. UCOFI will submit withdrawal applications to the Bank and handle all disbursement 
issues. The Loan proceeds will be transferred from the Bank to a Designated Account (DA) 
in dollars managed by UCOFI in a commercial bank acceptable to the Bank. For component 
1, UCOFI will transfer an initial deposit of an amount to be agreed with the Bank to a 
“project account” in pesos opened by AySA in a bank acceptable to the Bank, from which 
AySA will make payments for eligible expenditures. In order to monitor the uses of Loan 
funds, this bank account should not receive funds from other sources. In addition, transfers 
from UCOFI will be made solely on the basis of eligible expenditures incurred or advances 
supported by expected expenditure reports prepared by AySA. For payments under 
components 2, 3 and 4, UCOFI will open its own project account in pesos. 
6. 
arrangements is under preparation and is a condition of effectiveness. 
An Operational Manual with a detailed description of the agreed implementation 
A. Implementation Arrangements for Component 1 (Sanitation) 
7. The sanitation component that will be implemented by AySA pursuant to the terms of 
the subsidiary agreement mentioned in paragraph 3 above consists of large infrastructure 
works whose implementation requires expertise, knowledge, and capacity. Various organic 
units of AySA need to participate in the implementation of this component, and the entire 
AySA institutional structure needs to support it. Therefore, AySA will constitute a 
coordination unit rather than a project implementation unit which will coordinate the 
implementation of the sanitation component and involve the organic units of the company in 
implementation processes as required. 
8. The coordination unit will consist of a project coordinator, a procurement specialist, a 
financial management specialist, and a technical expert, as well as support staff. The 
procurement and legal departments of AySA will be involved in preparing the bidding 
documents, and the planning department of AySA will be in charge of technical aspects. 
When legal support is required, it will be provided by the legal department of AySA. The 
environmental, social relations, and communications departments of AySA will also 
participate in project outreach program. 
9. AySA will be in charge of the conceptual and detailed designs required under the 
sanitation component. AySA will (i) prepare the bidding documents related to implementing 
the sanitation component, (ii) conduct bidding processes and propose contract awards; (iii) 
sign contracts with construction contractors and supervision firms and assume the role of 
“employer” during the supervision process; and (iv) make all payments due under these 
contracts. 
80 
<<PAGE 90>>
B. Implementation Arrangement for Components 2 (Industrial Pollution Abatement), 3 
(Environmental Territorial Management) and 4 (Institutional Strengthening and 
Project Management) 
10. The SAyDS and ACUMAR (as the interjurisdictional entity) will be the implementing 
agencies for components 2, 3 and 4. SAyDS, through UCOFI, will prepare bidding 
documents and requests for proposals with technical input fiom UEP and ACUMAR. Bids 
and proposals will be jointly evaluated by the SAyDS and ACUMAR but SAyDS will sign 
all contracts for components 2, 3 and 4 and make all payments. There will be no transfer of 
loan proceeds to ACUMAR. Technical studies, engineering designs and supervision of works 
will be contracted out to “technical support agencies” including universities, technical 
institutes and -where warranted -to individual consultants or firms.

Annex 7: Financial Management and Disbursement Arrangements 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project 
A. Executive Summary and Conclusion 
1. A Financial Management (FM) Assessment of the arrangements for the pro osed 
project was carried out in accordance with OPBP 10.02 and Bank specific guidelines! It is 
considered that the financial management arrangements in place meet minimum Bank 
requirements. The Coordination Unit for International Technical and Financial Management 
(Unidad de Coordinacibn para la Gestidn Te‘cnica y Financiera Internacional, or UCOFI), 
the unit in the Secretariat of Environment and Sustainable Development ( Secretaria de 
Ambiente y Desarrollo Sustentable, or SAyDS) will be responsible for undertaking the 
financial management functions of the project comprising budgeting, accounting and 
financial reporting, internal control, disbursements and documenting expenditures to the 
Bank and external auditing arrangements. UCOFI has qualified staff experienced in World 
Bank-financed activities. However, there is a moderate element of risk because of the large 
amount of funding to be managed by Argentina Water and Sanitation Utility (Agua y 
Saneamientos Argentinos S.A., or AySA) under Component 1 of the project. 
B. Country Issues 
2. A Country Financial Accountability Assessment (CFAA)45 for Argentina was 
prepared jointly by the Bank and the IADB in 2006/2007, and the final report delivered to the 
Government of Argentina (GOA) in March 2008. The main findings indicate that Argentina’s 
national Public Financial Management (PFM) has well-designed and legally supported 
systems to control funds received by the GOA from all sources and to provide sufficient 
information to determine whether funds are used as planned. However, the CFAA concludes 
that the country’s PFM performance is below expectations. The Country Assistance 
Strategy46 (CAS) states that the FM portfolio risk at the federal level is Moderate. 
3. Fiduciary Action Plan. The Argentina CAS includes a Fiduciary Action Plan (FAP) 
to help strengthen the operating environment for Bank projects in Argentina. The FAP 
basically consists of three components: raising public awareness, bolstering Bank fiduciary 
monitoring, and increasing transparency and competition practices in public procurement. 
Regarding FM, the Plan aims to (i) improve the timeliness of external audit compliance for 
Bank-financed operations, (ii) increase the strategic focus and coverage of supervision tools 
to assess fiduciary risk in operations, and (iii) support streamlining and harmonization of 
fiduciary processes and reliance on country systems when these meet adequate fiduciary 
standards. 
4. 
measures are part of the project’s FM arrangements and contribute to the FAP objectives:

C. Risk Assessment and Mitigation 
5. The risk assessment process aims to identify FM risks in order to take appropriate 
measures to mitigate identified project risks. This enables the Bank make decisions on the 
appropriate level of supervision and allocation of FM resources in a manner consistent with 
assessed risks. Although projects of this type entail certain risks because of the large amount 
of money involved, every effort has been made to minimize risk stemming from FM. The 
simplest flow of finds has been agreed to with SAyDS and AYSA to avoid any additional 
complexity and reduce associated risk. FM risk is considered Moderate. Table 7.1. shows the 
risk assessment for the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project and the 
measures proposed to mitigate those risks.

D. Strengths and Weaknesses 
6. Strengths. The major strength for risk mitigation is the fact that SAyDSKJCOFI has 
skilled staff capable of fulfilling project needs. Previous experience in the implementation of 
Bank-financed projects is also an asset. 
7. 
Component 1 may pose some additional fiduciary risk. 
Weaknesses. The large amounts of money that would flow through AySA under 
E. Implementing Entities 
8. The sanitation component (Component 1) will be implemented by AySA making use 
of its own organizational structure and line departments including the relevant accounting, 
budget, and finance units of the company. A small coordination unit will be created within 
AySA to facilitate coordination of activities among the different departments and with the 
SAyDS. The coordination unit will consist of a Project Coordinator, a procurement specialist, 
a financial management specialist, and a technical expert, as well as support staff. 
9. The Coordination Unit for International Technical and Financial Management 
(Unidad de Coordinacidn para la Gestidn Te'cnica y Financiera Internacional, or UCOFI) in 
the Secretariat of Environment and Sustainable Development (Secretaria de Ambiente y 
Desarrollo Sustentable, or SAyDS) will undertake the FM functions of the project as a 
whole. UCOFI has qualified FM staff experienced in Bank-financed activities that is capable 
of undertaking the FM functions of the Project. AySA will receive funds to make payments 
for eligible expenditures under the Sanitation component, but UCOFI will have overall 
responsibility for the FM fwnctions of the entire project, including accounting, reporting (e.g., 
preparing interim unaudited financial reports, or IFR), internal controls, and management of 
the Designated Account (DA) and external audit process. In time, UCOFI may need 
additional FM staff to handle the additional workload. It has been proposed that a 
professional accountant with experience in WB-funded projects who is currently working on 
the IDB project also managed by UCOFI will be reassigned to the project once the Loan is 
effective. 
F. Budgeting and Accounting 
10. Budget execution in Argentina is recorded in the Federal Government's integrated 
budget and accounting system (Sistema Integrado de Informacidn Financiera, or SIDIF) and 
subject to control over the budgetary execution process. SAyDS will be required to create and 
maintain a specific budget line entry for the project in its annual budget to allocate budgetary 
resources and track project execution, specifying the sources of funds. The UEPEX system 
will be used to maintain project accounting records. This in-house information tool was 
developed by the Federal Government. Its use is mandatory for multilateral-financed 
operations at the federal level, and it is considered adequate for accounting purposes. The 
project chart of accounts will reflect disbursement categories, project components, and 
sources of financing. Cash basis accounting will be used to record the project transactions (to 
be confirmed). 
11, AySA will also reflect the transactions for Component 1 in its own accounting books 
and annual financial statements. AySA will be required to submit to the Bank its annual 
audited financial statements as a revenue-generating entity. 
85 
<<PAGE 95>>
G. Internal Control and Internal Auditing 
12. UCOFI within SAyDS and the Office of the Cabinet of Ministers (Jefatura Gabinete 
de Ministros, or JGM) are subject to internal audit by the General Syndicate of the Nation 
(Sindicatura General de la Nacidn, or SIGEN), which is the Federal Government’s internal 
audit agency under the jurisdiction of the executive branch. SIGEN supervises and 
coordinates the actions of Internal Audit Units (IAUs) in all federal agencies, approves their 
audit plans, conducts research and independent audits, and systematizes the information from 
its own reports and those produced by the IAUs. If necessary, the internal audit reports on 
project performance will be reviewed during project supervision. 
13. AySA, as a revenue-generating company, has an Internal Audit Unit, the Direccidn de 
Auditoria Interna (DAI), that is also under the oversight of SIGEN. The DAI is headed by a 
professional accountant with some auditing background and broad experience in the public 
sector. DAI staff also comprises two professional auditors: a supervisor and a senior auditor 
for administrative and commercial operations. In addition, DAI has technical and information 
technology staff. DAI prepares annual audit plans following a risk-based approach. Annual 
audit plans are submitted to SIGEN for review and approval. The internal audit function is 
supplemented by an Audit Committee composed of three members of the board of directors 
of AySA.

evidence for the related works, goods, and services procured in line with Bank guidelines and 
procurement thresholds for the project.

16. Flow of Funds for Components 2: 3 and 4. To facilitate project implementation, the 
Coordination Unit for International Technical and Financial Management (Unidad de 
Coordinacidn para la Gestidn Te‘cnica y Financiera Internacional, or UCOFI will operate a 
segregated DA in US. dollars to be opened at the National Bank of Argentina (Banco de la 
Nacidn Argentina, or BNA). Funds deposited into the DA as advances will follow the Bank’s 
disbursement operating policies and procedures described in the Disbursement Letter. 
Withdrawals from the DA will be made solely for payments of eligible expenditures as 
incurred. The proposed ceiling for advances to the DA would be $40 million until 
disbursements reach a total of $100 million, and $90 million thereafter, estimated to be 
sufficient for peak disbursement periods of project execution. UCOFI will also control an 
operational bank account in local currency (pesos) from which it will make payments for 
eligible expenses incurred under components 2; 3 and 4 and from which it will transfer 
advances in pesos to AYSA for payments under Component 1. 
17. Flow of Funds for Component 1. UCOFI will submit withdrawal applications to the 
Bank and handle all disbursement issues. The Loan proceeds will be transferred from the 
Bank to the Designated Account (DA) in dollars managed by UCOFI. UCOFI will transfer an 
initial deposit of an amount to be agreed with the Bank to a “special account” in pesos 
opened by AySA in a bank acceptable to the Bank, from which AySA will make payments 
for eligible’expenditures under Component 1. In order to monitor the uses of Loan funds, this 
bank account should not receive funds from other sources. In addition, transfers from UCOFI

22. Retroactive Financing: The Bank will reimburse the expenditures for payments made 
up to one year prior to the expected date of the Loan Agreement. These expenditures will not 
exceed US$6,000,000 equivalent. 
23. The new Policy Framework on the Eligibility of Expenditures in World Bank Lending 
applies to this project, since the country financing parameters for Argentina were approved 
by the Regional Vice President on May 1 1, 2005. 
24. The project will request access to the Bank’s Client Connection web page to 
download the Withdrawal Application Form and to periodically reconcile its bank account 
and resources received from the different sources. 
I. Financial Reporting 
25. UCOFI will be in charge of preparing the annual and interim financial statements for 
the project as a whole in line with Bank requirements. For that purpose, AySA will produce 
financial information for Component 1 and send it to UCOFI, which will compile all the 
information in one financial report. 
26. The UEPEX system will be used to produce the requisite financial statements 
following public sector accounting standards in Argentina. The public sector accounting rules 
are comprehensive and consistent with public international standards, in particular IPSAS # 
1. Said standards are set by the General Accounting Office (Contaduriu General de la 
Nacidn, or CGN).

Annex 8: Procurement Arrangements 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project 
1. Procurement for the proposed project will be carried out in accordance with the 
World Bank’s Procurement Guidelines (“Guidelines: Procurement under IBRD Loans and 
IDA Credits,” May 2004, revised October 2006), Consultant Guidelines (“Guidelines: 
Selection and Employment of Consultants by World Bank Borrowers,” May 2004, revised 
October 2006), and the provisions stipulated in the Loan Agreement. The items under 
different expenditure categories are described in general below. For each contract to be 
financed by the Loan, the Borrower and the Bank agree on the different procurement methods 
or consultant selection methods, the need for pre-qualification, estimated costs, prior review 
requirements, and time frame in the procurement plan. The procurement plan will be updated 
at least every 3 months or as required to reflect actual project implementation needs and 
improvements in institutional capacity. 
2. Procurement of works. Works procured under this project will include the 
construction of large sanitation works in the Riachuelo wastewater system, which comprises 
wastewater and industrial collectors, a sewage pretreatment plant, pumping stations, and sub- 
aquatic outfall. These works will be constructed to collect domestic and industrial 
wastewater. Contracts for sanitation works will be procured by Argentina Water and 
Sanitation (Agua y Saneamientos Argentinos S.A., or AySA) through the International 
Competitive Bidding (ICB) method, preceded by pre-qualification using the Bank’s Standard 
Pre-qualification Document and further carried out using the Spanish version of the Bank’s 
Standard Bidding Document (SBD) for the Procurement of Works, with the necessary 
amendments to make it fully consistent with the 2004 Bank Procurement Guidelines (revised 
October 2006). As discussed with AySA, sanitation works would be grouped in two large 
packages for bidding: one comprising three lots (Lot 1: Left Bank collector and Costanera 
bypass collector, Lot 2: Riachuelo wastewater pretreatment plant and pumping stations, and 
Lot 3: Riachuelo outfall), and the other comprising two lots (Lot 1 : Right Bank collector, and 
Lot 2: Industrial collector). AySA is already preparing the Pre-qualification Documents for 
the first package. Designs are also being prepared by AySA and it is expected that they would 
be finished by May, 2009 for the first package, and by December, 2009 for the second. 
Works under Components 3 (infrastructure for territorial development and for flood control) 
will be bid through the Secretariat of Environment and Sustainable Development (SAyDS) 
following the International Competitive Bidding (ICB) method and using the Smaller 
Contracts SBD with post-qualification. National SBDs agreed with or satisfactory to the 
Bank will be used for small works costing less than US$5 million. If needed, shopping 
procedures will be followed for works costing less than US$ 350,000. Possible rehabilitation 
of ACUMAR’s headquarters could eventually be financed under Component 4 using NCB or 
Shopping methods. 
3. Procurement of goods. Goods procured under this project will include industrial 
equipment for cleaner production, environmental control and monitoring equipment, 
laboratory equipment, office furniture, computer hardware and software, office equipment 
and supplies, to be procured through ICB using the Bank’s SBD. Additionally, contracts 
below US$500,000 will be procured using National SBDs agreed with or satisfactory to the 
Bank. Shopping procedures might be followed for contracts worth less than US$lOO,OOO. 
4. Procurement of non-consulting services. All contracts for services not related to 
consultant services (hydrometeorological control system, geographic information system, 
92 
<<PAGE 102>>
water quality control system, logistics, organization of seminars and workshops, office rental, 
laboratory analyses, and printing services) may be procured under the same methodologies 
and thresholds specified for goods. The specific bidding documents will be prepared on the 
basis of the World Bank’s sample bidding document for the procurement of non-consultant 
services. 
5. Selection of consultants. Consulting firms will be contracted in the following areas 
of expertise: design and supervision of works, environmental impact studies, information 
management, communication strategies, personnel search, public consultation, and training 
services. All contracts will be procured using the Bank’s guidelines for hiring of consultants. 
For firms, Quality- and Cost-Based Selection (QCBS) will be the preferred method, but Least 
Cost Selection (LCS), Quality-Based Selection (QBS) and consultant qualifications (CQS) 
may be used as well. CQS and LCS will only be acceptable for assignments estimated to cost 
less than US$lOO,OOO. Shortlists of consultants for services estimated to cost less than 
US$500,000 equivalent per contract may be composed entirely of national consultants, in 
accordance with the provisions of paragraph 2.7 of the Consultant Guidelines. Individual 
consultants will be hired to complete the staffing of the implementing agencies to provide 
specialized advisory services. These individual consultants will be selected by comparing the 
qualifications of three candidates and hired in accordance with the provisions of paragraphs 
5.1 through 5.3 of the Consultant Guidelines. The consultants should be selected and 
consultant awards authorized on the basis of the expected overall duration and cost of the 
assignment, but contracts may be signed for shorter periods if the Borrower prefers. 
6. The loan would finance operating costs, which would be 
procured using the implementing agencies’ administrative procedures that were reviewed and 
found acceptable to the Bank. Such procedures will be described in the Operational Manual. 
Operating costs. 
7. Training Expenses. The loan will finance training expenses for strengthening the 
capacity of ACUMAR staff and for designing ACUMAR’s communication strategy (such as 
expenditures for rental fees of training facilities, books, workshop and seminar fees, and 
domestic and international travel and per diems). These expenses will be procured using the 
implementing agencies’ administrative procedures that were reviewed and found acceptable 
to the Bank. 
8. Grants for subprojects. Subprojects aimed at private beneficiaries would be 
financed through matching grants under Component 2 of the project. The matching grants 
program will help small and medium enterprises improve their environmental compliance, 
and meet requirements under their Plans for Industrial Restructuring (PRIs). Procurement of 
works, goods and services would be carried out in accordance with the procedures set forth in 
this annex. The Borrower will decide which enterprises to finance based on specific 
eligibility criteria agreed with the Bank, which include (i) categorization as an SME; (ii) 
having a signed PRI; and (iii) lack of access to commercial credit. Matching grants will be 
allocated to eligible enterprises, prioritizing on the basis of the relative pollution load 
contributed by the enterprise. Detailed eligibility criteria for assistance to SMEs, as well as 
the specific disbursement modalities will be clearly defined and disclosed in the project’s 
Operational Manual to ensure transparency of resource allocation. The grant application will 
contain a business plan for cleaner production investments including a list of the works, 
goods and services to be procured and their estimated cost. The hiring of consultant firms and 
specialized individual consultants would also be listed in the grant application and follow the 
procedures for hiring firms and individuals established in this annex. 
93 
<<PAGE 103>>
B. Assessment of the agency’s capacity to implement procurement 
9. Because of the complex nature of the sanitation works, specific procurement 
responsibilities for the implementation of Component 1 (Sanitation), including official 
communication with the Bank, will be undertaken by AySA, the water and sanitation 
concessionaire in the City of Buenos Aires and 17 nearby urban districts, which has acquired 
the required implementation capacity in tendering, contracting, managing, and administering 
large civil works contracts as part of its responsibilities in providing water supply and 
sanitation services. 
10. The procurement responsibilities for the Components 2 (Industrial Pollution 
Abatement), 3 (Environmental Territorial Management), and 4 (Institutional Strengthening 
and Project Management) will be undertaken by the Coordination Unit for International 
Technical and Financial Management (Unidad de Coordinacidn para la Gestidn Te‘cnica y 
Financiera Internacional, or UCOFI), a unit in the SAyDS with knowledge of and 
experience in managing projects financed by international financing institutions. UCOFI will 
handle the official communication with the Bank regarding procurement under these 
components, and will carry out procurement activities in close coordination with the UEP and 
ACUMAR. 
11. An assessment of the capacity of the Implementing Agencies to implement 
procurement actions for the project was carried out by the Bank’s Procurement Specialist 
assigned to the project in August, 2008, and updated in January 2009. The assessment 
reviewed the organizational structure for implementing the project. Both UCOFI and AySA 
have project implementation experience in procurement under international financial 
institution procedures. Loan proceeds may support AySA’s plan to hire an international 
procurement specialist, since its local procurement staff needs to be strengthened. This 
international procurement specialist experienced in large works contracts under Bank 
procedures was already hired by AySA with its own resources. 
12. UCOFI was created in September 2007 to formulate, supervise, and coordinate 
environmental projects with international financing and provide management control, 
procurement, and financial management services to SAyDS. UCOFI has project 
implementation experience from successive World Bank and Inter American Development 
Bank operations, which include coastal cleaning (US$ 8 million), sanitation and sewerage 
works (US$ 100 million), and industrial restructuring (US$ 20 million). UCOFI is staffed by 
experienced managers (General, and Financial Coordinators) and a team of less than 20 
people, which needs to be strengthened to assume the project’s procurement responsibilities. 
Loan proceeds will also support UCOFI’s plan to hire a procurement specialist with 
knowledge of Bank procedures who will specifically be assigned to the Project. Audits are 
carried out by the Office of the National Auditor General (Auditoria General de la Nacidn, or 
AGN). 
13. Infrastructure planning for water and sanitation works in the concession area is 
periodically prepared on a 5-year basis by AySA through its Planning Office, and presented 
for approval to the Planning Agency (Agencia de Planzjicacidn, or APLA of the Ministry of 
Infrastructure. Once planning is approved by APLA, procurement for water and sanitation 
works is implemented by AySA through its Works Office, and procurement for goods and 
services is implemented by the Logistics Office. Projects and designs for maintenance of 
existing infrastructure and expansion of the system are also prepared by the Planning Office, 
which has 100 staff (80 engineers and 20 technicians) organized in 4 management areas for 
94 
<<PAGE 104>>
preparing the designs on the basis of a project approach. The Planning Office has prepared 
approximately 300 projects since 2006, including large detailed projects such as the Paranit 
de las Palmas tunnel works. The Bank was informed by the Planning Office during the 
capacity assessment that designs for the main works to be carried out through the project are 
being prepared and will be finished at the minimum required level for bidding the works 
under ad-measurement type contracts. Construction drawings (detailed engineering) will be 
prepared by works contractors. 
14. The Works Office of AySA is staffed by approximately 140 employees in the areas 
of drilling, contracts and bidding, works supervision (90 staff), and management control. This 
office receives the project documents, cost estimates, and finalized bidding documents from 
the Planning Office and handles the bidding process, signs contracts, and supervises the 
execution of works until they are completed and ready to be delivered to the operational area 
of the company. A commission designated by the Works Office Director prepares the bid 
evaluation reports, which are further reviewed by the legal department of the company. The 
Works Office supervises several large works, including the southwestern plant expansion, the 
Parana de las Palmas tunnel works, and Berazategui sewage preliminary treatment plant. 
15. The Logistics Office of AySA carries out approximately 1,200 contracts a year for 
goods and services (e.g., materials, general and production consumables, and networks 
maintenance) and is staffed by approximately 25 employees, 50 percent of whom are 
professionals. The office handles the procurement processes and the supervision of the 
contract execution. 
16. AySA has an internal audit unit and is also audited by AGN and external auditors 
selected by the General National Audit, (Sindicatura General de la Nacidn, or SIGEN). The 
internal audit unit, staffed by a principal auditor, two professional assistants, and three 
engineers, carries out biannual audits of the Logistics Office and the Works Office on a 
sample basis. The company carries out its procurement on the basis of a procedures manual 
called Purchase and Contracting Regulation (Reglamento de Compras y Contrataciones) that 
was approved by SIGEN. Bidding processes for contracts that exceed 10 million Argentine 
Pesos are reviewed by a control commission including two representatives of SIGEN and one 
of AySA’s union workers. APLA also audits AySA from a technical point of view.

project: 
Legal aspects 
National Public Procurement in Argentina is governed by separate and incomplete 
statutes. Supreme Decree No. 1023/200 1 and its subsequent modifications enacted 
through Supreme Decrees Nos. 666/2003 and 204/2004 establish the principles for 
public procurement and govern the procurement of goods and services. The 
applicability and enforceability of these Supreme Decrees is, however, limited, as 
not all the necessary regulations have been issued. Decree 436/2000, which 
regulated the procurement-related articles of the National Account Law 23354 of 
1956, is still used despite the fact that those articles were repealed by Supreme 
Decree No. 1023/200 1. Public Works Law 13064 of 1947 (Ley de Obras Pziblicas) 
governs the specifics of procurement of civil works. Law 22460 of 1981 for the 
contracting and promotion of consulting services was partially repealed in 1994 and 
is in practice ignored. Decree 1184/2001 governs the hiring of individual 
consultants, whose approval process is regulated by Decree 577/2003. In addition, 
the 1992 Law 24156 on Financial Management and Control Systems (Ley de 
Administracibn Financiera y de 10s Sistemas de Control) contains provisions related 
to procurement, particularly in respect to reference prices. 
National laws only cover procurement by the central administration and 
decentralized agencies of the executive branch. Government-owned enterprises, the 
legislative and judicial branches of government, the provinces, and the City of 
Buenos Aires have their own public procurement rules and regulations, which apply 
to Bank funds that are on-lent by the Federal Government to the provinces and the 
City of Buenos Aires. 
Because national procurement regulations are not totally consistent with Bank 
guidelines, legal documents should include the Special Procurement Provisions 
listed in the section below. Although provincial procurement regulations were not 
assessed in all cases; practice has demonstrated that the proposed provisions are 
enough to mitigate risks of misinterpretation, as provincial regulations are modeled 
along federal rules in most cases. 
Because regulations concerning the hiring of individuals result in lengthy approval 
processes by government authorities and unrealistically low fees for consultants, 
implementing agencies often resort to procurement agents. 
The following Special Procurement Provisions shall apply to procurement under the 
General 
(i) All procurement shall be done using standard bidding documents, standard requests 
for proposals, model bid evaluation forms, model proposal evaluation forms, and 
contract forms previously agreed with the Bank. For consultant services contracts, 
97 
<<PAGE 107>>
only the types of contracts listed in Section IV of the Consultant Guidelines may be 
used. Convenios will not be permitted. 
(ii) Foreign and local contractors, service providers, consultants, and suppliers shall not 
be required to register, establish residence in Argentina, or enter into association 
with other national or international bidders as a condition for submitting bids or 
proposals and/or for contract award. 
(iii) The invitations to bid, bidding documents, minutes of bid opening, requests for 
expressions of interest, and pertinent summaries of the evaluation reports of bids or 
proposals of all processes for the procurement of goods, works, and services 
(including consultant services), as the case may be, shall be published in the web 
page of the National Contracts Office (OJicina Nacional de Contrataciones) in a 
manner acceptable to the Bank. 
(iv) The Borrower shall (i) Provide data to include in the Bank’s publicly accessible 
Procurement Plan Execution System (Sistema de Ejecucidn de Planes de 
Adquisiciones, or SEPA) within 30 days of loan approval with the information 
contained in the initial procurement plan, (ii) update the procurement plan at least 
quarterly or as required to reflect the actual project implementation needs and 
progress and include in the SEPA the information contained in the updated 
procurement plan immediately thereafter., 
(v) Bidders and consultants shall not be allowed to review or make copies of other 
bidders’ bids or consultant proposals, as the case may be. 
(vi) The provisions set forth in paragraphs 2.49, 2.50, 2.52, 2.53, 2.54 and 2.59 of the 
Procurement Guidelines shall also be applicable to contracts for goods, works and 
Non-Consultant Services to be procured under National Competitive Bidding 
procedures. 
(vii) Consultants shall not be required to submit bid or performance securities. 
Goods, non-consultant services, and works 
(i) A two-envelope system of procurement shall not be used for the procurement of 
goods, services (other than consultants services), or works. 
(ii) Contracts of goods, services (other than consulting services) and works shall not be 
awarded to the “most convenient” bid but to the bid determined to be substantially 
responsive and the lowest evaluated bid, provided that the bidder is hrther 
determined to be qualified to perform the contract satisfactorily. 
Works 
(i) Price adjustment for civil works contracts shall follow the price adjustment 
methodology agreed between the Government of Argentina and the Bank. 
(ii) Where large works or complex items of equipment are involved, not less than 8 
weeks shall be allowed for the preparation and submission of prequalification 
applications, and not less than 12 weeks shall be allowed for the preparation and 
submission of bids.

Annex 9: Economic and Financial Analysis 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project 
1. The Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Program (APL-1 + APL-2) 
is economically feasible, with net benefits of US$0.5 billion and an internal rate of return of 
22 percent. From a financial point of view, the program shows a net loss of US$480 million. 
The financial analysis of the program differs from the financial analysis of the entity (see 
Annex 11B) that operates the project, Le. AySA; and hence the conclusions reached for the 
financial viability of the program do not apply to the financial viability of the company. A 
detailed analysis is presented in this annex, complemented by a distributional analysis and 
sensitivity and risk analyses. 
A. Methodology 
2. The objective of this comprehensive urban environment program is the clean-up of 
the Matanza-Riachuelo (M-R) Basin through several components aimed at upgrading social 
conditions and urban infrastructure in the basin. On the basis of this objective, a cost benefit 
analysis was carried out to determine the financial and economic feasibility of two 
components of the program, sanitation (Component 1) and industrial pollution abatement 
(Component 2). The evaluation was carried out to assess the accompanying benefits from (i) 
sewerage expansion, (ii) clean-up of the M-R River, and (iii) clean-up of the M-R River and 
improvement of the water quality of La Plata River. 
3. The analysis was carried out from three perspectives: financial, economic, and 
distributional. From a financial perspective, each activity was appraised by measuring its 
costs and benefits at market prices. From an economic perspective, each activity was 
evaluated by converting financial cash flows into economic cash flows through (i) conversion 
factors applied to the investment and operating costs in order to eliminate market distortions, 
and (ii) measurement of the welfare impact on households within the program’s area of 
influence. The fiscal impact was estimated by measuring the taxes and subsidies that occur in 
the transactions associated with the program and its financing. A distributional analysis was 
conducted based on the results of the economic and financial evaluation. This analysis 
assesses the impact of each activity on specific groups of stakeholders, such as government, 
customers, employees, and suppliers. Finally, the results of the economic and financial 
analyses were tested against real world uncertainties by conducting a sensitivity and risk 
analysis. 
4. The net benefit of each activity equals the difference between the incremental benefits 
and the incremental costs of two scenarios: with and without the program. The “with 
program” scenario considers the proposed investment program implemented. Hence the 
program achieves environmental recovery of the M-R River as well as the associated benefits 
for La Plata River, sewerage expansion, and complementary investments. The “without” 
program scenario assumes that pollution levels and sewerage coverage remain at the current 
level. The activities were appraised by measuring the flow of costs and benefits for the 
lifetime of the program, estimated at 30 years. Costs and benefits were expressed in constant 
102 
<<PAGE 112>>
prices as of October 2007.49 The discount rate corresponded to the opportunity cost of capital 
in Argentina, estimated at 11 percent.5’ 
5. Benefits expected from the program consist of (i) an improvement in the water quality 
of the M-R River, (ii) an improvement in the water quality of La Plata River, and (iii) 
provision of sewerage by 2016 to about 1.2 million households5’ that are currently not 
connected. 
6. The program will have a large impact on the quality of life of households in the basin. 
The benefits from the improvement in water quality include positive health impacts, new 
recreation opportunities, and convenience from a more convenient sanitation solution, and the 
indirect impacts that will result from follow-on development in the basin that will become 
possible with the environmental improvement of the condition of the M-R River. Benefits 
from sewerage expansion will accrue to low-income households currently without sewage 
services that this program aims to cover. Poorer households tend to reside in close proximity 
to the M-R River and suffer more from the poor quality of the river than the better-off 
households that tend to live fbrther away. The welfare effect of the extension of sewerage 
provision will be particularly pro-poor, given the comparatively lower current access to 
sewerage among poor households. 
7. Economic benefits were estimated based on a combination of revealed and stated 
preference approaches. The revealed preference approach, which relies on data from 
observed transactions in the market or on expenditures by households or other agents, was 
used to estimate (i) the benefits from sewerage expansion through the avoided costs and 
hedonic pricing methods and (ii) benefits of water quality improvement in the M-R River 
through the hedonic pricing approach. The stated preference technique, which is based on 
survey methods-in this case, asking households how much they are willing to pay for an 
improvement in water quality-was used to estimate the benefits of the clean-up of the M-R 
River only, as well as the benefits of the clean-up of both the M-R and La Plata Rivers. 
Estimates of the benefits of different components of the program were obtained using 
different valuation methods and aggregated to yield total program benefits without double- 
counting. 
8. Financial benefits were estimated based on current sewerage tariffs charged by the 
water and sewerage utility Argentina Water and Sanitation (Agua y Saneamientos Argentinos, 
SA., or AySA), the number of households connected to the sewerage network, and the 
volume of sewage discharged. Financial benefits were programmed for two scenarios: with 
and without the program. The “with program” scenario assumes that sewerage coverage will 
increase from the current level of 35 percent to 72 percent by 2016 for the 14 municipalities 
that lie (at least partially) in the M-R River Basin, including the upper basin municipalities 
that lie outside AySA’s concession area.52 The “without program” scenario assumes that 
current sewerage levels remain constant. The average bill for sewerage service per household 
every 2 months is approximately ARS 13.
