B. Costs 
9. The flow of costs comprises investment as well as operating costs. Investment costs 
include all the works needed to attain the benefits expected from the program (Le., works to 
be financed by the Bank in APL-1 and APL-2, as well as by AySA), as well as those 
investments in sewerage expansion to be carried out for AySA with financial sources other 
than the World Bank (estimated at US$ 400 million in the second phase). Operating costs 
were estimated using the two scenarios, “without program,” which assumes that current unit 
costs remain constant, and “with program,” which includes all operating costs incurred when 
the program is implemented. The financial flow of costs includes taxes and excludes 
subsidies. Financial costs were transformed into economic costs using conversion factorss3 to 
eliminate market distortions created by taxes, tariffs, and subsidies. Financial costs included a 
100 percent subsidy on investment costs to be fhded by the Federal Government. A value 
added tax (VAT) of 21 percent was added to financial costs. 
10. Investment costs. Total investment costs required for cleaning up the M-R and La 
Plata Rivers is US$2.8 billion, including over US$800 million for sewerage expansion. 
Details of investment costs can be found in Annex 5. 
C. Benefits 
11. The M-R and La Plata Rivers receive large quantities of domestic and industrial 
wastewater from densely populated centers, including Buenos Aires. Of the 60 m3/second of 
domestic wastewater generated by the Buenos Aires area, only about 30m3/second is 
currently conveyed to the Berazategui site, where it is discharged without treatment 2.5 km 
offshore through an ~utfall.’~ Another 3 m3/second is treated at the Sudoeste treatment plant 
and then discharged to the M-R River, while the remaining 27 m3/second is discharged at 
many points at tributaries of different rivers, including the M-R River, as well as at La Plata 
River. 
12. Benefits for La Plata River. La Plata River has a “moderate” pollution level &e., the 
water quality is unsuitable for drinking without prior treatment, and only non-contact 
recreational activities are permissible). Through works proposed in the program (construction 
of a coastal collector along the banks of La Plata River, preliminary treatment at two new 
treatment plants, and discharge of treated wastewater farther away from the river banks), the 
program is expected to improve water quality in La Plata River. According to the results of 
water quality modeling done by The Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Authority (Autoridad de 
Cuenca Matanza-Riachuelo, or ACUMAR), the program will protect water quality at the 
source water intakes and open up a large stretch of La Plata River for non-contact 
recreational activities that are currently not recommended because of water pollution. 
13. Benefits for the M-R River. The M-R River Basin is contaminated by sewage 
disposal from households with no sewerage connection and by discharge of untreated 
wastewater by the AySA sewerage system. Industrial discharge with inadequate or no 
treatment is also a major problem in the basin. The program is expected to bring about a 
significant improvement of water quality of the M-R River. ACUMAR’s water modeling 
shows that the program would result in the elimination of anoxic conditions and recuperation 
53 Conversion factors for the main inputs of the investment and operating costs were estimated according to the 
Phillips, Robert, and Beatriz Villegas. Review of the Capital and Berazategui Wastewater outfalls Buenos 
uidelines of the National Directorate of Public Investment (Direccion Nacional de Inversion Pliblica). 
Aires. January, 2008. 
104 
<<PAGE 114>>
of oxygen in the water body. The river’s bad odor will be eliminated along the entire course 
of the river as it becomes suitable for recreational activities with no direct contact with water. 
14. Benefits from provision of sewerage connections. Currently only 35 percent of 
households in the 14 municipalities of the M-R River Basin have sewerage coverage. The 
remaining 65 percent of unconnected households have their own on-site solutions: 30 percent 
use septic tanks, 17 percent use pits, and 18 percent directly discharge to the streets, nearby 
streams, or drainage systems. The lack of sewerage connections causes serious problems for 
the population and the environment and results in costs associated with maintaining on-site 
systems, flooding, and health problems. Furthermore, on-site sewerage systems discharge 
wastewater into the aquifer, increasing its water level and contaminating it. In some parts of 
the 14 municipalities of the basin, this contributes to high groundwater tables, causing 
damage to people’s homes. Finally, lack of access. to sewerage increases the incidence of 
diarrhea and other water-borne illnesses. 
15. The program aims to connect by 2016 approximately 3.6 million people currently 
without service in the AySA’s concession area. Of these, 2.4 million live in the 14 
municipalities of the M-R River Basin, and the remaining 1.2 million live along the banks of 
La Plata River. Through this program, coverage for the whole concession area is expected to 
increase from the current level of 58 percent to 83 percent by 2016, while coverage will 
increase from 35 percent to 72 percent in the 14 municipalities that lie (at least partially) in 
the M-R River Basin (including those municipalities outside AySA’s concession area). 
16. 
program: 
The following data sources were used to measure the economic benefits of the 
(i) The Real Estate Survey of 1,200 properties for sale (apartments, houses, and empty 
lots) generated data on sale prices and attributes of the properties included in the 
survey. These data have been used to estimate the hedonic price function and 
evaluate the following benefits of the program: elimination of odor of the M-R River 
and provision of sewerage. 
(ii) A Contingent Valuation Survey of 1,001 households residing in the M-R and La 
Plata basins generated data on willingness to pay (WTP) for the elimination of odor 
from the M-R River, recreational possibilities in the M-R and La Plata Rivers, and 
the protection of water quality at source water intakes. This survey separately 
elicited WTP for the environmental benefits for the M-R River alone and in 
combination with environmental improvement in La Plata River. 
(iii) Data on avoided costs of sewerage connection was collected from (i) ACUMAR, 
which provided geo-referenced data for all municipalities in the basin, including the 
number of households, type of sewage disposal system (sewerage network, septic 
tank, pit, or other), altitude, and water table level, (ii) a small sample of households 
that use on-site sewerage solutions to generate data for cost of operating all types of 
sewage disposal systems, (iii) operators of vacuum pumping trucks to get 
information on actual costs paid by households, and (iv) experts on hydrology and 
operators of sewage disposal systems to get accurate information of required 
maintenance for proper functioning of the disposal systems. 
(iv) AySA provided data on avoided costs of moving the Bernal drinking water intake 
hrther out into La Plata River.

(v) Partial data permitting the estimation of the costs of illness for households without 
sewerage connections were collected through the contingent valuation survey (the 
incidence of diarrheal diseases among children under 5 years old from the survey 
sample). Supplementary data were also collected from other sources, such as 
treatment costs for the public healthcare system.

17. Summary of benefits from different approaches. Benefits were measured using the 
hedonic pricing, contingent valuation, avoided costs, and cost of illness approaches (table 
9.2). In estimating the program’s benefits using alternative methods, special care has been 
taken to ensure that there is no double-counting, to ensure robust assessment of the economic 
viability of the program. For example, the benefits from sewerage connections were 
measured separately through two different approaches: hedonic prices and avoided costs, the 
results were used for comparative purposes and they were not added. The same with the 
estimation of the benefits from cleaning up the M-R River, which were measured through 
hedonic prices and willingness to pay; both benefits were analyzed and they were carefully 
used avoiding double counting. The benefits of cleaning up of the River de la Plata were 
measured along with the benefits of cleaning up of the M-R River, given that the benefits 
along the River de la Plata would not occur without the investment for the M-R River. 
Moreover. The estimated benefits for each component were carefully added to calculate the 
total benefits of the project.

18. In addition to avoiding double-counting, the beneficiary population for each type of 
category of benefits has also been carefully chosen. For example this economic analysis 
assumes that the total beneficiary population from sewerage provision consists of both (i) 
households within AySA’s concession area that will obtain sewerage connections under the 
program; as well as (ii) households outside AySA’s concession area that could become 
connected in the future because of the project. Thus, a total population of approximately 1.1 
million households would benefit from sewerage connections. The beneficiary population of 
the clean-up of the M-R River, on the other hand, includes population in GBA (about 10 
million people); and the benefits will correspondingly be the highest for households within 
approximately 20 blocks of the river (about 1 ~‘XO), whose housing prices are notably affected 
by the proximity to the river and its quality.

19. Contingent valuation survey. During preparation a contingent valuation survey was 
carried out to evaluate the benefits expected from cleaning-up the M-R River as well as the 
Rio de la Plata, that is: elimination of bad odor along the entire flow of the M-R River, the 
possibility of using the M-R River for recreation without direct contact with water, the 
possibility of using La Plata River for recreation without direct contact with water (but for a 
wider range of water-related sports, including rowing, than in the case of the M-R River), and 
the protection of the water intakes for the public water supply. Benefits from the provision of 
sewerage to households in the M-R River Basin were not evaluated in this survey or 
mentioned as an outcome of the program. 
20. Results showed that the average WTP is approximately US$2.6 per household per 
month for the M-R River and US$ 3.13 per household per month for both rivers.” The WTP 
for the clean-up of the M-R River is expected to vary depending on the distance to the river, 
as households that live closer to the river are directly affected by the contamination and the 
bad odor of the river. In that sense, the WTP of these households reflects their “use value.” 
Moreover, the M-R River may have a symbolically important value for the population of 
Greater Buenos Aires, as there is great awareness of contamination in the basin as one of the 
most severe environmental contamination problems in the country. In that sense, the clean-up 
of the M-R River may benefit households that do not use the river or frequent the 
surroundings. The WTP of those households reflects their “non-use” value of the river. 
2 1. Hedonic pricing survey. Hedonic models were used in Buenos Aires to estimate the 
affects of access to sewerage on property values. In the hedonic literature, several studies 
have estimated the impact of proximity to a water body (e.g., canal or river) and view on 
housing prices, but very few papers have estimated the effect of water quality on property 
prices (Leggett and Bockstael 2000).56 
22. Results of the 2008 Real Estate Survey” conducted as part of this economic 
evaluation revealed a statistically significant difference between the average prices of houses, 
apartments, and lots from the survey sample with and without a sewerage connection. The 
difference between the average prices of properties affected and not affected by the odor was 
also significant, particularly for odor from any source of contamination (e.g., M-R River, 
factories). Property prices were found to fall from an average of US$662/m2 to US$591/m2 
for houses, from US$889/m2 to US$757/m2 for apartments, and from US415/m2 to 
US$259/m2 for empty lots closer to the river odor. 
23. The magnitude of the benefits from cleaning up the M-R River for specific properties 
varies, As expected, the results show that once the M-R River is cleaned-up, the closer the 
property is to the river, the higher the value of the benefits. The impact on property values 
varies with distance, type of property (house, apartment, or empty lot), and location 
(properties in Buenos Aires have higher impact than those in the province). These results 
indicate that the WTP is significantly lower more than 20 blocks from the river than it is for 
households within 20 blocks of the river. Table 9.3 presents the increase in property values 
predicted by the hedonic pricing model in percentage terms.

24. The estimated benefits of clean-up the M-R River using the hedonic pricing method 
are conservative in a longer-term perspective. In the long run, environmental remediation of 
the M-R River, elimination of its bad odor, and potential recreational uses is expected to 
revitalize the real estate market in the M-R River Basin and encourage new types of real 
estate development that are currently not possible because of the river’s poor environmental 
condition. Thus, the estimated short-term benefits from the elimination of odor are a lower 
bound on the longer-term benefits. 
25. Avoided costs. This technique was used to estimate two types of benefits to be 
attained through the program: (i) sewerage connections and (ii) protection of source water 
intakes. Access to sewerage was evaluated through hedonic pricing and avoided cost 
approaches, the results obtained with both methodologies were not added and were used only 
for comparison purposes. Benefits from the protection of water intake were measured through 
the avoided cost approach, as well as with the WTP method; the results obtained with both 
methods were not added as the results from the avoided costs were implicitly included in the 
WTP. However, avoided cost results were added to the results obtained with the hedonic 
price for the clean-up of the M-R River. 
26. Avoided costs due to sewerage connection. Currently 65 percent of households in the 
14 municipalities of the M-R River Basin do not have access to a sewerage connection. They 
use different solutions for sewage disposal: 30 percent of households have septic tanks, 17 
percent have holes with no septic chambers, and 18 percent have other solutions such as 
illegal discharge into the drainage system or direct discharge into the street. When households 
have access to a public sewerage connection, they will not have to operate these on-site 
systems, resulting in cost savings. On the other hand, households will have to pay the bill 
from the water and sewerage utility. The difference between these two costs is the lower 
bound on households’ benefits. 
27. Current costs of on-site sewage disposal systems vary depending on the frequency of 
cleaning, volume of discharge, and -- very important in this area -- altitude of the house 
location, as rising water tables cause frequent floods that cause septic tanks to overflow. The 
costs were calculated for proper maintenance of the systems according to the water table level 
in a particular location (table 9.4). The information collected from the willingness to pay 
survey and a small sample of households with on-site systems reveals that many of the 
households incur lower costs given improper system maintenance. 
108 
<<PAGE 118>>
Table 9.4. Costs of on-site sewage disposal systems assuming proper maintenance 
28. Households that rely on on-site sewerage solutions incur an additional investment cost 
of an “in-house adjustment” to connect to their on-site sewerage system. A cost ranging from 
US$l50 to US$250 was assumed depending on the type of adjustment. This cost was applied 
to about 40 percent of the households. 
29. Apart from this gain (assuming the increase in the households’ bills is lower than the 
cost savings), households may incur additional health benefits when a connection to public 
sewerage is provided. Also, households incur increased risk of water-borne illness if the 
private systems are not optimally maintained and operated. Because the averting actions 
(pumping and maintaining sewerage systems) do not completely eliminate the health risks for 
all households-particularly with improper system maintenance-the residual health risks 
remain, and the costs of illness (averaged for the population) can be added to the averting 
costs (on average for the population) to obtain the total benefits of sewerage provision. 
30. An additional benefit of providing sewerage connections is the value of time savings. 
Provision of public sewerage saves each adult approximately 5 minutes a day. If those time 
savings are valued at 75 percent of average wages (8 Argentine Pesos per hour), the net 
present value (NPV) of the total time savings reaches US$871 with a discount rate of 5 
percent and US$1,325 million with a discount rate of 10 percent.58 However, because the 
valuation of time savings is controversial, these were not added to total program benefits to 
avoid overestimating the program’s expected benefits. 
3 1. Avoided cost for water intake. If the program were not implemented, AySA would 
have to move the Bernal water intake to a site further out into La Plata River. With the 
program, AySA would avoid the cost of moving the intake. All sewage discharge will have 
appropriate treatment and will be diluted by La Plata River through the outfalls at an 
adequate distance from the water intake. Currently the Bernal water intake is close to the 
point of the untreated sewage discharge, and even though at present AySA deems that the 
water quality at the intake is adequate, increasing pollution in the “without program” scenario 
may require moving the water intake further out into the river in the near future. AySA 
previously moved that water intake to ensure adequate water quality at the intake. 
32. If the program is implemented, AySA will not have to incur this cost. Hence, this is a 
financial benefit generated by the program. This cost was calculated by AySA and estimated . 
at US$140 million in present value. It is purely an engineering cost that is useful from the 
financial point of view but does not provide an estimate or bound to the welfare impact of 
protecting water quality at the water intake. The actual benefit of protecting water quality was 
assessed by estimating the health cost caused by poor water quality at water intakes. 
Strukova, Elena. 2008. Health Benefits of Water-Sanitation-and Water Source Improvements in M-R and de 
la Plata Basins. Background paper for the economic evaluation of the project.

33. Cost ofillness approach. The cost of illness approach was used to estimate (i) the 
additional benefits of sewerage provision after taking into account the avoided costs of 
individual sewage disposal solutions and (ii) the health benefits associated with protecting 
water quality at the Bernal intake. These two interventions result in health benefits through 
the reduction of diarrheal mortality and morbidity as well as the risk of other water-borne 
diseases. For this program, the value of risk reduction from diarrheal morbidity and mortality 
was estimated by applying a benefit transfer approach to the value of statistical life numbers 
from developed countries.59 The expected risk reduction in mortality and morbidity from 
diarrheal diseases in children under 5 and in the rest of the population was derived from 
meta-analysis of studies of health risk reductions, and the data on the incidence and 
prevalence of diarrheal illness among children under 5 with different types of sanitation were 
estimated from the contingent valuation survey undertaken for the program.

34. The health benefits analysis provides two estimates of cost savings: health benefits 
resulting from the provision of sewerage to households that will obtain connection through 
the program and health benefits from the protection of drinking water quality. The latter 
estimation assumes that if the water intakes are not moved farther out into the river, then in 
the absence of the program, drinking water quality would deteriorate with an increased 
incidence and prevalence of diarrheal illnesses. The health costs analysis uses the average 
current incidence and prevalence of diarrheal illnesses-taking into account the fact that 
some households already undertake averting actions and incur averting expenditures to 
reduce their. health risks in the absence of a public sewerage connection. For that reason, the 
health costs estimated using this approach can be added to the averting expenditures to obtain 
an estimate of total benefits from the provision of sewerage connections.60

D. Results of the Evaluation 
36. The results of the evaluation are presented below for each component ((i) sewerage 
expansion, (ii) clean-up of the M-R River, and (iii) clean-up of both the M-R and La Plata 
Rivers) and for the entire program. 
37. Financial results. The results show a net loss of the program of US$480 million. The 
financial analysis of the program differs from the financial analysis of the entity (see Annex 
11B) that operates the program, (AySA), and hence the conclusions reached for the financial 
viability of the program do not necessarily apply to the financial viability of the company. 
The program would require an increase of sewerage tariffs for financial viability of the 
program, but that it is not the required increase for the financial viability of AySA. 
38. 
(i) , 
The main differences between these two analyses are as follows: 
The financial evaluation of the program calculates the incremental financial benefits 
and costs generated by each activity of the program (sewerage component and 
cleaning up of M-R and La Plata Rivers component) on an incremental basis (“with” 
and “without” program scenarios). The financial assessment of AySA analyzes the 
viability of the company as it is most likely to evolve following the implementation of 
the proposed program (“with” program scenario only). 
The financial evaluation of the program addresses only the cash flow generated by the 
program, without including other services provided by the company. The financial 
evaluation of AySA, on the other side, includes all the services it provides, i.e., water 
and sewerage services.

IRR from 16 percent to 19 percent. However, the component for cleaning up both the M-R 
and La Plata Rivers requires that the additional benefits for the sewerage component become 
economically viable. 
42. When benefits are added to obtain the benefit of all the components of the program, 
results vary depending on the approach used to aggregate the benefits (table 9.9). Five 
different combinations of approaches to measure program benefits are shown in the first 
column in table 9.9. For example, combination 1 combines the hedonic estimates of the 
impact of sewerage provision and eliminating odor for people living within 20 blocks of the 
M-R River with the contingent valuation estimates for cleaning up both rivers for the rest of 
the population of Greater Buenos Aires. 
43. The rationale for combining these two sets of results is the following. The prices of 
properties in proximity to the river are depressed by the odor and environmental 
contamination, and the adverse effect of the M-R River on property prices disappears beyond 
20 blocks from the river. Since revealed preference data are less likely to be affected by the 
hypothetical bias that is a common weakness of stated preference approaches, the hedonic 
estimates are likely to be closer to the true value of the benefits from removing the river’s bad 
odor than the WTP estimates from the contingent valuation survey for households living in 
the proximity of the river. However, for the population living hrther than 20 blocks away 
from the river, the only way to estimate the value of the benefits from removing the bad odor 
of the river (Le., the non-use value) is through the contingent valuation approach, since 
housing prices are not affected by the river at such a distance. 
44. Combination 1 is also preferable to the other approaches that rely on averting 
expenditures and health benefit estimates to estimate the benefits of sewerage provision or 
the benefit from protecting water quality at the water intake given the methodological issues 
and the quality of the available data on health risks and the avoided costs., The measure of 
the benefits of sewerage obtained from the hedonic function in this program is more robust. 
The net returns estimated using this approach are US$ 1 billion and the economic internal 
rate of return is 3 1 percent.

45. Distributional impact. The difference between economic and financial flows represents 
rents or monetary flows that accrue to an entity other than AySA. Taxes represent monetary 
flows accruing to the Government, while subsidies are transfers from the Government to AySA. 
Winners and losers from the program were identified by deconstructing economic and financial 
costs for each input of the program and then estimating the difference between economic and 
financial results. This analysis uses the economic results obtained with estimation 1, which was 
considered the preferred approach. 
46. As table 9.10 shows, the big winners are the customers, with a return of US$2.8 billion. 
If this benefit is distributed among the poor and non-poor, assuming that the poor are the 23 
percent who lack basic services, according to geographical information on the beneficiaries and 
the 2001 census, results show that poor beneficiaries will reap approximately US$645 million of 
this benefit. The earnings come from the benefits obtained with the increase in property values 
when sewerage service is connected. This benefit offsets the payment these households have to 
make to AySA when connected and billed, as well as the investment cost they incur to adjust in- 
house connections. The big loser is the Government because of the investment subsidy, which is 
not offset by the higher taxes received. The total loss for the Government is approximately 
US$1.7 billion. The net gain for the economy as a whole is US$l billion, as shown in table 9.10).

47. If sewerage tariffs were to increase to make the program financially viable, households 
that currently have access to sewerage would be adversely affected; and the provision of access 
to sewerage has a positive impact on household welfare. Households that did not have access to 
sewerage and who will gain it through the program will experience welfare gains; households 
that did not have sewerage and will not gain it will not be affected; and households that had 
sewerage service before the program will experience welfare losses if they have to pay higher 
tariffs for service as a result of the program. 
48. The magnitude of the combined impact of a change in access and tariffs on welfare of 
households at different income levels can be assessed through a comparison of access rates and 
budget shares at different income levels. The welfare impact of access provision is an average of 
US$ 40 per month per household for households living in apartments and US$ 64 per month for 
households in houses, as estimated from the hedonic analysis.61 Access to sewerage tends to be

49. The welfare impact of an increase in water and sewerage tariffs is proportional to the 
magnitude of the tariff increase and to the share of water and sewerage expenditures in total 
household budgets.62 Thus the welfare impact will be higher for households that spend a higher 
share of their total income on water and sewerage. As estimated from the data on utility 
expenditures in the contingent valuation survey, on average, households spend 1.1 percent of 
total income on water and sewerage, ranging from 0.5 percent for households in the highest 
income quintile to 1.9 percent for the poorest households in the bottom quintile. Expenditures on 
all utilities (water, sewerage, street lighting, gas, electricity, and fixed telephone) average 7.5 
percent of total income and range from 4.3 percent for households in the highest income quintile 
to 12.6 percent for those in the lowest q~intile.~~ The relatively low share of water and sewerage 
expenditures in total household income means that a 50 percent increase or even a doubling of 
water and sewerage tariffs will on average have a limited impact on household incomes. 
However, the impact will be higher for the poorer households, as a result of the relatively higher 
share of water and sewerage expenditures in their total incomes. 
E. Sensitivity and Risk Analyses 
50. The results obtained in the analysis carried out so far assume certainty regarding all the 
variables. The sensitivity and risk analyses measure the impact of changes in assumed values for 
critical variables on the results. The sensitivity analysis measures the outcome if one of the 
variables changes while all others remain fixed. The risk analysis measures the outcome when all 
selected variables change at the same time, each one based on a probability distribution. Both

apartments and the census radius where the homes are located, the quantity consumed before and after the tariff 
increase cannot be estimated.

estimated by Delfmo and Casarin (2001) using the 1998 Household Budget Survey data (INDEC) for Argentina.

analyses were undertaken to test the robustness of the economic outcome. The financial results 
were not tested because the program is not financially viable without a sewerage tariff increase.

Direct impact on investment costs. Largely under management control. Conveys a 
moderate risk to the economic results. The project would show positive returns for an 
increase in costs as high as 28 percent.

low risk to the economic results. The operating costs could overrun 68% and still 
shows positive results.

risk to economic results. If the project is delayed two or more years, the returns 
would be negative. 
It corresponds to the acceptance of population to effectively connect to the sewerage 
network and eliminate household on-site sewage disposal systems. It has a direct 
impact on benefits. It depends largely on the communication strategy of AySA and 
financial alternatives offered to the beneficiaries to make it easy to accept the 
connection and adjust households’ in-house facilities. It carries an important risk for 
the project. The connection rate has to be higher than 72% for the project to still have 
positive returns. 
Direct impact on benefits and beyond management control. It conveys a medium to 
low risk to the project. If the real estate market does not react as expected, and the 
magnitude of the property value increase when sewerage is connected is only 74% of 
the assumed benefit, or just 17% of the assumed increase for the clean-up of the M-R 
River, the project would still show positive returns. 
Direct impact on benefits and beyond management control. It conveys a low risk to 
the project. If the willingness to pay were zero for the non-use value (for the 
households living more than 20 blocks away from the M-R River), the project would 
still generate benefits totaling US$ 200 million and have a 18% internal rate of return. 
Risk Analysis 
52. To enhance the accuracy of the economic analysis, the risk analysis has been carried out 
using a Monte Carlo simulation. The analysis measures the extent of various risks and their 
impact on the results of the project by modeling a likely probability distribution that best 
describes the behavior of each of the selected variables. Based on a simulation of 1,000 trials the 
117 
<<PAGE 127>>
model recalculated the results of the financial and economic analyses by simultaneously 
changing each of the selected risk variables according to their probability distribution. 
For each variable a probability distribution was chosen with the following criteria: 
For the WTP, a normal distribution with standard deviation of 2.76 was chosen for the 
M-R River and La Plata River, and 2.94 for the M-R River, based on the results of the 
WTP survey. 
For the benefits of hedonic prices the analysis assumes an average reduction of 10% with 
the normal distribution with a standard deviation of 3%. 
For costs overrun, the analysis assumes a triangular distribution with the most likely 
outcome of no increase, minimum value a reduction of 5%, and maximum increase of 
15%. 
For project delays, the analysis assumes the following probabilities: no delays: 70%; one 
year delay: 15% probability; two year delay: 10% probability, and three year delay: 5% 
For connection rate, the analysis assumes 10% probability of a 70% connection rate; 70% 
probability for an 80% connection, 15% probability for a 90% connection; and 5% 
probability for 100% connection. 
Using the Monte Carlo simulation, which calculates 1,000 possible scenarios by 
repeatedly sknpling values from the probability distributions defined for the uncertain variables, 
the result shows that the probability of having a positive outcome is 77%. This result 
underscores the robustness of the project's expected positive net benefits under a range of 
scenarios.

Annex 10: Safeguard Policies 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project 
A. Introduction 
1. This annex summarizes the findings and recommendations of Environmental and Social 
Impact Assessments prepared for the proposed Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable 
Development Project. It provides a brief description of the assessment process and main 
documents prepared; the legal and institutional framework governing the project; project and 
strategic alternatives considered; the likely environmental impacts associated with project 
execution and operation; and the principal management and mitigation measures proposed for 
addressing those issues arising from the environmental For a complete 
description of the project objectives, phasing of the APL program and the specific scope of 
project components refer to Annex 4 of the PAD. 
2. Environmental assessment process. The environmental risks, impacts, and benefits of the 
project have been analyzed at two different levels using two closely related environmental 
assessment approaches. The environmental impacts (both positive and negative) of specific 
project components have been assessed through a detailed seven-volume EIA conducted for 
specific works proposed under Component 1 and through separate Environmental Management 
Frameworks (EMF) prepared for activities to be carried out under Components 2 (Industrial 
Pollution Abatement) and 3 (Environmental Territorial Management). In addition to the site- 
specific EIA and the EMF, an Integrated Environmental Assessment (IEA) has been prepared 
which serves two purposes: first, it summarizes the main findings of the more detailed EIA 
reports; second, the IEA presents the overall regional context and strategic goals for the long 
term recovery and management of the Matanza-Riachuelo basin in its entirety. 
3. At the site-specific level, a detailed EIA report was prepared for the works in APL-1 
under Component 1, for which the final technical solutions have been identified. For those 
works where the final technical solutions have not yet been identified, site specific EIAs will be 
prepared and will be reviewed by the Bank during project implementation. For those works at or 
before pre-feasibility stage (such as the right bank collector or a suitable alternative for the same 
function), site-specific EIAs, acceptable to the Bank will be prepared by AySA6’. A seven- 
volume EIA was prepared under the general direction of the Ay SA Environmental Management 
Unit using independent environmental engineering consultants for key aspects of the analysis. 
These assessments, which were coordinated with associated modeling and other technical 
evaluations, indicate the overall environmental viability of the proposed project activities. The 
EIA provides a thorough assessment of the project’s environment impact during the construction

and operational stages for the project’s main civil works. The EIA for Component 1 includes 
specific volumes on the general conditions and background of the project; a description of

plant; the pumping stations; the Riachuelo sub-aquatic outfall; as well as the colector margen

management measures. Furthermore, since the Government advanced the construction of the 
Berazategui preliminary treatment plant (BPTP)66 (functionally an investment under APL-2), an 
EIA was prepared in July 2007 and has been disclosed in-country by AYSA.~~ The Bank team

Environmental Management Plan for the BPTP, acceptable to the Bank, will be presented by 
AySA within 6 months of loan effectiveness. 
4. The Integrated Environmental Assessment was prepared under the overall guidance of 
ACUMAR and corresponds to a regional, basin-wide assessment of environmental risks and 
benefits. The IEA describes the baseline conditions of the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin, the legal 
and institutional setting and the strategic challenges and options that underpin the choice of the 
specific project components and investment activities. This IEA was used to evaluate the 
regional strategic objectives of the project (including infrastructure works that belong to the 
PISA but that will not be financed through the proposed Bank loans such as the Berazategui 
preliminary treatment plant) and to provide a clear rationale for the project components that 
respond to these objectives. The IEA also describes the structure of the project as a whole, 
including a detailed description of the environmental risks and benefits of the three project 
components and the institutional arrangements for implementing the Environmental 
Management Plan (EMP) for the project. 
5. Social assessment process: Given the complexity of the Project, two complementary 
social assessments were prepared for ACUMAR and AySA during project preparation. 
ACUMAR’s social assessment was a basin-wide study, describing the social baseline conditions 
which exist in the M-R River Basin, identifying stakeholder perceptions, positions, and influence 
on the project, highlighting the existing communication channels, pinpointing the overall social 
impacts of the project, and assessing its various risks. AySA’s social assessment, on the other 
hand, explored the opinion of stakeholders about the proposed infrastructure works to be carried 
out by the company. 
66 In the 19703, Obras Sanitarias de la Nacidn (OSN), the public water and sewerage authority for the Buenos Aires 
Metropolitan area that preceded Aguas Argentinas, constructed a 2.5 km sub-aquatic outfall in the municipality of 
Berazategui to discharge sewage from its service area to La Plata River. In 2004, the municipality of Berazategui 
and local residents initiated judicial proceedings against Aguas Argentinas for discharging sewage to the La Plata 
River without adequate treatment. In 2006, the case was elevated to Argentina’s Supreme Court, which ruled in 
favor of the municipality and local residents and ordered the construction of a preliminary treatment plant. After 
Aguas Argentinas’ concession was rescinded in March of 2006, AySA assumed responsibility for complying with 
the Supreme Court ruling and, in 2008, awarded a contract for construction of the Berazategui sewage preliminary 
treatment plant (BPTP). While work on the BPTP is expected to be carried out in parallel with APL-1, from a 
technical standpoint, the plant complements a Bank-financed civil work under APL-2 (Le. the Berazategui sub- 
aquatic outfall) and has therefore been included as a counterpart work under APL-2.

6. Both SAs were developed based on available project information and consultations with 
relevant stakeholders. The teams that conducted the SA carried out 45 meetings with 
stakeholders including representatives of community organizations, NGOs, the industrial sector, 
provincial and municipal officials, and members of academia. Stakeholders also participated in 
workshops, including those for the presentation of the TOR’S for the EIAs and the draft EIAs 
themselves. 
7. Regarding the BPTP, the World Bank team was recently informed (March 2009) that the 
construction site required resettlement of 18 families that had illegally occupied the site and 
erected precarious sheet-metal dwellings. In October 2008, families were relocated to new 
concrete houses approximately 2-3 km from the BPTP site. The Bank considers that the 
relocation of families from the BPTP site does not require application of OP 4.12 since 
construction of the BPTP is neither directly related to the project (APL-1) nor necessary to 
achieve its objectives. This conclusion notwithstanding, the Bank team conducted a preliminary 
assessment of the resettlement program and has found no evidence to indicate worsened living 
conditions. Families have been provided housing built from concrete blocks on 300 m2 lots with 
running water. Schools and health facilities are nearby and transport services are accessible. The 
Bank will continue to monitor living conditions going forward. 
B. Legal and Institutional Framework 
8. The legal and institutional context for the management of the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin is 
complex. It involves 17 jurisdictions and 29 relevant agencies that co-exist in the Matanza- 
Riachuelo. Each of these agencies has jurisdiction over and/or responsibility for some aspects of 
water resources and/or their areas of influence. The institutional complexity stems from the need 
to create an environment of coordination that would allow legal-institutional consistency in order 
to implement the integrated management of water resources. Therefore, there is a risk that these 
various regulators may clash and fail to respond to an integrated management focus on water 
resources, making it impossible to achieve the objective of reversing the basin’s polluted status. 
To respond to these challenges, Law 26.168, approved on November 15, 2006, created the 
Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Authority (Autoridad de Cuenca Matanza-Riachuelo, or ACUMAR) 
as an inter-jurisdictional public agency under the scope of the Secretariat of the Environment and 
Sustainable Development (Secretaria de Ambiente y Desarrollo Sustentable, or SAyDS) in the 
Office of the Chief of the Cabinet of Ministers. The duties of the Authority include (i) inter- 
jurisdictional coordination under a principle of subsidiarity, giving ACUMAR some 
preeminence over local authorities; (ii) execution authority to adopt measures necessary or 
relevant for the basin’s sanitation and execute the budget of ACUMAR; (iii) regulation and 
control responsibilities, among others, to harmonize regulatory initiatives dealing with liquid 
effluents and gas emissions, environmental land use planning, rates for services, ACUMAR’s 
organizational bylaws, establishment of a special administrative procedure, implementation of 
preventive measures, intervention on allocations, audits, monitoring, EIAs, sanctions, warnings, 
writs, inspections, closures, seizures, and cessation of activities. The manner in which these 
activities will be undertaken will also require that there is compliance with the law on access to 
environmental information.68 
Law No. 25831 on Access to Public Environmental Information approved on 23 November, 2003. 
121 
<<PAGE 131>>
9. Under Resolutions 1/2007 and 2/2008, ACUMAR has in place permissible limits 
(relating to organic and toxic loads) for discharges of effluents into sewage networks, surface 
water bodies as well as soil absorption. For effluent discharges, these limits are in accordance 
with the norms established by AySA for discharges into its sewage networks.69 Additionally, the 
Government is in the process of finalizing norms/ regulations relating to uses and quality of the 
receiving water bodies in the short, medium and long terms. These regulations and norms will 
serve as the underlying basis for determining the treatment to be undertaken and targets to be met 
by polluting enterprises in the M-R River Basin. 
C. Analysis of Alternatives 
10. Approach to Alternatives Analysis. The Analysis of Alternatives carried out for the 
project has taken place both at the strategic level through the PISA and the PDSCMR and 
through the evaluation of alternatives for each of the proposed works with respect to their 
technical configurations such as size, flow volumes, and location. In addition to the no-project 
alternative, the project has identified a comprehensive set of alternatives for the management of 
the municipal wastewater of the M-R River Basin and analyzed these alternatives in conjunction 
with alternatives for management of the industrial wastes of the basin. A detailed description of 
alternatives analyzed and their implications for the environment is presented in Annex 1 1 A. 
1 1. Alternatives considered. Six strategic alternatives were considered in the development of 
the sanitation component of the project. These include: (i) a decentralized treatment option 
involving a series of treatment plants using different treatment levels, discharging their effluents 
into the Matanza-Riachuelo River, its tributaries, and the Rio de la Plata; (ii) a centralized 
treatment option with a single preliminary treatment plant in Berazategui and a sub-aquatic 
outfall discharging the effluent into La Plata River; (iii) a centralized treatment option with two 
points of discharge into La Plata River through sub-aquatic outfalls; (iv) different levels of 
wastewater treatment prior to discharge into La Plata River (these alternatives involve 
determining the lengths of the outfalls as a function of the treatment levels, as higher levels of 
treatment may require shorter outfalls); (v) secondary treatment options followed by chlorination 
of the effluent and discharge of the effluent into La Plata River, coupled with using a short 
outfall, and (vi) alternatives for the main wastewater conveyance system to ensure the collection 
of all the wastewater of the basin and of the entire Argentina Water and Sanitation (Agua y 
Saneamientos Argentinos S.A., or AySA) concession area. 
12. Among the conveyance alternatives analyzed was (i) a single left bank collector with the 
capacity to also convey all wastewater from the middle and upper basin of the Matanza- 
Riachuelo River toward the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant and (ii) two collectors. 
Factors taken into consideration in selecting the preferred strategic alternative included 
69 This is in line with the World Bank Group’s Environmental Health and Safety Guidelines applicable to the World

<<PAGE 132>>
achievement of environmental objectives, avoidance of unwarranted environmental risks, total 
cost and accommodations of future expansion of the wastewater system. 
13. In addition to the strategic alternatives identified above, a detailed description of site- 
specific alternatives was carried out by AySA through the EIA process for the works in APL-1 , 
for which technical solutions have been identified. Alternatives were discussed for each of the 
specific civil works investments, including the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant, the 
Riachuelo outfall, the Left Bank collector, the Baja Costanera bypass collector, and the 
Riachuelo preliminary treatment plan associated pumping stations. Alternatives evaluated 
included alternative site selection and routing, design capacity, and technology. These 
alternatives and their environmental implications are discussed in detail in the seven-volume EIA 
for AySA works. 
14. The two alternatives considered for reducing the industrial pollution were (i) high-level 
treatment of all industrial wastes and discharge of all effluents except those containing toxic 
matter into the M-R River and (ii) lower-level treatment of all industrial wastes and discharge of 
all effluents except those containing toxic matter into the municipal sewerage network for further 
processing with domestic wastewater. In addition to the strategic alternatives, ACUMAR will 
evaluate alternative specific initiatives to be financed under the industrial pollution abatement 
component under the Environmental Management Framework (EMF) developed for this 
component. 
15. Because sub-projects to be financed under Component 3 (Environmental Territorial 
Management) of the project have yet to be identified, it is not possible to identify site-specific 
alternatives. Under the terms of the EMF for this component, ACUMAR will be required to 
collaborate with local communities to identify specific alternatives through the planning and sub- 
project selection process. 
16. The Selected Alternative. Based on the analysis of alternatives, Alternative 3 -- 
distributing wastewater flow to two sub-aquatic outfalls -- is the preferred option for the 
sanitation component. This alternative distributes the total collected wastewater flow into two 
preliminary treatment plants, one located on the southern bank of the M-R River at its confluence 
with La Plata River and the other in Berazategui. The effluents will be discharged into La Plata 
River through two long sub-aquatic outfalls. This alternative will result in lower and more 
affordable capital costs, the technical advantages of simple operation and maintenance, 
significant positive environmental impacts in the M-R River, and low, and broadly acceptable, 
adverse impacts with minimum negative social impacts in the Rio de la Plata. See Annex 4 of the 
PAD for a detailed description of the project options selected. 
17. The industrial pollution abatement component of the selected alternative will incorporate 
(i) generation of databases on the contributions of industrial pollutants, (ii) definition of priorities 
and order of implementation of Industrial Restructuring Plans (Planes de Reconversion 
Industrial, or PRI), (iii) definition of discharge conditions and setting of permissible limits of 
pollutants in the short, medium, and long terms, (iv) identification of specific objectives or 
targets to be met in the PRIs of each industry, (v) prioritization of PRIs in industrial plants with 
higher pollutant loads and/or greater toxic effects, and (vi) implementation of real-time systems 
123 
<<PAGE 133>>
to monitor water input flows, wastewater discharge flows, and concentration of polluting 
substances in the industrial effluents. 
D. Environmental and Social Baseline Conditions 
18. General. The Matanza-Riachuelo (M-R) is a tributary of the Rio de la Plata and pollution 
of the river basin is one of the most visible environmental issues in the country. Over the past 
century, the M-R River Basin has been used as an open sewer for a large potion of the city of 
Buenos Aires. Pollution levels have increased steadily with urbanization in the greater 
metropolitan area of Buenos Aires and accompanying industrial growth within the basin. It is 
estimated that more than 4,000 industrial facilities are located in the lower and middle sections of 
the basin. Many of these industries discharge untreated effluents into the local drainage system 
or directly into the M-R River. In addition to high levels of organic pollution, these discharges 
contribute toxic contaminants such as heavy metals from petrochemical industries, tanneries, and 
meat processing facilities. The acute environmental and social degradation of the M-R River 
Basin has created serious health risks, especially for the highly vulnerable social groups. 
19. Project Area of Influence. The environmental assessments (EA) prepared by ACUMAR 
took a broad approach in defining the project’s area of influence. The area of influence includes 
the entire Matanza-Riachuelo Basin as well as the entire concession area of AySA. These two 
areas overlap but are not entirely contiguous. The project’s EA also took into consideration three 
other zones: (i) the fluvial sections of the Lujan River from the mouth of the Reconquista River 
up to its confluence, (ii) the section of La Plata River between the coast and the outer limit of the 
so-called Parana de las Palmas flow corridor to the height of the channel entering the Port of La 
Plata, (iii) the coastal section between the bank of La Plata River and the neighborhood access 
road to the river approximately 14 km southeast of the boundary of the General Belgrano plant 
site. The area of influence includes coastal sections of La Plata River up to 50 km downstream 
from the treatment plants as well as approximately 30 km upstream. 
20. Population and Development Context. The M-R basin is home to Argentina’s largest 
concentration of urban poor. Of the approximately 3.5 million inhabitants in the basin, 1.2 
million live below the poverty line. An estimated 10 percent of the total population in the M-R 
River Basin lives in informal settlements, often in flood-prone areas near open garbage dumps. 
The poorest populations living alongside the river are in constant contact with numerous 
contaminants ranging from untreated organic waste to toxic industrial chemicals. The M-R also 
floods frequently because of high flows in the rainy season and high levels of water in La Plata 
River. This flooding spreads highly polluted waters into informal settlements, exposing the 
inhabitants to further health risks from contaminated flood waters. 
21. The M-R basin includes the jurisdictions of 14 municipalities and of the City of Buenos 
Aires, with an estimated population of 3.5 million (projected by the 2001 Population and 
Housing Census). The distribution of population in the basin responds to a progressive evolution 
of the location and not as a result of a logical distribution or planning of urban land uses and 
services. The population in the northern zone of Buenos Aires ranks in the highest socio- 
economic level, while the southern zone has a population in a lower socio-economic level. 
124 
<<PAGE 134>>
22. Water Oualitv. The significant deterioration of surface water quality in the M-R River 
and most of the tributaries in the middle and upper parts of the river is a result of decades of 
poorly treated or uncontrolled pollutant discharge. These water courses have received organic 
discharges, mineral and inorganic substances, heavy metals, pesticides, fungicides, herbicides, 
and sediments at levels far exceeding the river’s capacity for dilution and self-purification. 
Residents along the course of the river must endure both the sever health and aesthetic impacts of 
the water pollution. In some sections, the odor from the river can be overwhelming. 
23. Land use. Land use within the lower M-R basin has a strong industrial profile with over 
4,000 companies located within the basin. Industrial development along the banks of the water 
courses in the basin has had a direct and profound impact on the nature, levels, and frequency of 
liquid effluents disposed into the water bodies of the basin. The industrial sector is highly 
concentrated in the lower part of the basin near the Dock Sud Petrochemical Hub. Preliminary 
data show that the industrial sector is the major source of organic contamination, contributing 
about 73 percent7’ of the total load of organic pollution. These industries are also a significant 
source of other toxic pollutants, including heavy metals. 
24. The composition of industries is highly varied with many different types of activities and 
industrial processes. Those with the greatest environmental impacts are in the chemical, 
petrochemical, food, tanning, cold storage, and electrometallurgical sectors. Many industries use 
arsenic and heavy metals such as chromium, mercury, and lead for their production processes, 
and all of these substances have been found in the waters of the M-R River in concentrations that 
exceed the levels permitted by relevant regulations 
25. Groundwater. These domestic and industrial discharge problems also affect regional 
groundwater resources related to the basin. In zones lacking sewerage, pollution from discharges 
from domestic septic tanks can reach the water table. Aquifers are also affected by the 
inadequate treatment and disposal of industrial wastes. Frequent floods in the M-R River Basin 
produce considerable losses and damage to goods and people. Flood risks in all urban zones are a 
consequence of the low-lying terrain, insufficient drainage and deficient discharge from main 
pipelines, and unplanned urbanization that overtook natural drainage areas. 
26. The project’s area of influence 
covers a large geographic area that includes a number of potentially sensitive terrestrial and 
aquatic sites of biodiversity importance. While the area as a whole has been subject to a number 
of threats and impacts from a wide range of development pressures, there remain some areas of 
biological interest. For example, there are two known terrestrial ecological reserves within the 
project area of influence. 
Critical Natural Habitats and Biological Diversitv. 
27. The Reserva Natural Costanera Sur is located upstream of the planned treatment and 
outfall works near Puerto Madero. The Reserva Natural Punta Lara is a terrestrial reserve 
downstream of the planned works to the southeast of Quilmes and close to Gran la Plata. 
28. There are no declared aquatic conservation areas within the project’s area of influence, 
but there are two areas which have been identified as priorities for coastal and marine protection. 
~ 
70 From Modeling Report No 5. 
125 
<<PAGE 135>>
FREPLATA has proposed two such potential areas of bi-national protection. One in the area 
known as Frente de Delta in the delta of the Rio Paraguay and the second in the area referred to 
as the Frente de Turbidez. Both of these areas are far from the impact zone of the project (see 
Section on Impacts). The Atlas of Sensibilidad de la Costa y El Mar Argentino identifies the 
region as providing habitat for marine birds, fresh water fishes and of habitat for dolphins and 
porpoise. 
E. Expected Environmental and Social Risks Associated with component 1 (Sanitation) 
Construction Phase 
29. General. The environmental and social impacts and risks associated with this component 
will vary during the construction and operational phases of the project. During construction the 
main socio-environmental risks relate to: (i) the temporary construction-related impacts 
associated with the large-scale civil works. Among others these include the disruption to local 
traffic flows, and access to commercial areas, dust and noise control, handling and management 
of hazardous construction materials, emergency response planning; erosion and sediment control 
and disposal of construction debris, (ii) the potential impacts on bio-diversity and human health 
associated with disposal of spoil materials from tunneling; and (iii) the potential biodiversity and 
health impacts of re-suspension of dredge materials during dredging operations. 
30. Impacts during construction stem from such activities as transportation of sand, concrete 
and construction materials, earth compacting, concrete pouring, and operation of construction 
equipment. Some of the specific impacts from the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant and 
pumping stations, based on AySA’s EIA, are discussed below. 
3 1, Tunnel moil disposal. The main collectors and sub-aquatic outfalls will be installed 
using trenchless tunnel technology methods. That is, pipes will be installed by tunneling using 
pipe jacking for smaller diameter (up to 3 m) pipes and segment installation for larger-diameter 
pipes. The trenchless pipe-jacking technology will significantly reduce the negative 
environmental impacts of the works by concentrating the impacts in the limited areas of the 
attack shafts, which are installed at distances of 1-1.5 km along the pipe route and for segment 
tunneling at distances of approximately 5 km. 
32. Air qualitv and odors. During the construction stage, air quality may be affected by an 
increased concentration of suspended particles and carbon monoxide as a consequence of earth 
moving and machinery operation. Bothersome odors may be expected from the removal of dirt 
as a product of excavations. Another action that can generate odors is the temporary storage and 
disposal of construction waste. These impacts were characterized as negative with a low long- 
term environmental risk. They will generally be of medium or low intensity, brief in duration, 
and localized, appear immediately, and have a direct effect, ongoing while the operation that 
produces them lasts, and be reversible. These impacts are expected to be temporary and not 
significant, and they will be addressed through the contractor’s site-specific management plans. 
33. Noise. Temporary or continuous elevation in noise levels in the areas directly affected by 
the construction work may occur as a result of the movement and operation of trucks and 
mechanical equipment. The main sources of noise and vibrations will be (i) operation of heavy 
equipment and manual tools, (ii) movement of personnel and light vehicles, and (iii) mobile 
126 
<<PAGE 136>>
equipment and machinery such as electric generators. Noise impacts will be temporary during 
the construction phase. They are likely to be negative and have a medium or moderate effect, a 
low to medium intensity, an immediate effect of short duration, a local scope, and intermittent 
occurrence while the works that generate them continue. The contractor’s site specific 
management plan will include measures to address these effects. 
34. Soil uualiv and stabilitv. Soil quality may be affected by leaching, dumping, and hauling 
of solid or liquid materials that are in temporary use or transported to their final disposal site. 
Possible impacts on soil quality in these cases could be of medium or high intensity depending 
on the type of material involved and the local context. Overall, direct effect and will be 
temporary in duration and strict controls on dumping will be included as part of the contractors 
site specific management plans. 
35. Compacting andor settling of soils around construction sites may result from a variety of 
project construction activities. These include excavation, movement of heavy machinery, 
temporary disposal of large volumes of materials, residues, and debris, depression of the water 
table, large-scale, heavy installations, and demolition. These possible impacts will be negative, 
of medium or high intensity, of local scope, with a direct effect and of an occasional nature, and 
of temporary duration. 
36. During earth moving and excavations, the walls of ditches may cave in, causing the loss 
of soil stability and potentially affecting the foundations of buildings and public facilities or 
existing pipelines and underground infrastructure (e.g., water network, gas network, electrical 
and phone lines). The impacts that may occur in these cases will be negative, of medium or high 
intensity, of local scope, with direct effect, of an occasional nature, and of temporary or 
permanent duration. Although such impacts are highly unlikely if the works are properly 
designed and executed, all necessary measures to avoid these risks must be taken into account. 
The contractor’s site specific management plans will include specific references to contingency 
measures in the event of unexpected construction accidents. 
37. Surface water. aualitv. Construction activities that may affect the quality of water 
resources during the construction stage include (i) hauling of solids and/or liquids during the 
clearing of work sites, (ii) leaching, dumping, and hauling of solids that have been in temporary 
use or are transported to their final disposal site, and (iii) sediments from earth movement may 
reach surface waters. The negative impacts that these activities may generate will be direct, of 
low to medium intensity, of short duration, of local scope, and occasional. No long-term negative 
impacts were identified in this aspect, taking into account the current baseline conditions of the 
water bodies in the project area. 
38. Groundwater levels and auality. Available data on the relationship between the behavior 
of the groundwater table and the activities carried out during construction (e.g., depression of the 
water table, disposal of extracted water) are not adequate to evaluate their effect. However, based 
on AySA’s experience in this type of works, the activities associated with the project are not 
expected to modify the aquifer’s behavior in the zone. 
39. Re-suspension of sediments during dredging operations in the rivers. One of the impacts 
associated with dredging operations is the movement and re-suspension of sediments that may 
127 
<<PAGE 137>>
contain significant concentrations of pollutants. In this regard, two types of impacts can be 
identified for the project: (i) increased turbidity from the re-suspension of solids and increased 
concentration of suspended solids in the water body, with a consequent decrease in transparency, 
and (ii) transfer of polluting substances between solids in suspension and water, producing an 
increase in the concentrations of these contaminants in the aqueous phase. 
40. This impact is considered moderate to high, depending on the factors affected (especially 
turbidity) and the zone where dredging operations will be conducted. Especially important will 
be the impact of works in La Plata River and their potential impact on the turbidity of water in 
the intakes for the Buenos Aires drinking water treatment plants. To evaluate potential impacts 
from dredging, a mathematical simulation model was developed for solids behavior in La Plata 
River. Its results are presented in the AySA EIA study. This model takes into account extreme 
scenarios of current velocities and winds, identifies the risks, and proposes mitigation measures. 
A mitigating effect is attributed to the fact that the outfalls will be constructed using trenchless 
technology, with the majority of tunneling works located below the riverbed. 
41, Impacts on flora and fauna. No significant construction impacts on terrestrial flora or 
fauna are expected during the construction phases of the project. Project construction works will 
not affect any known sensitive or critical terrestrial natural habitats in the project’s area of 
influence. All project works are either located underground or in highly degraded or heavily 
industrialized areas. Conservation areas are located within the project’s area of influence, but 
these areas will not be affected by project construction. Plant cover and small shrubs may be 
affected by the establishment of work staging sites and storage areas, the temporary disposal of 
surplus earth and work debris, and the movement of vehicles and heavy machinery. These 
impacts are considered to be temporary and insignificant. 
42. The construction of the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant will occur in the Dock Sud 
area on a parcel of land given to the national government by the municipality of Avellaneda 
under a 99-year lease. Based on the EIA and confirmed by site visits, it is apparent that the 
selected site for the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant is highly degraded and has been 
heavily impacted by previous industrial development of the petro-chemical industry. AySA has 
confirmed that it has already taken possession of the site. 
43. The construction of the Riachuelo outfall is expected to have temporary but limited 
impacts on aquatic biodiversity since these works are mainly underground. Re-suspension of 
sediments during dredging operations may contribute to some temporary incremental 
degradation of the water quality but this must be viewed in a context of high incidence of 
maintenance dredging which is already occurring throughout the major shipping lanes 
throughout project area of influence. 
44. It is possible that construction site accidents could lead to some unexpected impacts on 
vegetated areas or urban parks. Special care will be taken to avoid spilling construction site 
contaminants that may be harmful to vegetation. The impacts resulting from such accidents, 
though negative, would be likely be localized as no construction site is near any important 
vegetated areas. 
128 
<<PAGE 138>>
45. Occupational health and public safety. Because of the magnitude and extent of civil 
works, a large number of skilled and unskilled workers will be employed during project 
construction. Strict occupational health and safety measures will be applied to avoid accidents 
affecting workers and people living in the project construction areas. The movement of heavy 
equipment may pose some risk to local residents and all construction contractors’ site 
management plans will include specific reference to public safety concerns. 
Operational Phase 
46. General. During project operations the principal environmental risks relate to (i) the 
environmental and human health effects of long-term discharges of wastewater into the Rio de la 
Plata through the two sub-aquatic outfalls; (ii) the environmental and human health risks 
associated with management and disposal of continuously generated solids from the Riachuelo 
preliminary treatment plant; and (iii) localized nuisance issues relating to operations at the 
Riachuelo plant such as odor and noise. The main social risks are associated with (i) pollution on 
the coast resulting from the discharge into the Rio de la Plata, affecting recreational activities and 
navigation, (ii) sewerage tariff increase, and (iii) displacement of low income groups which 
cannot afford higher land costs (gentrification). 
47. Consideration of the potential adverse operational risks should be balanced with a 
consideration of the very important potential environmental and human health benefits of the 
project which include: reduced exposures to existing high-levels contaminants in the Matanza- 
Riachuelo and improved environmental conditions on the shoreline of the Rio de la Plata 
including the interface with conservation areas. In addition to the health benefits of the project, 
significant quality of life benefits are expected to result over the long term including 
improvements in recreational value of the basin and improved livability in the impact zones. 
48. Long-term positive impacts on water quality. Dissolved oxygen (DO) concentrations in 
the M-R River system were forecast during the low flow period after project implementation. 
The simulation rendered positive impact in water quality in various sections of the basin showing 
positive concentrations of dissolved oxygen in previously anoxic conditions (Menendez, Angel. 
UTN. 2008). 
49. Figure 10.1 shows the Rio de la Plata zones which are inadequate as a water source for 
human consumption under current conditions, prior to project implementation. It shows that most 
of the inadequate zone is affected by pathogenic organisms. Pathogens are a critical pollutant 
because organic matter discharges do not significantly modify the DO levels of La Plata River. 
Metals and toxic substances are also not the main problem, because of the pre-existing load in 
the river arriving from its upstream sections. Figure 10.2 illustrates the expected improvement of 
water quality after project implementation, showing the alleviation of the risk of inadequate 
water quality for human consumption in the intake zones of Bema1 and San Martin, as well as 
the major positive impact of the project in terms of reducing pathogenic contamination along the 
coast of La Plata River. 
50. During the operational phase of the project, there will be a redistribution of zones currently 
considered unsuitable for recreation with direct contact, alleviating pollution in the coastal zone 
and permitting its use for recreation. A significant part of the coastal zone will also be recovered 
129 
<<PAGE 139>>
for recreation without direct contact. The unfavorable status of chronic toxicity for aquatic life 
in La Plata River will not change because the contribution of pollutants from the Parana de las 
Palmas River (upstream from Buenos Aires) continues and is independent of the project. The 
reduction of the zone unsuitable for aquatic life because of acute toxicity is insignificant, because 
Sarandi and Santo Domingo creeks continue to contribute significant amounts of chromium.

water source for human consumption, current
