after project implementation (APL-I and APL-2) 
5 1. Impacts on Critical Natural Habitats and Aquatic Biodiversitv. A conclusive assessment 
of project's incremental impacts on critical natural habitats and on the aquatic biodiversity of La 
Plata River is difficult due to the complexity of the project context and because detailed 
information on the status of regional bio-diversity is incomplete. The disposal of wastewater in 
the Rio de la Plata will have some adverse impacts on fish and other aquatic biodiversity in the 
immediate disposal zone surrounding the outfall diffusers. In the immediate vicinity of the 
diffusers the likely implications for aquatic life are likely to be negative as a result of direct 
impacts leading to lower dissolved oxygen levels, increased turbidity and settling of sediments. 
Under current project design, these risks are largely unavoidable. 
52. However, determining whether these risks are significant or not is difficult because the 
region is already under significant pre-existing pressure from contamination, shipping, and 
ongoing channel dredging. According to FREPLATA -- a project sponsored by GEF and jointly 
implemented by Argentina and Uruguay to assess and evaluate the principal threats to 
biodiversity in the Fluvial Zone of La Plata River and its Shoreline (Zona Fluvial del Rio de la 
Plata y su Frente Maritimo (RPFM)) -- the entire Rio de la Plata region is already under 
immediate pressures from a range of issues including over-fishing; incidental fisheries catch; 
cumulative pollution loads along the entire length of the river; introduction of exotic species; and 
harmful algal blooms. It is important to bear in mind that, while portions of the Zona Fluvial are 
considered important biologically, there are no existing aquatic biodiversity conservation areas 
currently declared in the project's area of impact. 
130 
<<PAGE 140>>
53. FREPLATA had identified several major priority threats which are currently affecting the 
RPFM and it is important to consider how the proposed project may further contribute to or 
exacerbate existing threats. For example, among the most important issues affecting regional 
biodiversity identified by FEPLATA are the following: (i) incidental fisheries capture; (ii) 
invasive species; (iii) lack of tools for conservation of threatened species including the absence 
of a system of aquatic protected areas; and (iv) harmful algal blooms. Understanding these issues 
in a complex environment is hampered by lack of information on key considerations. 
54. Considering these high priority issues it is clear that the project would not contribute to 
problems of over-exploitation of the fisheries resource through promotion of harmful fishing 
techniques and practices. Furthermore the project would not contribute to the problems 
associated with introduction of exotic or invasive species. Since there are no declared aquatic 
protected areas the project would have no impact on such areas. Two areas where the project 
could have impacts are in the area of incremental contributions to water quality decline and the 
problems associated with harmful algal blooms. 
55. Potential for Harmful Algal Blooms (HAB). HAB are events which occur periodically as 
a result of the rapid multiplication of micro-algae in the Rio de la Plata. Not all algal blooms are 
harmful to human or ecological health but some may be depending on the species involved. 
According to FREPLATA, the incidence of these HAB has grown in recent decades and some 
have produced impacts such as fish mortality and alterations to the food chain. Human health 
impacts from such HAB can generate allergies or reactions of varying degrees of intensity. 
56. The large inputs of nutrients from discharged wastewater may increase the risks of HAB 
but the timing, intensity and impact of such blooms will be very difficult to predict with any 
degree of accuracy. Also, the extent to which the nutrient loads from the project will be 
responsible for such blooms will be difficult to ascertain in practice. At present, it is very 
difficult to reduce or suppress these outbreaks when they occur. The EMP recommends that 
monitoring programs and early warning mechanisms would need to be set up to monitor 
outbreaks and to inform the public of appropriate measures to avoid contact and reduce the risks 
of contact 
57. Nuisance impacts associated with treatment plant and pumping station Operations. The 
Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant may generate odor and noise problems. Fortunately, the 
plant is located in an area well removed from residential neighborhoods, so potential negative 
impacts will be limited. Also, modern design takes care of noise and odor problems through the 
use of adequate technologies such as collection and treatment of odor generating gases, isolation 
of noise-generating equipment. The pumping stations that will be constructed under the project 
are located in the treatment plant sites, and odor and noise problems will be taken care of as part 
of managing the treatment plants. 
58. Impacts Associated with disposal of solids generated in the treatment plants. The total 
quantity of solids of various types (grit and sand, oil and grease, solid residues) that will be 
generated by both treatment plants (Riachuelo and Berazategui) during the initial years of 
operation will oscillate in the range of 340-500 tons per day. These considerable quantities will 
require a sludge management plan to guide overall operations. Trucks will remove these solids 
and deposit them in the sanitary landfill of the State Company for the Ecological Coordination of 
131 
<<PAGE 141>>
the Metropolitan Area (Coordinacibn Ecolbgica Area Metropolitana Sociedad del Estado, or 
CEAMSE). Handling and transporting such quantities of solids present environmental risks. 
AySA has experience in handling the solids generated in its wastewater treatment plants in a 
similar manner. 
59. The project will take precautions to handle the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant 
solids according to acceptable technical standards and as defined in the EMP. Installations for 
recovery of sand for reuse and generation of energy from the oil are included in the project, but 
investigations are required before finalizing specific actions. The impact of such installations 
will be assessed after decisions are taken. 
F. Impacts Associated with component 2 (Industrial Pollution Abatement) 
60. This component is designed to pilot investments that help reduce industrial wastewater 
discharges (organic as well as toxic effluents) that directly enter the M-R River, and over the 
next 15-20 years will contribute to the Government’s PISA objective of overall environmental 
recovery of the river. As part of the Government program, ACUMAR will first focus on the 50 
main industrial polluters contributing approximately 95 percent of the total industrial organic 
load (approximately 70 percent of the total organic load) discharged to the M-R River and the 
170 industries with the highest toxic load that currently discharge to the M-R River. The 
component will finance technical assistance to improve monitoring, control, and enforcement by 
the M-R River Basin Authority and will help in collecting baseline data and carrying out 
diagnostics. Additionally, APL- 1 will include the initial phase of a matching grants program to 
support private sector investments in pollution control and cleaner production processes, 
targeting SMEs with weaker financial and technical capacity to comply with environmental 
regulations. 
6 1. The environmental impacts associated with investments to be supported under 
Component 2 will be mostly positive, with very few having some potential for adverse impacts. 
The initiatives contemplated under sub-component 2 of this component are intended to limit and 
control industrial discharges from a number of industries into the M-R River Basin and improve 
environmental compliance and, as a result, are expected have significant environmental and 
human health benefits. Building on the TA activities that will help identify, develop and sign 
PRIs with polluting industries in accordance with established environmental standards and 
permissible limits for discharge into the AySA sewage network, a matching grants program will 
be initiated to provide support to micro, small, and medium enterprises in the M-R River Basin 
area for investments in cleaner production processes These cleaner production investments will 
increase the efficiency of the plants and processes, minimize the use of resources, eliminate toxic 
inputs, and reduce the quantity and toxicity of effluents and emissions. 
62. What is not known at this time is whether any part of this sub-component would involve 
some physical interventions requiring environmental management since the exact scope of work 
and details such as sub-project location have yet to be determined. Furthermore, it is not known 
whether the implementation of the PRIs would reduce employment at the firms, or if the 
potential closure or relocation of industries would result in reductions in local taxes. 
Consequently, it is not possible at this stage to assess full environmental and social impacts. 
132 
<<PAGE 142>>
63. The project has developed an Environmental Management Framework (EMF) which, 
among other things, describes the applicable institutional and legal framework, the types of 
eligible expenditures, and the overall screening mechanisms for sub-project activities. The EMF 
will be used to assess possible environmental impacts and to guide the implementation of this 
component, including an environmental management of activities with adverse impacts, which 
will be carried out once decisions are taken on respective works. A Social Management Plan is 
being developed in order to avoid, minimize or mitigate social adverse impacts. 
G. Impacts Associated with component 3 (Environmental Territorial Management) 
64. This component’s main objective is to improve public policies on environmental 
territorial management within the M-R River Basin. Towards this end, the component seeks to 
improve territorial planning and develop pilots of integrated investments in basic infrastructure 
services and flood control to improve urban living conditions in selected low-income areas of the 
basin. 
65. Environmental impacts of Component 3 are expected to be mostly positive, but there is 
potential for some adverse impacts associated with the construction of civil works for local flood 
control and drainage. These works are intended to resolve existing environmental problems 
through investment in improved infrastructure. Any impacts associated with these works are 
expected to occur during the construction phase. 
66. Typical impacts associated with construction such as dust and noise control, waste 
management, construction site management are to be expected but these impacts are expected to 
be localized and manageable using known mitigation measures. Most of the works contemplated 
are of a small scale nature. An environmental management framework (EMF) has been 
developed to guide the implementation of this component. The EMF describes the measures to 
be taken with respect to environmental screening of proposed works, definition of eligible works, 
mechanisms for triggering additional assessment of proposed activities where necessary. 
67. Social adverse impacts as a result of involuntary displacement are not foreseen but a 
Resettlement Policy Framework has been developed in case any actions require the application 
of OP 4.12. (See Section K, below) 
H. Potential Regional and Cumulative Impacts 
68. As mentioned previously, the impacts of the project are expected to contribute to 
significant improvements in the water quality and quality of life for those living in Matanza- 
Riachuelo basin and along the coastal areas of the Rio de la Plata. Adverse impacts will be 
associated with incremental degradation of the water quality in a limited part of La Plata River. 
69. The water quality simulation modeling undertaken to predict the project’s impact on La 
Plata River were based on wastewater flow values forecast for the next 20 years. The simulation 
assumes the full implementation of the infrastructure works under APL-1 and APL-2 and other 
works planned in the PISA and in AySA’s Master Plan such as the Berazategui preliminary 
treatment plant and the full expansion of secondary networks. In addition, the simulations 
included the combined impacts of both outfalls, the impacts of the different project stages (one 
complete outfall and the current Berazategui outfall), as well as the available baseline data on 
133 
<<PAGE 143>>
water quality which reflects the combined effects of development in the project’s area of 
influence. In this respect, the assessment carried out takes into account potential cumulative 
aspects. 
I. Stakeholder Consultation and Information Disclosure. 
70. As part of project preparation, extensive stakeholder consultations were carried out using 
formal as well as informal methodologies. Information on the project was made available to the 
public through a series of public disclosure events and workshops. Informal focus groups 
discussions were conducted with nongovernmental organizations (NGOs), local government, 
affected people, and beneficiaries at various points during preparation and appraisal. The project 
EIAs were posted on the ACUMAR and AySA web sites and made available through the Bank’s 
Infoshop. Other stakeholder consultation events are planned to disseminate information on the 
project during project implementation as part of the Communications Strategy of the project. The 
EIA and Social Assessment solicited stakeholder concerns and ensured an open process of 
debate and discussion about project objectives and goals. 
7 1. ACUMAR and AySA sponsored a public consultation event in Buenos Aires on July 10, 
2008 to present and discuss the detailed scope of work and terms of reference developed for the 
EIAs proposed for the project. This scoping workshop was attended by 140 participants 
representing a wide range of public and private stakeholders, including the Secretariat of the 
Environment and Sustainable Development (Secretaria de Ambiente y Desurrollo Sustentuble, or 
SAyDS), ACUMAR, Ay SA, the Province of Buenos Aires, municipal governments, national and 
local environmental NGOs, national government agencies, community organizations, and 
professional associations. The terms of reference and proposed scope of work for the EIAs were 
discussed and agreed on by the workshop participants. This formal workshop built on informal 
dialogue and discussion with various partners and stakeholders including ACUMAR, Ay SA the 
Bank, technical specialists, and local government. 
72. During the workshop many questions were raised by participants and answered by 
ACUMAR and AySA. Written comments received after the workshop were taken into account 
during project preparation. Important questions were raised relating to the project alternatives, 
specifically the level of wastewater treatment proposed, and the overall strategy for the basin, 
including concerns about the relative emphasis on industrial versus domestic wastewater 
controls. 
73. A second workshop was conducted on November 7, 2008 to present the advanced drafts 
of the EIAs and to solicit stakeholder feedback on the findings, issues, and recommendations of 
the reports. During this workshop a number of issues were raised relating to modeling results on 
water quality, the technical scope of the project internal review and clearance procedures, and the 
importance of continued communications with stakeholders. 
74. Additional consultations and project information dissemination were carried out as part 
of the preparation of the economic evaluation studies. A survey of more than a 1000 households 
and a large number of community meetings were held to collect information on a range of 
project design issues, including willingness to pay and perceptions and valuation of expected 
benefits. During this study, important project information on the project design, expected 
134 
<<PAGE 144>>
benefits, and potential risks was shared with the sample populations selected for the survey. This 
process was a tool for information disclosure, dissemination, and stakeholder feedback. 
75. As part of the outreach and disclosure program, two separate consulting teams conducted 
social assessment studies. AySA contracted a consulting team for a social assessment of 
Component 1 works, and a second team was retained to carry out a social assessment of the 
overall proposed project. As part of the preparation of these studies, the two consulting teams 
held a series of interviews and meetings with stakeholders including community organizations, 
industry representatives, and municipal governments. The main objective of these studies was to 
independently inform stakeholders and affected people about the project and collect feedback. 
76. In addition to the project-specific consultation and disclosure efforts, both AySA and 
ACUMAR have social outreach units within their organizational structures. These teams have 
been established to liaise continuously with the local communities and facilitate communication 
tools. ACUMAR, for example, established a network of approximately 300 community and 
environmental NGOs as part of its broader communications strategy on the issues facing the M- 
R River Basin. This network provides an opportunity for information disclosure, not only on the 
project but also on a wide array of development issues facing the basin. AySA business practice 
for planning of civil works includes disclosure of information and stakeholder consultations 
which will be applied to the civil works in APL-1 which are at or before pre-feasibility stage 
(e.g., right bank collector). 
77. A Communication Strategy (see Annex 11D) has been designed with ACUMAR, in order 
to inform key actors and stakeholder groups in the basin about the project in a transparent 
manner, strengthen the credibility of and support for the project, and to contribute to the overall 
consultation, participation and empowerment framework for the project. The Communication 
Program includes the creation of a Communication Unit in ACUMAR, which would be 
responsible for (i) providing adequate and timely information prior to consultation events; (ii) 
maintaining documentation on consultation processes and decisions taken; and (iii) providing 
feedback to concerned stakeholders. Staff in the new Unit will coordinate with those in AySA’s 
Communication Unit. 
78. In addition to general public disclosure, feedback on the project design was solicited from 
all international experts (including the four that reviewed the EIA, and the three technical 
experts). These high-level experts provided technical reviews in the fields of sanitation, 
engineering, and the environment were given an opportunity to comment and give advice. 
79. Additional consultations and information-sharing workshops are planned as part of the 
project communication strategy and will be conducted at different project stages, including 
during project implementation. 
J. Environmental Management Plan (EMP) 
80. Ke-y Elements ofthe EMP. As part of the EIA process, ACUMAR and AySA have 
developed an EMP that includes measures to avoid, minimize, mitigate, and monitor potential 
project impacts during construction and operational phases of the project. This EMP will be 
further strengthened during project implementation as described in paragraph 89. 
135 
<<PAGE 145>>
81. The core programs of the EMP include: (i) Management of impacts during the 
construction phase; (ii) Management of impacts during the operational phase; (iii) Management 
of impacts associated with Component 2; (iv) Environmental monitoring in the project’s area of 
influence; (v) Contingency planning and management; (vi) Consultation, communications and 
disclosure; and (vii) Capacity building, training and research. 
82. Overall Institutional Responsibilities. ACUMAR will be responsible for overall project 
adherence to required environmental measures under all components of the project, with 
particular emphasis on Components 2 and 3. ACUMAR will assign specific qualified staff 
dedicated to the management and supervision of all aspects of the EMP during project 
implementation. ACUMAR will also be responsible for ensuring periodic audits and supervision 
of environmental performance and they will take the lead in coordinating information disclosure 
and consultations. 
83. AySA will have specific institutional responsibilities to ensure that all construction 
contracts for works carried out under Component 1 include explicit requirements to ensure 
minimization and avoidance of all construction-related impacts. The costs associated with 
meeting these requirements will be included in such contracts. Through its existing 
environmental unit, AySA will ensure that competent core staff will be available at all times to 
supervise the implementation of environmental management measures directly under its control 
and to ensure compliance of contractual obligations of contractors. AySA will also have the 
responsibility to ensure that future operations of facilities under Component 1 adhere to the 
agreed EMP. 
84. Construction contractors will be required to follow site-specific EMP that describe 
various measures to follow at each site. Each contractor will be required to assign an 
environmental engineer or other suitably qualified staff to oversee compliance with the site- 
specific plans on a day-to-day basis. 
85. Monitoring. The monitoring program will be implemented to track indicators relating to 
the medium to long term needs of the basin thus ensuring sustainability after project completion. 
Environmental monitoring will be required for all three components during both the construction 
and operational phases with associated costs incorporated in the relevant contracts. Ay SA will 
have the primary responsibility for establishing baselines and monitoring specific works and 
facilities operations under Component 1 both during construction and operation of facilities. 
ACUMAR will be responsible for establishing baseline and monitoring programs associated with 
Components 2 and 3. ACUMAR will also establish a regional environmental trends monitoring 
program at the level of the project area of influence. These monitoring programs will be mutually 
supportive, and a large degree of coordination and cooperation is expected between project’s 
implementing authorities and the various stakeholders. The water flow, quality and sediment 
monitoring program will be financed by the proposed loan under component 4. 
86. Disclosure of Information. A high degree of public interest is expected in the progress of 
the project and the results of monitoring activities, especially during project operations. 
ACUMAR and AySA will work together to ensure close collaboration related to public 
disclosure of monitoring reports and data throughout the life of the project. ACUMAR will 
136 
<<PAGE 146>>
establish a public web site where periodic status reports, monitoring data, and information will 
be available. Stakeholders will also be able to obtain information through the environment units 
of both AySA and ACUMAR. 
87. Information will be made available to local government and local stakeholders through a 
comprehensive communications and outreach strategy, which will provide regular project 
updates and organize media events and other information campaigns at the local level. Both 
ACUMAR and AySA will establish communications links with concerned stakeholders 
including local government and NGOs working in the project area. 
88. Institutional Capacitv Building The institutional capacity within AySA and ACUMAR 
to implement an EMP for a project of this type can be considered limited. AySA has a small 
Environment Unit with some experience in overseeing operations but lack sufficient technical 
skills to oversee a complex project of the scale envisaged under the proposed project. For this 
reason, AySA tends to outsource technical studies. At this time ACUMAR has no equivalent unit 
and in many respects will be executing the operational aspects of this project for the first time. 
89. Because some of these activities will be new to both ACUMAR and AySA, the project 
will support specific activities to enhance their capacity to plan and execute the monitoring and 
information disclosure aspects of the project. The project will support specific training to 
enhance organization, staffing, and other resources to assist in building core capabilities in 
monitoring, communications, and outreach, as well as workshops, seminars and public 
information events. EMP activities, scope of work, institutional responsibilities and further 
details are available in the project file and in the EIA reports prepared by AySA and ACUMAR. 
K. Social Safeguards 
90. Social analysis. With regard to the social impact of the project, results of economic and 
social analysis studies indicate that the project will have a positive impact on the population in 
this basin and in the Buenos Aires metropolitan area. Preliminary results of a recent study that 
interviewed nearly 1,000 households about their perception of the current status of the M-R and 
La Plata rivers indicate that the project will help solve problems identified by the population. 
According to this study, ". . . the pollution of both rivers affects people mainly because of damage 
to the environment and risks of disease and because space for recreation is lost". For a detailed 
description on social impacts assessment see Annex 1 1 C. 
91. Because the project will contribute to restoring the condition of the M-R River Basin, 
improving the environmental situation will have a positive impact on the health of the affected 
population and on spatial use. Several stakeholders-groups that use the river for recreation 
(yachting), artisanal fishermen, La Plata River navigation authorities (because of potential 
interference with navigation), and municipalities downstream of Buenos Aires-may perceive 
that they are negatively affected by the project. In reality, all these stakeholders will benefit from 
the project's improvement of the quality of the water in La Plata River along the shoreline. The 
communication strategy that is being developed will include actions to convey this message to 
the referenced stakeholders. 
137 
<<PAGE 147>>
92. Involuntary Resettlement. In addition, no resettlement of population is foreseen under 
Component 1 of the project. Selected pipe routes are located in public areas and will not affect 
private property. During construction, access to some small extensions of public areas may be 
temporarily restricted, but the trenchless methods to be used for pipe laying will minimize such 
restrictions. Maps, satellite photos, and draft designs suggest that no resettlement will be needed. 
As the physical interventions of Components 2 and 3 of the project have not been determined, a 
Resettlement Policy Framework was prepared for implementation if and when warranted. 
L. Compliance with World Bank Safeguard Policies 
93. The proposed project is categorized as an Environmental risk category “A” based on the 
expected long-term impacts of the large-scale sanitation civil works investments under 
Component 1. Works to be carried out under Components 2 and 3 are expected to have impacts 
that are mostly positive and where adverse impacts may occur they are expected to be small- 
scale, temporary, local in nature, and can be addressed with known mitigation measures.

103. Natural Habitats (OP/BP 4.04). Within the project’s area of influence, there are two 
known terrestrial ecological reserves and no existing aquatic reserves. Based on studies 
conducted by the GEF-funded FREPLATA project, priority areas are proposed for the future for 
protection of aquatic biodiversity have been identified but these areas are far from the immediate 
impact zone and not likely to be affected by the project. 
104. Because the project aims to contribute to the significant improvement of water quality 
conditions in the M-R River Basin and in the coastal areas of the La Plata River, it is expected to 
have significant positive impacts for the natural riverine habitats found in the project area. An 
area of the receiving waters in La Plata River will be degraded as a result of the pollution 
discharge through the outfalls. Models developed for the outfalls indicate that the discharge 
plume is expected to be fully contained within a relatively small area of the receiving waters. 
The pollution is not expected to affect downstream coastal areas designated as conservation 
areas. 
105. Pest Management (OP 4.09). The project will not procure nor will it lead to increased use 
of pesticides. Therefore no action is required under this policy. 
106. Forests (OP 4.36). There are no forests affected by the project and there are no activities 
proposed which would promote improved forest management. Therefore no action is required 
under this policy. 
140 
<<PAGE 150>>
107. Safety of Dams (OP 4.37). The project does not finance the construction or rehabilitation of 
dams therefore no action is required under the policy. 
108. Physical Cultural Resources (OP 4.11). Preliminary site evaluations for proposed civil 
works construction do not show any evidence of cultural property that will require mitigation or 
management measures. However, since many of the proposed works are underground or will 
require some excavation, all construction contracts will include a "chance finds" clause that will 
require contractors to halt construction if any underground cultural property sites are encountered 
during construction. Site specific management plans for any cultural property identified by 
chance finds procedures will be developed before construction can start up again. 
109. Involuntary Resettlement (OP 4.12). Because the vast majority of construction will take 
place underground or on sites that do not require land acquisition or resettlement, the project 
expects minimal need for resettlement. However, given the complexity of works, and since the 
physical interventions of components 2 and 3 of the project have not yet been fully determined, a 
Resettlement Policy Framework has been prepared for the project as a precaution. Among other 
things, the framework spells out the eligibility criteria, forms of compensation, and dispute 
resolution mechanisms that will be available to potentially affected people. 
110. Indigenous People (UP 4.10). There are no indigenous people affected by this project as 
defined under the World Bank's policy. Therefore, no action is required under this policy.

2009, Argentine representatives gave a presentation of the project before CARP and answered

written response from CARP that did not indicate any objection to the project.

Annex 11A: Analysis of Alternatives 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Sustainable Development Project: 
A. Conceptual Framework for the Development of Alternatives 
1. The original concept for managing the wastewater of the City of Buenos Aires consisted 
of intercepting all its wastewater, conveying it to the Berazategui District to the southeast, 
treating it at that point, and discharging the effluent to the La Plata River through a sub-aquatic 
outfall. On the basis of this plan, three main large-diameter sewers (cloacas rndxirnas) were 
constructed to convey an average flow of approximately 18 m3/second of wastewater to the 
existing outfall located in Berazategui. The Berazategui outfall, 3.8 m in diameter and 2.5 km 
long, discharges the wastewater, without prior treatment, to La Plata River. The plan to construct 
a fourth main sewer to the Wilde pumping station, a treatment plant in Berazategui, and a longer 
outfall at this site with capacity to discharge the entire wastewater flow of the Buenos Aires 
metropolitan area, was never completed. 
2. Based on the original concept for managing the metropolitan area’s wastewater and the 
fact that the existing main conveyance of wastewater toward Berazategui forms only part of the 
system originally planned, it is necessary to seek solutions for the environmental problems of the 
Matanza-Riachuelo (M-R) Basin that are compatible with the existing system. This means 
treating and disposing of the wastewater in the Argentina Water and Sanitation (Agua y 
Saneamientos, S.A., or AySA) concession area (Le., the entire City of Buenos Aires and most of 
the metropolitan area) along with that of the entire basin. The raw wastewater from the northern 
part of the city and nearby districts contributes to and influences the wastewater of the basin even 
though these parts are not part of it geographically. 
3. To comply with its commitment to expand the coverage of sanitation services in its 
concession area, AySA has developed a five year investment plan (Plan Director). AySA’s 
concession area does not cover the entire M-R River Basin, but covers most of the basin’s 
urbanized area and other areas of the city north of the basin. The AySA Management Plan 
modified the original concept of conveying all the wastewater of the concession area to 
Berazategui, but it retains the principle of intercepting the rest of the wastewater in the 
concession area and providing adequate disposal for the entire wastewater flow. 
4. The AySA Management Plan does not provide for the evacuation of all wastewater from 
the basin to La Plata River outfalls. Instead, the wastewater of part of the middle and upper basin 
located in the concession area would be diverted to various inland wastewater treatment plants. 
Some of these plants already exist and need to be expanded (Sudoeste and Jaguel), while new 
plants will be built at Laferrere and Fiorito. According to AySA’s Management Plan, their 
effluents will discharge to the Matanza-Riachuelo River. 
5. In addition to domestic wastewater, the basin has a large amount of liquid industrial 
waste, most of which is currently not treated. Part of the waste is discharged directly to the M-R 
River and its tributaries, and part into AySA’s existing sewerage network. Because the AySA 
Management Plan does not include a comprehensive solution for the basin’s sanitation problem, 
a broader program is required. 
145 
<<PAGE 155>>
6. The magnitude of the difference between the AySA Management Plan and the basin’s 
needs can be illustrated by the fact that the estimated future flow of wastewater in the entire M-R 
River Basin, which according to the AySA Management Plan will not reach the outfall systems 
of La Plata River, is approximately 13.5 m3/second. All previously mentioned flows already 
include the contributions from industrial liquid wastes. 
7. Because the goal of the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project is to 
achieve the comprehensive sustainable development of the entire Matanza-Riachuelo Basin, not 
only to expand sanitation in part of the basin, alternatives were sought to the solution proposed in 
the AySA Management Plan. The alternatives that were analyzed cover the entire basin area and 
take into account the impact of industrial waste as well as domestic wastewater. 
B. Alternatives Studied 
8. Sanitation alternative, To meet the requirements of improving the quality of the 
watercourses in the M-R River Basin and achieving sustainable development in the basin, a 
series of viable project alternatives were developed. Each aimed to avoid environmental damage 
such as contamination of the potable water intakes in La Plata River (the Palermo intake for the 
San Martin plant and the Bernal intake for the Belgrano plant) and support the environmental 
recovery of the basin without causing other negative impacts on the receiving water bodies. The 
following alternatives were developed for analysis: 
(i) Decentralized treatment by various plants within the basin with different levels of 
treatment, discharging their effluents to the M-R River, its tributaries, and La Plata 
River. 
(ii) Centralized treatment with a single treatment plant in Berazategui and a sub-aquatic 
outfall at this site discharging the effluent to La Plata River. This was the original 
alternative adopted for the sanitation of the City of Buenos Aires, the construction of 
which was initiated but never completed. 
(iii) Centralized treatment with various points of discharge into La Plata River through sub- 
aquatic outfalls. Although a solution with several discharge points is theoretically 
feasible, only two treatment sites-one in Berazategui and one in Dock Sud, have been 
approved, and it would be difficult to find additional treatment sites for such large-scale 
plants on the banks of La Plata River. An analysis of the coastal land strip within the 
City of Buenos Aires and between the mouth of the M-R River and current Berazategui 
outfall, taking into account the location of the Bernal water intake for the Belgrano 
potable water purification plant, the recreational area on the Quilmes coast, and the State 
Company for the Ecological Coordination of the Metropolitan Area (Coordinacibn 
Ecolbgica Area Metropolitana Sociedad del Estado, or CEAMSE) sanitary landfill 
sector in Villa Dominico, showed that there are no other sites for a wastewater treatment 
plant with areas large enough for treatment installations. 
(iv) Alternatives based on different levels of treatment before discharge to La Plata River. 
These alternatives involve determining the lengths of the outfalls as a function of the 
treatment levels, as higher levels of treatment may require shorter outfalls. The outfall 
lengths were determined using a simulation model for the water quality in La Plata 
River. The development of these alternatives took into account their impact on the uses 
146 
<<PAGE 156>>
of the river water (e.g., source of water supply for human consumption with 
conventional treatment, recreational uses with and without direct contact, preservation of 
aquatic life, and impact on navigation), as well as the environmental and social impact 
of the operation of different treatment systems (e.g., odor control, amount of sludge 
generated, truck traffic for sludge removal, and necessary space for the plants). 
(v) Secondary treatment followed by chlorination of the effluent and discharge of the 
effluent to La Plata River, coupled with using a short outfall. 
9. Main conveyance system alternatives. The following alternatives were defined for the 
main wastewater collection and conveyance system in conjunction with the treatment and 
disposal alternatives in order to ensure the collection of the wastewater of the entire M-R River 
Basin and AySA concession area. 
(i) A separate left bank collector and right bank collector with capacity to convey all 
wastewater from the middle and upper basin of the M-R River to the Riachuelo 
pretreatment plant; 
(ii) A single left bank collector with capacity to also convey all wastewater from the middle 
and upper basin of the M-R River toward the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant. 
10. 
for reducing industrial pollution: 
Industrial pollution abatement alternatives. The following alternatives were considered 
(i) High-level treatment of all industrial wastes and discharge of all effluents except those 
containing toxic matter into the M-R River; 
(ii) Lower-level treatment of all industrial wastes and discharge of all effluents except those 
containing toxic matter into the municipal sewerage network for further processing with 
domestic wastewater. 
1 1. Comparison of alternatives. The information developed for each of the alternatives was 
compared using technical, economic, environmental, and social criteria. As a result of this 
comparison, the most favorable alternative was selected and its stages of execution determined. 
12. On the basis of water sampling at various points along the M-R River and its tributaries 
and along the coastal strip of La Plata River and an analysis of the water quality parameters of 
the samples drawn, mathematical models were developed to simulate the water quality of these 
rivers using the water quality data to calibrate the models. Mathematical models simulate the 
processes of transport, dilution, and transformation of pollutants in a receiving water body 
system. From a practical standpoint, the mathematical model has been used as a virtual 
laboratory that makes it possible to evaluate the receiving water body system’s response 
(distributions of the concentrations of pollutants in the water body) to discharges of pollutants. 
13. The mathematical models developed were an important instrument for technical analysis 
of the alternatives studied, Le., as assessing the current state of contamination of the water of the 
M-R and La Plata Rivers, estimating the levels of contamination that will result from applying 
each alternative, and assessing the project’s environmental impact after the favorable alternative 
is implemented. 
147 
<<PAGE 157>>
14. The most suitable alternative found was Alternative 3: distributing the total collected 
wastewater flow to sub-aquatic outfalls in Berazategui and in Dock Sud near the confluence of 
the M-R River with La Plata River. This alternative offers a combination of lower cost, technical 
advantages, simple operation and maintenance, more positive environmental impacts, fewer 
negative impacts, and minimum negative social impacts. 
15. 
(0 
(ii) 
(iii) 
The other proposed solutions were rejected for the following reasons: 
Alternative 1 (decentralized treatment with a variety of treatment plants with different 
levels of treatment that discharge their treated effluents into the M-R River, its 
tributaries, and La Plata River) was rejected for technical reasons. The modeling results 
showed that the M-R River has a low capacity to receive large amounts of organic loads. 
That means that the river would remain anoxic even if treated effluent were discharged 
into it. During the early stage of APL-1, AySA will continue to study technical 
alternatives that might involve retaining some inland treatment plants in the basin while 
offsetting their discharges with additional oxygenation of the M-R River. Any design 
alternative that might eventually be presented, however, needs to be consistent with the 
goal of achieving at least minimal levels of dissolved oxygen even in the M-R River’s 
lower sections. 
Alternative 2 (centralized treatment with a single treatment plant and a single outfall in 
Berazategui) has more environmental and economic disadvantages than the alternative 
of two outfalls and is not acceptable for legal, social, and political reasons, as it 
discharges all the wastewater of the Buenos Aires metropolitan area into La Plata River 
in an area located in the jurisdiction of a downstream municipality. 
Alternative 4 (in fact, a series of alternatives of different levels of treatment prior to 
effluent discharge into La Plata River) involves establishing the lengths of the outfalls as 
a function of the level of treatment using the water quality simulation model developed 
for La Plata River. Simulation results demonstrated that treatment to a level higher than 
preliminary does not provide an advantage in terms of water quality. This finding is 
consistent with the recommendation of the World Health Organization (WHO) that 
treatment beyond preliminary treatment prior to discharge does not reduce the public 
health risk of human exposure to wastewater discharged to the sea through effective 
out fall^.'^ That is, the risk of preliminary treatment followed by an effective outfall is 
identical to the risk of secondary treatment followed by an effective outfall. The WHO 
report refers to risk related to human exposure through bathing, which is not exactly the 
risk in the case of discharge to La Plata River. However, because pathogenic organisms 
are the main risk from effluent discharge, which is also the case in the La Plata River 
system, the exposure risk referred to in the WHO report is a good proxy for the risk in 
La Plata River. Higher than preliminary treatment levels also imply enormous additional 
investment costs, as well as high operation and maintenance costs, which are not 
affordable at this time for Buenos Aires given the large population to be served and large 
wastewater flows to be treated. Plants with higher treatment levels also generate very 
74 WHO (2003): Guidelines for Safe Recreational Water Environments. Volume 12, Coastal and Fresh Waters, 
World Health Organization, Geneva, Switzerland, p. 80. Available at 
hm://www.who.int/water sanitation health/bathina/snveI/en/. 
148 
<<PAGE 158>>
large quantities of biological sludge. Thus, the combination of preliminary treatment 
plants followed by long and effective outfalls was the preferred solution for the project 
because it is scientifically solid and economically favorable. However, sufficient area 
was reserved at the sites for the Riachuelo and Berazategui preliminary treatment plants 
to expand the treatment plants and increase the level of treatment if required. 
(iv) Alternative 5 (secondary treatment followed by chlorination of the effluent and its 
discharge to La Plata River through a short outfall) is costlier than the one selected and 
environmentally very risky because of the need to handle enormous amounts of chlorine 
in close proximity to the city and the negative impact of the chlorine and its byproducts 
on aquatic life in the river. In this alternative, based on a short outfall, the effluent can be 
discharged into the river only if it is disinfected (Le,, chlorinated). Otherwise it will 
cause heavy pathogenic contamination along the riverbanks and threaten the water 
supply intakes and all other uses of La Plata River. Chlorination, however, is not reliable 
because the continuous provision of such large amounts of chlorine cannot be assured. 
Other disinfection methods are also impractical because of the huge effluent flow 
generated in Buenos Aires. Ozonation would be very expensive and is not practiced on 
such a large scale. Ultraviolet disinfection is also not practiced on such a large scale and 
would probably not be successfwl. The use of nanofiltration membranes to remove 
pathogens from the effluent would require increased investment for pretreatment and is 
an untested technology that is not yet recommended for such a large-scale project. 
16. Comparison of the alternatives for industrial pollution abatement resulted in rejection of 
Alternative 1 (high-level treatment of all industrial wastes and discharge of the effluents to the 
M-R River) because mathematical modeling showed that the discharge of industrial effluents to 
the M-R River would render the river anaerobic even after a high level of treatment. The only 
way to induce aerobic conditions in the river and recover its water quality is the adoption of 
Alternative 2 (a lower level of treatment of all industrial wastes and discharge of all effluents 
except those containing toxic matter into the municipal sewerage network for further processing 
with the domestic wastewater). This alternative was coupled with the alternative selected for the 
sanitation component in the sense that the simulation model confirmed that the organic matter 
loads, including those of industrial origin, that would be discharged to La Plata River through the 
two outfalls, can be absorbed by the river and will not generate water quality problems. 
Similarly, the conveyance and treatment system were dimensioned to include all the industrial 
waste flows generated in the M-R River Basin. 
17. The main wastewater collection system and the conveyance and distribution of the 
wastewater between the two preliminary treatment plants are an important part of the project. 
The following alternatives were studied for this system: 
The first consists of two main collectors along the banks of M-R River, one on each 
side (figure 12.1). The Left Bank collector is short and has the capacity to collect only 
the AySA concession area wastewater (domestic and industrial). The Left Bank 
collector is 12.5 km long and 0.8- 2.9 m in diameter, followed by the 8.5 km Baja 
Costanera collector with a 4.5 m diameter, which ends at the Riachuelo preliminary 
treatment plant site. The Right Bank collector is approximately 37 km long. The Right 
Bank collector starts at the upper part of the basin with a small diameter of 2.3 m and 
increases in diameter as it moves along the middle and lower parts of the basin and 
149 
<<PAGE 159>>
collects more and more wastewater. This collector conveys the wastewater generated in 
the middle and upper parts of the M-R River Basin, both domestic and industrial. 
Figure 12.1, Scheme of conveyance alternative 1 
(two main collectors along the banks of the M-R River 
(ii) The second alternative consists of only the Left Bank collector, which would also be 
used to convey the middle and upper basin wastewater flows on top of the flows 
generated in the AySA concession area (figure 12.2). This collector would increase in 
diameter, as mentioned above and be extended by approximately 27 km to reach the 
upper part of the basin. The pipe diameter along the 27 km section would vary between 
2.2 m in the upper basin and 3.5 m in the middle basin. In the section of the lower 
basin, the Left Bank collector is 12.5 km long and 0.8-2.9 m in diameter, followed by 
the 8.5 km Baja Costanera collector 4.5 m in diameter, which ends at the Riachuelo 
preliminary treatment plant site. A preliminary analysis of these two alternatives 
showed that the costs were similar, with similar environmental and social benefits. 
However, alternative 1 was selected, as it provided more flexibility to the GOA in terms 
of the design of future investments to expand coverage in the upper and middle basin.

<<PAGE 160>>
Fig. 12.2. Scheme of conveyance alternative 2 
(one main collector along the left bank of the M-R River) 
1 8. Selected industrial pollution abatement alternative. The selected alternative is therefore 
based on intercepting most of the domestic and industrial wastewater of the AySA concession 
area (except that diverted to the North and Hurlingham treatment plants, which discharge their 
effluents to the Reconquista River) and the municipalities in the upper Matanza-Riachuelo Basin 
and diverting this entire wastewater flow to La Plata River through two new sub-aquatic outfalls. 
The Riachuelo outfall will be located on the left bank of Sarandi Creek. It will be 3.8 m in 
internal diameter and 11.5 km long, of which the final 2.3 km are the diffuser zone. The new 
Berazategui outfall will have an internal diameter of 3.8 m and a length of 7.5 km, of which the 
final 3.5 km are the diffuser zone. The maximum design flows of each outfall are 25 m3/second 
at Riachuelo and 33.5 m3/second at Berazategui. 
19. Prior to its discharge through each outlet into La Plata River, the wastewater will be 
subjected to preliminary treatment in two plants, one in Dock Sud and the other in Berazategui 
near the inlet to the existing 2.5 km long outfall. The preliminary treatment plants consist of 
coarse screens to retain heavy material, followed by milliscreens with 6 mm openings to remove 
finer material (the milliscreens opening to the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant might be 
smaller than 6 mmj, followed by aerated grit chambers to remove sand and floating material such 
as oil and grease. 
20. Selected sanitation alternative. The selected sanitation alternative is also coupled with a 
conceptual alternative selected for managing the industrial wastewater generated in the basin. 
The selected industrial pollution abatement alternative consists of providing a relatively low 
151 
<<PAGE 161>>
level of treatment (meeting quality standards in effect) of all industrial wastes except those 
containing toxic mater and discharging the effluents to the municipal sewerage network for 
further processing with the domestic wastewater. Industries that discharge toxic wastes will have 
to provide full treatment at the source before discharging the effluents to the sewerage network. 
2 1. Selected conveyance alternative. The selected main conveyance alternative consists of a 
separate left bank collector and right bank collector with a combined capacity to convey all 
wastewater from the middle and upper basin of the M-R River to the two pretreatment plants. It 
includes the (i) Left Bank collector, with a total length of approximately 12 km, in the lower part 
of the basin (the diameter varies between 0.8 and 2.9 m), and the Right Bank collector, with a 
total length of about 37 km with an internal diameter ranging from 2.3 m to 3.5 m. Additional 
complementary works will include the (i) the Baja Costanera bypass collector with a length of 5 
km and a diameter of 4.5 m; (ii) the Baja Costanera expansion collector (Radio Antiguo section); 
and (iii) the Baja Costanera expansion collector (Capital Federal-Vicente Lopez section). 
22. The selected alternative allows connecting the entire population and all industries in the 
Matanza-Riachuelo Basin to the sewerage network, thereby eliminating most of the discharges to 
the M-R River. This will enable recovery of the water quality of this river and consequently 
contribute to the sustainable development of the M-R River Basin. 
23. As currently designed, APL-1 includes the Right Bank Collector (CMD), a key piece of 
sewerage infrastructure that would receive and transport wastewater collected in the coverage 
area of the Southwest, Jaguel, Fiorito and Laferrere treatment plants as well as wastewater 
generated in the municipalities of the upper M-R River Basin located outside the AySA 
concession area. Initial water quality modeling during project preparation has identified the 
withdrawal of these discharges to the M-R River as a key enabler of improved water quality. 
Given the size of the proposed investments (estimated at about US$275 million (without 
contingencies) based on preliminary designs), Ay SA will conduct a pre-feasibility study of 
alternatives to the CMD. AySA is considering, for example, whether side stream elevated pool 
aeration (SEPA) stations, being used in Chicago, Illinois, could be used -- possibly in 
conjunction with higher levels of treatment at existing and future treatment plants - to meet 
water quality objectives established by ACUMAR. Water quality modeling of various 
alternatives is already underway in conjunction with ACUMAR. Should a better alternative in 
terms of economic, environment and social aspects emerge, the latter would need to be submitted 
to SAyDS and ACUMAR for approval before it would be presented to the Bank for review. A 
covenant has been added to the Loan Agreement requiring that the study of alternatives to the 
CMD be completed by March 3 1 , 2010. Any alternative to construction of the CMD would need 
to be approved by the Bank and may need to be reflected in an amendment to the Loan 
Agreement. Given this uncertainty, the Bank and the Borrower initially included the CMD under 
APL-2. Subsequently, however, the Bank and the Borrower agreed to advance the CMD to APL- 
1 to provide the Government with an instrument for accelerated basin clean-up in the short and 
medium-term. Including the CMD in APL-1 also has the important advantage of concentrating 
all major trunk infrastructure works needed to remove contaminant flows from the M-R River 
Basin in a single phase over the next 6 years. On the other hand, construction of the CMD during 
APL-1 increases the size of the project’s first phase to nearly US$1.5 billion, meaning that the 
Bank’s US$840 million loan would, on average, finance only about 56% of APL-1 activities.

Appendix to Annex 11A 
Wastewater Treatment Prior to Disposal in the Sea through an Effective Submarine Outfall 
Tertiary plus disinfection 
Lagoons 
1. Approximately 50 percent of the world’s population lives in or near coastal areas. The 
oceans and seas are the bodies of water most likely to receive the sewage and treated effluents 
generated by this population. Clean disposal of sewage and its treatment prior to discharge are 
therefore issues of major importance. For developing countries, where resources for wastewater 
treatment and disposal are limited, low-cost solutions are critical. In October 2003, WHO 
published guidelines for recreational water quality protection. Table 12.1 presents the WHO 
findings on the relative health risks to human health of exposure to effluents discharged from 
submarine outfalls, classified according to various schemes combining sewage pretreatment and 
submarine outfall length. 
Table 1. Relative risk to human health of exposure to sewage through outfalls 
- - - 
High High Low 
Discharge type 
I Preliminary I Veryhigh I High I Low 
I Primary (including septic tanks) I Very high I High I Low 
I Secondary High 1 High I Low I I Secondary plus disinfection I - 1-1 - 
I Tertiary I Moderate I Moderate I Very low 
Source: WHO. 2003. Guidelines for Safe Recreational Water Environments. Volume 12, Coastal and Fresh 
Waters, Geneva, p. 80. Available at httv://www.who.int/water sanitation healthhathindsnve l/en/. 
2. The methods to discharge sewage or effluent into oceans can be classified into three 
principal types: (i) discharge directly onto the beach, (ii) “short” outfalls, where sewage or 
effluent is likely to contaminate recreational waters, and (iii) “effective” outfalls, designed so 
that the sewage or effluent is efficiently diluted and dispersed to ensure that it does not pollute 
recreational water areas. While the terms “short” and “long” are often used, outfall length is 
generally less important than proper location and effective diffusion. An effective outfall is 
assumed to be one that is properly designed, with sufficient length and diffuser discharge depth 
to ensure that the sewage does not reach recreational areas. 
3. According to WHO, if an effective outfall is provided to discharge effluent to the sea, the 
level of pretreatment of domestic sewage has practically no bearing on the risk to human health 
from the discharged effluent. The risk to human health of secondary effluent discharged through 
155 
<<PAGE 165>>
an effective outfall is classified as low. The risk of primary effluent discharged through an 
effective outfall, and even the risk of preliminary treated effluent discharged through an effective 
outfall, is classified as low. The WHO findings lead to a conclusion that if an effective outfall is 
provided, preliminary treatment is sufficient prior to discharge of domestic sewage to the sea. 
Conversely, if a short (ineffective) outfall is provided, even secondary treatment is not sufficient 
to reduce the high risk to human health of effluent discharged trough that type of outfall. These 
findings are important for developing countries as they imply that only preliminary treatment 
may be required prior to discharge of effluent into the sea through an effective outfall. 
Preliminary installations as pretreatment prior to discharge of effluents into the sea can save a lot 
of money in developing countries, whose financial resources are usually scarce. The investment 
costs, as well as the operation and maintenance costs of preliminary treatment, are approximately 
one-tenth of those for secondary treatment, while the use of preliminary treatment yields results 
comparable to those of secondary treatment. 
4. It can also be concluded from the WHO findings that stabilization lagoon effluents 
discharged into the sea directly on the beach or through a short outfall constitute a high risk to 
human health, and that this effluent needs to be discharged into the sea through an effective 
outfall to reduce this risk. This is an important conclusion because developing countries 
commonly treat wastewater in stabilization lagoons, and it is wrongly believed that effluents of 
such lagoons pose no risk to human health and can be discharged directly on the beach. 
5. 
originating from industrial wastewater. 
The conclusions above refer to typical municipal sewage that does not contain toxic wastes 
6. For coastal cities in developing countries, the strategy of domestic wastewater disposal by 
preliminary treatment followed by an effective submarine outfall is an affordable, effective, and 
reliable solution. It is simple to operate and free of negative health and environmental impacts. 
Many outfalls of this type are successfully functioning and have a proven track record in many 
coastal cities all over the world. This strategy should be coupled with a monitoring program 
beginning before and continuing after construction of the treatment installations and outfall to 
verify the performance of the outfall and help determine whether higher treatment levels are 
necessary. This is a logical strategy that would prevent unnecessary investments in costly 
treatment installations. 
7. A submarine outfall system, which includes the outfall itself and the near field zone, 
should in fact be considered a treatment plant. This treatment plant provides a high level of 
treatment, higher than what any conventional land-based plant can reach. Land-based plants can 
reach, in extreme cases, removal levels of biochemical oxygen demand (BOD) and total 
suspended solids (TSS) of up to 95 percent. If the effluent is not disinfected, they remove 50-80 
percent of pathogenic organisms, a very low removal level, leaving the effluent with practically 
the same level of risk to human health as that of raw sewage. On the other hand, a well-designed 
outfall system removes over 99 percent of all contaminants, including pathogenic organisms. 
Removal of 99 percent of pathogenic organisms might not be sufficient, but the mentioned value 
refers to physical dilution. Taking into account the biological decay of pathogenic organisms in 
the marine environment, a well-designed outfall can ensure their overall removal to levels that 
pose no risk to human health. Tables 2 and 3 show the average operating results of 5 years of 
measurements near the outfall discharge points of two submarine outfall systems in the 
156 
<<PAGE 166>>
Concepcion region of Chile, each consisting of preliminary treatment followed by an effective 
outfall.

Annex 11B: Background on ACUMAR and AySA 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project 
A. ACUMAR 
1. Law 26.168 was approved on November 15, 2006, creating ACUMAR as an 
interjurisdictional public agency. ACUMAR’s presidency is exercised by the Secretary of the 
Environment and Sustainable Development (Secretaria de Ambiente y Desarrollo Sustentable, or 
SAyDS) under the Office of the Chief of the Cabinet of Ministers. ACUMAR is a unique 
institution for the environmental management of the basin. 
2. Some of the triggers for the creation of the ACUMAR are listed below. 
(i) Ombudsman Report (2003), (Office of the National Auditor General or Auditoria 
General de la Nacidn) Report (2005); and 
(ii) Supreme Court lawsuit against national, provincial, and city of Buenos Aires 
governments and 44 industries, the Causa Mendoza (2004). 
3. While there are more than 200 regulations issued by different jurisdictions that co-exist 
side-by-side in the basin these do not add up to an integrated management focus on water 
resources, making it impossible in the past to achieve the objective of reversing the basin’s high 
levels of pollution. ACUMAR’s legal attributions are expected to contribute to a harmonized set 
of regulations and to integrated basin management that takes into account the M-R river’s very 
limited dilution capacity for organic pollutants. 
4. ACUMAR’s legal attributions include: 
- 
- 
- Promotion: implement plans. 
Regulatory functions: harmonize regulatory frameworks, parameters, etc. 
Control functions: set standards, monitor, audit, inspect, and take actions against those 
outside the norms; 
5. ACUMAR’s responsibilities may be grouped as follows: (i) Coordination: based on the 
principle of subsidiarity and pre-eminence over local authorities; (ii) Execution: Authority to 
adopt any measure necessary or relevant for the basin’s clean-up and execution of the budget; 
and (iii) Regulation and control: unify schemes dealing with liquid effluents and gas emissions; 
conduct environmental land use planning; create fees for services, issue organizational bylaws 
(for ACUMAR); establish a special administrative procedure; implement preventive measures; 
intervene by means of audits, monitoring, environmental impact assessments, and sanctions; 
issue warnings, writs, and inspections; and order closures, seizures, and cessation of activities. 
6. According to Article 2, ACUMAR’s Board of Directors is composed of eight members: 
the Federal Secretary of the Environment and Sustainable Development who acts as president, 
three other representatives of the National Government, two representatives from the Province of 
Buenos Aires, and two from the City of Buenos Aires. The Authority issues its own 
organizational and operational bylaws. Article 3 creates a Municipal Council (composed of one 
representative of each municipality in the basin), whose objective is to cooperate with, assist, and 
159 
<<PAGE 169>>
advise the entity. Article 4 creates a Social Participation Commission with the objective of 
communicating civil society organizations' comments and proposals to ACUMAR's 
management. This Commission has a consultative function, focused on promoting consultation 
and exchange with civil society organizations. Finally, Article 5 states that the Matanza- 
Riachuelo Basin Authority has functions including those of regulation, control, and promotion 
regarding industrial activities, provision of public services, and any other activities with an 
environmental impact on the basin. It may intervene administratively in the areas of prevention, 
sanitation, remediation, and the rational use of natural resources. ACUMAR' s current internal 
structure is illustrated in figure 13.1.

9. Resolution No. 1 , empowers ACUMAR to establish basin-specific regulations which 
replace existing norms for the control of liquid effluents. This implies that ACUMAR has unified 
regulations within the basin. In contrast to previous regulations that set specific standards, 
ACUMAR’s liquid effluent norms consider potential uses and quality objectives for the 
receiving water bodies. 
10. This is in line with the objectives of PISA, one of whose key targets is to recover and 
preserve the quality of surface water bodies in the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin and for La Plata 
River, in terms of uses and water quality objectives to be proposed, agreed, and set for the 
various sections and zones of the basin and river in the short, medium, and long term. 
11. The strategy contained in PISA consists of (i) proposing and reaching agreement on the 
uses and quality objectives for receiving bodies in the short, medium, and long term; (ii) 
evaluating their implications and correlation with AySA’s next five year investment plan (Plan 
Director) for raw sewage of domestic and industrial origin through the application of water 
quality models; and (iii) verifying the baseline of water bodies and implications of sanitation 
efforts and works through monitoring programs carried out in collaboration with of the National 
Water Institute (Instituto National de Agua, or INA), Naval Hydrology Service (Servicio de 
HidrograJia Naval, or SHN), and Dr. Raul A. Ringuelet Institute of Limnology (Instituto de 
Limnologi’a Dr. Rad1 A. Ringuelet, or ILPLA) in the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin and La Plata 
River; and (iv) assessing the implications of AySA’s Sanitation Management Plan on the basin’s 
aquifers based on the results of the study of hydrogeological conditions and groundwater quality 
in the basin being conduced in cooperation with the National University of La Plata (Universidad 
Nacional de La Plata, or UNLP). 
12. ACUMAR Resolution No. 1/2007 -- established to address control efforts based on 
common limits -- constitutes a transitional stage (Phase I) in a process of migration toward the 
new regulation (Phase II), based on a rational, coherent methodology with a comprehensive 
basin-level focus that considers the mass load of pollutants discharged to the receiving water 
body and its capacity to receive polluting substances in terms of the uses assigned to it. 
13. ACUMAR Resolution No. 1/2007 will not currently be applied to the system operated by 
AySA or to the evaluations of the implications of the AySA Sanitation Management Plan for raw 
sewage for water bodies in the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin and in La Plata River. What is 
applicable are the provisions contained in the AySA Regulatory Framework, in conjunction with 
161 
<<PAGE 171>>
the mass loads of polluting sources and the uses and quality objectives proposed for receiving 
bodies in the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin and in La Plata River. 
14. Thus, ACUMAR’s significant authority over environmental management of the Matanza- 
Riachuelo Basin has simplified the basin’s management, since ACUMAR encompasses national 
and provincial jurisdictions (Province of Buenos Aires and City of Buenos Aires) in a single 
Basin Authority. 
25. Institutional Strengthening. ACUMAR has a central role in the sustainable 
development of the M-R River Basin and in the implementation of the project. To ensure that 
ACUMAR is able to perform its tasks it needs to be strengthened. A series of activities is 
included in the project aimed at institutional strengthening of ACUMAR within a relatively short 
period of time (see description of component 4). 
16. Close coordination between the Social Participation Component, environmental staff and 
the proposed Communications Unit within ACUMAR will be key to ensure that engagement 
with key stakeholders throughout the implementation of the environmental management plan and 
consultations on social issues remain a two-way dialogue. To this end, terms of reference will be 
developed for the heads of key units emphasizing the need for coordinated action. In addition, 
ACUMAR (including its Social Participation, Industries and Environmental Territorial 
Management Components) will receive training on management of resettlement issues, including 
the ability to screen for impacts on livelihoods that go beyond physical resettlement and the 
ability to propose and assess alternatives that minimize any negative social impacts. A covenant 
. under the Loan Agreement has been included which reflects the Argentine government’s 
commitment to make available such national resources as are required to carry out any 
resettlement actions in accordance with Bank guidelines. The coordination between the units and 
staff within ACUMAR that will manage social, environmental and communications issues will 
be complemented by coordination between ACUMAR and AySA around the management of 
environmental and social issues related to the implementation of component 1. 
17. As part of component 4,, ACUMAR will undergo a 6-month participative planning 
exercise to identify weaknesses in its current organigram and to propose strengthening measures 
going forward. Loan proceeds under component 4 will allow ACUMAR to be strengthened with 
additional staff, equipment and the refurbishment/rental/constnction of new office space. 
B. AySA 
18. Water and sanitation services in Argentina are highly decentralized: over 1700 public and 
private service providers (public and private utilities, cooperatives, municipal and provincial 
entities) provide services in 23 provinces and the autonomous city of Buenos Aires. In 14 
provinces, regulatory entities nominally oversee these services, though in practice regulatory 
oversight has weakened considerably since the 2002 economic crisis. The Buenos Aires 
Metropolitan area represents a special case. The Government of Argentina (GOA) created AySA 
in March 2006 to provide water and wastewater services in this area75. At the same time, the 
’’ Decree No. 304/2006, subsequently ratified by Law No. 26.100/2006, created AySA to take over the service area 
previously served by Aguas Argentinas through its concession contract. Article No. 1 of this decree defines AySA’s 
service area as the Autonomous City of Buenos Aires and the municipalities of Almirante Brown, Avellaneda, 
162 
<<PAGE 172>>
GOA unilaterally terminated the concession contract with Aguas Argentinas S. A ., (AASA)76 for 
the area now served by AySA. Shortly thereafter, the Government established a new regulatory 
framework7’ for AySA’s service area which : 
(i) calls for a concession contract between AySA and the Government that expires on 
December 3 1 , 2020 (it can be extended beyond this date by mutual agreement) 
(ii) delegates the monitoring and supervision responsibility for the concession to the Sub- 
secretariat of Hydrological Resources (Subsecretaria de Recursos Hidricos, or SSRH), a 
dependency of the Ministry of Federal Planning, Public Investment, and Services 
(Ministerio de Planifzcacibn Federal, Inversibn Pziblica y Servicios, or MPFIPS), 
designating the SSRH as the enforcing authority (autoridad de apli~acibn).~~ 
(iii) creates the Regulatory Entity for Water and Sanitation (Entidad Reguladora de Agua y 
Suneamiento, or ERAS) and a Planning Agency (Agencia de PluniJcacidn, or APLA) 
for the concession area. 
19. The implementation of this regulatory framework is underway-ERAS and APLA have 
only recently begun functioning-but it is still incomplete. The concession contract has not yet 
been signed but is expected to be in place by loan effectiveness. AySA was created as a private 
company-with the Government as the controlling shareholder-to take over the concession of 
the service area held by Aguas Argentinas until April 2006. The SSRH, by delegation from 
MPFIPS, exercises authority both as majority owner and primary regulator of AySA. 
20. Ownership and governance ofAySA. As a private company with majority ownership by 
the Government, AySA is subject to controls mandated for rivate and government-owned 
entities. The government created AySA as a private companJ9 under the jurisdiction of the 
MPFIPS to provide water and wastewater services to approximately 9.5 million people living in 
the Federal Capital and 17 other municipalities in the metropolitan area of Buenos Aires. In 2006 
the Government capitalized AySA with an initial equity contribution of ARS150 million. In 
exchange for this equity contribution, the Government owns 130,000 of the company’s 150,000 
shares. Decree 304/2006 specifies that all of the Government’s shares are denominated as Class 
A shares and represent 90 percent of the company’s social capital. The other 20,000 shares, 
Ezeiza, Esteban Echeverria, La Matanza, Lanus, Lomas de Zamora, Moron, Quilmes, San Fernando, San Isidro, San 
Martin, Tres de Febrero, and Tigre y Vicente Lopez for drinking water and sewerage services; the municipality of 
Hurlingham e Ituzaing6 for drinking water services; and the municipalities of Berazategui and Florencio Varela for 
sewage effluent services. Said Decree also approved the articles of the corporation and bylaws of AySA. 
l6 Aguas Argentinas S.A. (AASA) was a company incorporated in Argentina. Shareholders were Suez, Vivendi 
Universal S.A., Sociedad General de Aguas de Barcelona S.A., AWG Group Ltd., and other minority shareholders 
including IFC. On April 28, 1993, Aguas Argentinas formally concluded a 30-year concession contract with the 
Government, and on May 1, 1993, took over the management of the designated water distribution and wastewater 
s stems. 
7y In October 2006, the Ministry of Federal Planning, Public Investment, and Services (Ministerio de Planificacidn 
Federal, Inversidn Pziblica y Servicios, or MPFIPS) signed a tripartite agreement with the Province of Buenos Aires 
and the Autonomous City of Buenos Aires regarding the provision of water and sewerage services by AySA. This 
tripartite agreement was approved by Congress in February 2007 through the enactment of Law No. 26.22 1, which 
approved the regulatory framework for AySA.

denominated as Class B shares and representing 10 percent of the company’s social capital, are

who had purchased shares in Aguas Argentinas. 
21. AySA’s Board of Directors consists of five members, four of whom are designated by the 
Government as the Class A shareholder and one of whom is designated by the Class B 
shareholders. Decree 304/2006 provides a key role for MPFIPS in the governance of AySA. 
According to this decree, MPFIPS is responsible, directly or through its representatives, for the 
following: 
(i) Exercising the Government’s representation of the Class A shares (Article 2); 
(ii) Designating the Board members on behalf of the Class A shares (Article 5);
