22. Decree 304/2006 also specifies a key role for the SSRH in exercising the majority 
shareholder’s oversight of AySA. Article 38 of AySA’s bylaws states that AySA will act in 
accordance with the plans, programs, and policies developed by the SSRH. This is in addition to 
SSRH’s designation as primary regulator, a dual authority which has potential to give rise to a 
conflict of interest. 
23. AySA ’s capital structure. When the company was created, no debt was transferred to the 
company, and its fixed assets were transferred to its balance sheet with a value of zero. Since no 
new loans have been contracted by AySA, at the end of 2007 the company had close to zero 
debt. Its fixed assets were equivalent to approximately US$90 million, with a net book value to 
customer ratio of about US$3 1. Moreover, in 2007 AySA had no interest or principal payments, 
and its total depreciation equaled less than 0.5 percent of its operating revenues. 
24. An important result of this capital structure is that the company’s revenue requirements are 
significantly lower than the full cost of providing the service. This will continue to be the case, 
since the Government intends to cover the company’s capital costs-capital expenditures and 
loan and interest payments-for the foreseeable future. 
25. From 2008 to 2012 the Government intends to provide substantial subsidies to AySA, 
through budgetary transfers, to cover the company’s operating costs and a large capital 
expenditure program. Recently the National Congress approved an ARS 1250 million 
(approximately US$412 million) capital improvement budget for CY2008, including transfers 
covering operating costs. 
26. AySA ’s financial indicators. AySA’s financial model projects that its EBITDA*’ will be 
negative in 2008 and will average -$523 million pesos during 2008-2012 (see Figure 13.2). This 
EBITDA: earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization. The term is arrived at by subtracting 
operating expenses from operating revenues. 
164 
<<PAGE 174>>
deterioration of EBITDA is due to a large rise in operating costs while operating revenues 
remain stagnant. The company projects that in 2008 its operating costs will exceed tariff 
revenues by about 45 percent and by an average of 67 percent during 2008-2012. Operating costs 
are projected to rise based on larger volumes of wastewater pumped and treated, increasing 
salaries and the increasing unit costs of inputs (especially electricity and chemicals), while tariffs 
have been frozen at the same level since January 2002. AySA’s management has calculated the 
tariff increase it would need to reverse this trend in the short and medium term. The last graph in 
figure 13.2 shows the resulting cash flow taking into account this increase in tariffs. 
27. AySA intends to undertake a capital expenditure plan that will total about $7.9 billion 
pesos from 2008 to 2012 (see Figure 13.2). These capital expenditures will fall into two broad 
categories. The first category is referred to by the company as “AySA Investments”; these are 
investments for maintenance and renewal of the company’s existing assets. The second category 
corresponds to investments contemplated in AySA’s Master Plan (“Plan Director”) comprising 
new infrastructure investments. The proposed APLs (APL-1 as well as APL-2) include a number 
of key sanitation works identified under AySA’s Master Plan which have been brought into 
agreement with ACUMAR’s Integrated Cleanup Plan for the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin (PISA) 
to ensure that they will serve the dual purpose of expanding sewerage coverage while improving 
water quality of the M-R River Basin. 
28. The government intends to provide AySA with the majority of the funds required for this 
large capital expenditure program on a grant basis (see below). The SSFW indicated that the 
Government intends to provide subsidies to Ay SA through annual budgetary allocations to cover 
any operating deficit and all capital expenditures. The government will borrow from the Bank to 
finance assets that will be operated and maintained by AySA. 
29. AySA will borrow directly from the Brazilian Development Bank (Banco Nacional de 
Desenvolvimento EconGmico e Social, or BNDES) and the National Social Security 
Administration of Argentina (Administracidn Nacional de la Seguridad Social, or ANSES) to 
finance the capital expenditures that will not be met by transfers from the Government (including 
transfers financed through loans to the national government from the World Bank and IADB).

30. AySA 's regulatory framework. AySA's regulatory framework was created by Law No. 
26.221/2007 and comprises the following three main components: (1) concession contract, as yet 
unsigned, between AySA and the Secretariat of Public Works (Secretaria de Obras Ptiblicas, or 
SOP) in MPFIPSY8' (ii) an autoridad de aplicacidn with primary regulatory authority for the 
concession comprising the SSRH, a dependency of MPFIPS, and (iii) a regulatory entity 
(Regulatory Entity for Water and Sanitation (Entidad Reguladora de Agua y Saneamiento, or 
ERAS) and a planning agency (Agencia de Planijkacidn, or APLA), both created out of the 
previous regulatory agency,82 that are effectively advisory bodies to SSRH.83 
31. SSRH's role as the primary regulatory authority is in addition to its policy and planning 
responsibilities as the delegated majority shareholder of AySA. AySA's dual nature as public 
and private entity means it is also subject to both government and external audits. The apparently 
complex inter-relationships of all the entities involved in planning, control, and auditing Ay SA is 
illustrated in figure 13.3.

The regulatory framework and concession contract for AySA are modified versions of those that were in place for 
the concession held by Aguas Argentinas. 
82 Law No. 26.221/2007 dissolved the previous regulatory agency for Aguas Argentinas, Ente Tripartito de Obras y 
Sewicios Sanitarios, or ETOSS) and created the Regulatory Entity for Water and Sanitation (Entidad Reguladora de 
Agua y Saneamiento, or ERAS) and Planning Agency (Agencia de Planificacidn, or APLA) in its stead. In practice, 
most former ETOSS staff were allocated to either ERAS or APLA. 
83 For example, according to Article No. 38 Annex 2 of Law No. 26.221, SSRH has the authority to set tariffs. This 
article also specifies ERAS'S responsibility for the content of the tariffs established by the autoridad de aplicacidn. 
81 
167 
<<PAGE 177>>
32. This regulatory regime is still in the early stages of development and contains a number 
of inherent weaknesses (most notably the dual role of SSRH as executor of ownership and 
regulatory functions). The concession contract has been drafted but not yet signed. ERAS and 
APLA have only recently been established and are still developing their functions. Most 
importantly, the SSRH may not have the staff in place to carry out its broad responsibilities as 
the primary regulatory authority and shareholding authority even if it can steer clear of the 
conflict of interest embedded in its dual mandate. 
33. Tariff regime. Setting tariffs is one of the most important functions of the regulatory 
process. The SSRH, in its role as the autoridad de aplicacidn, is entrusted with the authority to 
set tariffs. In contrast with the regulatory framework in place for the concession held by Aguas 
Argentinas, which mandated full cost recovery, the section of Law No. 26.221 that deals with 
AySA’s economic and tariff regime (Chapter IX. Economic and Tariff System) does not appear 
to require that AySA’s tariffs cover its costs. The draft concession contract between AySA and 
the Government makes some additional provisions regarding AySA’s tariff regime, but it is still 
unclear what portion of its cost of service AySA is supposed to recover through its tariffs and 
how the Government intends to ensure the financial sustainability of AySA through budgetary 
transfers. Water and sewerage tariffs in AySA’s concession are low by the standards of 
neighboring countries (Chile and Uruguay in particular) and represent less than 1.5% of 
household income in the Buenos Aires Metropolitan area. Since few residential customers are 
metered, most consumers pay flat tariffs that are independent of actual consumption but vary by 
location (a zonal coefficient that is high in wealthy areas and lower in low-income 
neighborhoods adds a strong element of pro-poor cross-subsidy to the regime). Both the national 
government and AySA are keenly aware of the need to restore and then maintain AySA’s ability 
to cover its O&M costs through its operating income. 
34. Claims related to AySA’s conduct can be submitted to the company. According to the 
Regulatory Framework, users can file claims related to service quality level or non compliance 
with plans. If AySA doesn’t reply, users can file a claim before ERAS. Any other irregular 
conduct by the company that can affect users’ rights, service provision or the environment can be 
sent to ERAS directly. 
Strengthening AySA, Sector Policies and Institutions 
35. As part of project preparation, the Bank contracted a Due Diligence assessment of the 
Corporate and Regulatory Framework of Agua y Saneamientos Argentinos S.A. (AYSA).’~ The 
assessment found AySA to be a relatively efficient operating company whose financial 
sustainability was undermined by persistently low tariffs rather than operational inefficiencies. 
AySA reports a non-revenue water (NRW) level of 36% (not bad, but could be improved), labor 
productivity of about 1.5 employees per thousand customers (good) and a collection rate of about 
95% (very good). 
36. Under its US$200 million loan in support of AySA’s Master Plan (approved in 
November, 2008), IADB has included an institutional strengthening component designed, i.e., to 
(i) improve personnel management and provide training in key areas; (ii) improve bill collection 
~ .~ 
84 Castalia Strategic Advisors, April, 2008 
168 
<<PAGE 178>>
in low-income areas; (iii) support the efficient incorporation of new assets into AySA’s 
management systems and increase awareness among new customers about appropriate water use 
and payment for service. As part of the proposed Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable 
Development project, the World Bank’s team has also engaged AySA on the topic of 
information campaigns to promote rational water user, given that AySA’s historically low levels 
of household water metering provide little incentive for reducing water consumption. At a wider 
sector level, the World Bank has engaged the Government and civil society with a Progress Note 
on the Water and Sanitation Sectors5 covering topics from sector institutions, financing strategies 
to meet the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) to tariffs. 
Compliance by AySA with the Operator Eligibility Criteria established under the Buenos 
Aires Infrastructure Sustainable Development Project (APL-2) 
37. In June, 2007, the Bank’s board approved a US$270 million loan in support of the second 
stage (APL-2) of the Buenos Aires Infrastructure Sustainable Development Project which 
finances transport, drainage and waterhanitation (WSS) services (among other activities) in 
eligible areas of Buenos Aires province. As part of the PAD for this project, the Bank established 
four eligibility criteria for WSS operators that would be eligible to receive financing under the 
operation: 
Criterion No. 1: The operator has been granted or assigned the responsibility to operate such 
services by the corresponding authority (municipal, provincial or federal) under a veri$able 
procedure that establishes the conditions to guarantee the sustainability of these services. 
Criterion No. 2: There is in effect a regulatory framework that ensures the provision of water 
and sewerage services in a context of economic/Jinancial sustainability, with conditions 
acceptable to the Bank, such framework includes mechanisms for the control of the regulatory 
institutions. 
Criterion No. 3: The operator is in compliance with said regulatory framework, 
Criterion No. 4: The operator has the capacity to operate efficiently and within aframework of 
financial sustainability. 
38. For AySA to be in substantial compliance with these criteria, it would need a signed 
concession contract as well as some mechanism to provide greater certainty of its future financial 
sustainability. For the purposes of the proposed operation, the Government’s development 
program letter (see Annex 1 lE, which explicitly reiterates the Government’s commitment to 
AySA’s financial sustainability) as well as clause 4.11 of IADB’s loan agreement with 
Argentina for its November 2008 loan for AySA (which commits the Government to achieving 
operation, maintenance and administration cost recovery by year 4 of IADB’s operation) are 
deemed to satisfy the second requirement.

Annex 11C: Social Assessment and Public Consultation 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project 
1. The basin is home to Argentina’s largest concentrations of urban poor. Of the 
approximately 3.5 million inhabitants in the basin, 1.2 million live below the poverty line. An 
estimated 10 percent of the total population in the M-R River Basin lives in informal settlements, 
often in flood-prone areas and/or near open garbage dumps. The poorest populations live 
alongside the river and are in constant contact with numerous contaminants ranging from 
untreated organic waste to toxic industrial chemicals. Frequent flooding of the M-R River - 
because of high flows in the rainy season and high levels of water in La Plata River - spreads 
highly polluted waters into informal settlements, exposing the inhabitants to further 
contamination. 
2. With the worsening environmental conditions in the M-R River Basin, and rising social 
awareness of health problems, the complaints and legal claims filed by civil society have been 
growing. Failed attempts by governments to address the situation led to families in several 
communities filing a high-profile Supreme Court class action lawsuit to force government action. 
In 2006, the Supreme Court called on the Government to put into place a concrete action plan to 
improve environmental conditions in the basin. The Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable 
Development Project constitutes part of the Government’s response. 
3. As part of economic analyses carried out for the project, nearly 1000 households were asked 
through surveys about their perception of the current status of the M-R River and La Plata River. 
Results from these surveys indicate that the project will contribute to addressing the problems 
expressed by the respondents. 
4. Social Assessments (SA). Given the complexity of the Project, two complementary social 
assessments were prepared for ACUMAR and AySA during project preparation. ACUMAR’s 
social assessment was a basin-wide study, describing the social baseline conditions which exist 
in the M-R River Basin, identifying stakeholder perceptions, positions, and influence on the 
project, highlighting the existing communication channels, pinpointing the overall social impacts 
of the project, and assessing its various risks. AySA’s social assessment, on the other hand, 
explored the opinion of stakeholders about the proposed infrastructure works to be carried out by 
the company. 
5. Methodology. Both SAs were developed based on available project information and 
consultations with relevant stakeholders. The teams that conducted the SA carried out 45 
meetings with stakeholders including representatives of community organizations, NGOs, the 
industrial sector, provincial and municipal officials, and members of academia. Stakeholders also 
participated in workshops organized by Fundacion Metropolitana and by Universidad de La 
Matanza on the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin, and in workshops for the presentation of the TOR’S 
for the EIAs and the draft EIAs themselves. 
6. Main Findings The main finding of the SAs was that all stakeholders were in favor of the 
project, considering the present scenario whereby given the intervention of the Supreme Court 
and with the assistance by the World Bank, the environmental improvement of the M-R River 
170 
<<PAGE 180>>
Basin becomes achievable. None of the interviewed stakeholders spoke out against the overall 
project. However, critical views about diverse aspects of the project were expressed which need 
to be considered in order to ensure the social viability of the project. A complete list of positive 
and negative impacts is included below, by component: 
7. SA’S findings relating to component 1: The proposed infrastructure works are perceived 
as bringing about a number of positive impacts: improved environmental and health conditions, 
especially for the poor and vulnerable groups in the M-R River Basin; reduction of the 
contamination of the M-R River and groundwater; improved reliability and flexibility in the 
management of the sewerage system in the Buenos Aires Metropolitan Region; increase in the 
capacity of AySA’s trunk sewerage infrastructure which will enable AySA to expand service to 
1.2 million households; mitigation of groundwater contamination due to the reduction of 
effluents discharged by latrines and cesspits; likely employment opportunities and population 
density increases associated with the improved environmental conditions; and reduced costs of 
living associated with the closure of latrines that require costly sludge removal services. These 
benefits are considered significant for achieving social inclusion, mostly for the un-served poor 
and other vulnerable groups. 
8. Some stakeholders canvassed by the SA expressed concerns that the proposed level of 
treatment (preliminary only) prior to discharge via the outfalls would simply displace 
contamination from the M-R River to La Plata River. In response, the technical justification of 
the proposed solution supported by mathematical modeling and an analysis of alternatives 
(presented in Annex 11A) was presented in detail in workshops and meetings. Other stakeholders 
raised the possibility of negative impacts during construction, operation, and maintenance: 
possible temporary disruption of access to residences and commerce due to the implementation 
of sanitation works; the concern that large underground works might structurally affect existing 
buildings in the area; intense traffic of trucks transporting soil removed from underground works 
and waste from the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant which might cause traffic disruption; 
the challenge of managing and properly disposing of solid waste, sand, grease and oil from the 
plant. These impacts have been addressed by the Environmental Management Plan (Annex 10). 
In terms of a possible tariff increase, the impact on low-income populations would be mitigated 
by the cross-subsidies embedded in AySA’s tariff structure (see Annex 11B for further 
discussion). 
9. SA’S findings relating to component 2: The reduction of industrial contamination, by 
means of the implementation of the PRIs and the enforcement of appropriate pollution control 
policies, is perceived by stakeholders as essential to improve environment .and health conditions 
in the M-R River Basin. Other identified benefits were: greater public sector involvement to 
prevent and enforce pollution control policies; improved industrial territorial distribution; 
attraction of new population segments associated with new employment opportunities; improved 
road quality and reduction in maintenance costs faced by local governments due to increased 
control of unregulated industrial waste disposal on roads and in other public areas; and 
improved local industrial competitiveness associated with the adoption of clean production 
mechanisms. 
10. However, doubts were expressed on the industrial component in terms of ACUMAR’s 
capacity to carry out appropriate pollution control policies; unemployment increases and a 
171 
<<PAGE 181>>
reduction in local government revenues which could accompany the possible shut down of 
industries or their transfer to other areas; a reduction of non-skilled jobs and an increase of 
skilled jobs due to the change in industrial technology. As part of the pollution diagnostics under 
component 2, a validation exercise of the list of contaminating industries will also include an 
analysis of the possible social impacts of activities relating to industrial pollution abatement 
within the M-R River Basin. It is worth noting that Argentina has unemployment insurance and 
training programs available to support former workers, in part financed by a US$ 200 million 
World Bank loan approved in 2007 (Loan 7474). 
11. SA’s findings relating to component 3: The improvement in living conditions is 
associated with the proposed urban upgrading and infrastructure provision. Improvements 
perceived by stakeholders include the reduction of flooding, loss of assets and healthcare costs; 
reduction of informality in land use and increase in urban standards. The provision of basic 
infrastructure to un-served vacant pieces of land and the social housing programs to be located 
there are considered opportunities for low-income groups to gain social inclusion. 
12. The main concern on Component 3 is the possible displacement of low-income groups 
settled in areas affected by physical intervention, being unable to adapt to formal housing and 
livelihoods. 
13. SA’s findings relating to overall project implementation: With respect to perceived 
shortcomings during implementation, many stakeholders pointed to the shortage of easily- 
accessible, coordinated and updated information; the lack of opportunities for stakeholder 
participation, including civil society and municipalities; and the weaknesses of the Social 
Participation Commission (Comision de Participacion Social) of ACUMAR. 
14. Some stakeholders contrasted these shortcomings with the role that civil society 
organizations (including 5 entities recognized in the lawsuit as “interested third parties”) is 
exercising under the Supreme Court’s 8 July, 2008 ruling requiring clean-up of the M-R River 
Basin. As a group, these entities have been very active in following up on government actions 
and the advance of the Bank’s loan and it is expected that they will closely follow up on 
implementation issues (including procurement actions) as well. 
15. Measures to address SA findings. Several initiatives have been developed and are 
incorporated in project design to address the above findings. A Communication Management 
Program (see Annex llD) has been designed with SAyDS and ACUMAR, in order to 
significantly improve information flows to key actors and stakeholder groups in the basin about 
the project in a transparent manner, strengthen the credibility of and support for the project, and 
to contribute to the overall consultation, participation and empowerment framework for the 
project. The Communication Management Program includes the creation of a Communication 
Unit in ACUMAR to assume the responsibility for (i) providing adequate and timely information 
prior to consultation events; (ii) maintaining documentation on consultation processes and 
decisions taken; and (iii) providing feedback to concerned stakeholders. In parallel, AySA’s 
communications efforts are improving as demonstrated by its outreach to NGOs and improved 
internal harmonization of project information among its central and regional offices. 
172 
<<PAGE 182>>
16. In addition, in response to the institutional weaknesses of ACUMAR including the limited 
activity of its Social Participation Commission (Cornision de Participacion Social), component 4 
will focus on the institutional strengthening of ACUMAR and the revitalization its Social 
Participation Commission. Close coordination between the Social Participation Component, 
Environmental Unit and Communications Unit will be key to ensure that engagement with key 
stakeholders throughout the implementation of the environmental management plan and 
consultations on social issues remain a two-way dialogue. To this end, terms of reference will be 
developed for the heads of all three units emphasizing the need for coordinated action. The 
Social Participation Component will receive training on management of resettlement issues, 
including those that go beyond physical resettlement and to propose and assess alternatives to 
minimize negative social impacts. The Loan Agreement includes a requirement that the 
Government prepare and implement Resettlement Plans on the basis of the Resettlement Policy 
Framework should actual cases of resettlement arise and to ensure funds are available to carry 
out any resettlement actions in accordance with Bank guidelines. In addition, as shown in Annex 
1 1D (Communications Strategy), US$lOO,OOO have been budgeted to support further diagnostics 
and consultation around social impacts as needed during project implementation. Institutional 
strengthening will be an ongoing activity throughout the project’s lifespan, and will be supported 
through Component 4. 
17. Social as well as environmental considerations will guide the finalization of engineering 
designs under component 1 as well as the preparation of specific interventions under components 
2 and 3. The project’s social assessment process, combining a diagnosis of social context with 
transparent consultation, will be coordinated and integrated with technical, financial, 
environmental and other inputs in further preparing and implementing the project’s components 
and activities. In particular, the social assessment will constitute an important element in 
finalizing the designs for components 2 and 3. ACUMAR will develop a Social Management 
Plan that will integrate and coordinate the approaches suggested in the two social assessments 
and the project’s communication strategy for addressing identified social impacts, risks and 
opportunities under the project as well as stakeholder engagement and communications efforts. 
This plan will comprise of activities, budgets, responsibilities, timelines and indicators, for 
integration of social development issues into the project, on the basis of the following subjects: 
(i) the resettlement framework; (ii) management and mitigation of impacts in the construction 
phase of Component 1; (iii) management and mitigation of impacts in the operation phase of 
Component 1 ; (iv) management and mitigation of impacts associated with industrial conversion 
of Component 2; (v) monitoring; and (vi) consultation, communication and disclosure. 
18. Public Consultation and Communication. Consultation and communication activities 
have been carried out during preparation and will be continued during project implementation. 
As part of the project preparation process, extensive stakeholder consultation has been carried 
out using formal as well as informal methodologies (see Annex 10 for more details). Additional 
activities are planned for project implementation. Funding for these activities has been 
considered as part of component 4. 
19. Information on the project has been made publicly available through public disclosure 
events and workshops. Informal focus group discussions have been conducted with NGOs, local 
government, affected people and beneficiaries at various points during preparation and appraisal. 
173 
<<PAGE 183>>
It is worth mentioning some of these events: surveys and focus groups during the “willingness to 
pay” study for the economic analysis (see Annex 9), workshops for the consultation of the terms 
of reference for the EIAs on 10 July, 2008, and dissemination of the results of the EIAs on 7 
November, 2008, AySA meetings previous to the EIA results workshop with 11 NGOs, World 
Bank meetings and exchanges of correspondence with several NGOs, and multiple interviews 
with stakeholders carried out during the two social assessment studies. The project EIAs have 
been made available through the ACUMAR and AySA websites as well as through the Bank’s 
Infoshop. 
20. In addition to the project specific consultation and disclosure efforts, it is important to 
recognize that both Ay SA and ACUMAR have also established social outreach departments 
within their organizational structure. These teams have been established to be in constant contact 
with the local communities, and are used to facilitate communication tools. ACUMAR, for 
example, has established a network of approximately 300 community and environmental NGOs 
that are part of its broader communications strategy about the issues facing the M-R River Basin. 
This network provides an opportunity for information disclosure not just on the project but on a 
wide array of development issues facing the basin. AySA business practice for planning of civil 
works includes disclosure of information and stakeholder consultations which will be applied to 
the civil works in APL-lwhich are at or before pre-feasibility stage (e.g., right bank collector). 
21. Involuntary resettlement (OP 4.12): No resettlement is expected as a result of these 
project components. The design of the sewage collector routes has been conceived to minimize 
adverse impacts and avoid resettlement, and no displacement is projected regarding industrial 
and urban improvements. The Left Bank collector and the Bajo Costanero By-pass collector will 
be constructed by tunneling techniques (rather than open-trench construction) which limit above- 
ground works to a relatively small number of access shafts. Moreover, the Riachuelo pre- 
treatment plant is located in Dock Sud on uninhabited land. However, as a precaution, since the 
physical interventions of components 2 and 3 of the project have not yet been fully determined, a 
Resettlement Policy Framework has been prepared. As pilot and initial phase interventions for 
components 2 and 3 are selected, minimizing resettlement will be one of the selection criteria. 
22. Additional reputational risks to the Bank could materialize if ACUMAR, subnational 
governments or private firms in the basin carry out actions broadly in line with basin clean-up 
under the PISA entirely with their own funds without full consideration of social and 
environmental impacts (including relocation or restoration of livelihoods). 
23, Mechanisms for mediation, conflict resolution, and redress: There are several 
alternative mechanisms at various levels available to citizens who wish to seek information or 
complain if they feel the project does not adequately address their needs or concerns. At the 
lowest level, there are project consultation mechanisms through the Social Participation 
Commission (created within ACUMAR, with the objective of channeling comments and 
proposals fi-om civil society organizations to ACUMAR’s management), as well as submissions 
to the specific administration unit or institution (ACUMAR, SAyDS, or AySA). While some 
civil society organizations have expressed concern about the efficacy of ACUMAR’s Social 
Participation Commission, ACUMAR’s new management (in place since December, 2008) is 
committed to strengthening its outreach. 
174 
<<PAGE 184>>
24. At the next level, claims related to the implementation of the PISA can be presented to the 
Collective Body ("Cuerpo Colegiado) created by the Supreme Court's final decision in the 
MendozaS6 case. This body, is lead by the National Ombudsman and composed of the 5 NGOs 
participating in the case as interested third parties. This body is in charge of monitoring 
ACUMARs compliance with the Supreme Court decision. As this Monitoring Body has only 
recently been created, uncertainties about its capacity to handle these submissions remain. For 
the same reason, it is also still uncertain if claimants will still have the possibility to go to court 
directly or if they will have to rely on this Body to make the decision. On the other hand, 
according to the Supreme Court decision in the above-mentioned case, any claim submitted 
before this judge can be appealed directly to the Supreme Court. 
25. Alternatively, people can directly file "amparo'l claims (restraining orders) before local, 
provincial or national courts, which may eventually be redirected to the Federal Judge of 
Quilmes. 
26. Claims related to AySA's conduct will be submitted before the company using different 
ways of communication (telephone, office visits or email). According to AySA's Regulatory 
Framework, users can present claims related to service quality levels or non-compliance with 
plans. If AySA doesn't reply, users can file a claim with the Regulatory Entity for Water and 
Sanitation (Entidad Reguladora de Agua y Saneamiento, or ERAS). Any other irregular conduct 
of the company that can affect users' rights, their service or the environment can be directed to 
ERAS directly. 
86 2003 lawsuit: Mendoza Cause, Beatriz Silva and others against the National Government and others.

to the overall consultation, participation and empowerment framework for the project.

4. In order to ensure that these objectives are met, the following activities will be developed 
under the project:

strategy and to act as a focal point and source of information for all stakeholders; 
b. Implementation of a communication strategy targeting key audiences in 3 phases:

management of the basin and to provide specific information about the project to 
be financed by the World Bank. 
o Phase 2 - reach local communities and beneficiaries with information about the

7. Today, people living in the basin area perceive it as highly polluted, and the first association 
that comes to their mind when asked about the Matanza-Riachuelo River is “contamination”. 
With the implementation of the strategy, the project will act to promote a perception (once the 
contamination level in the M-R River is reduced) that the project contributes to improving the 
quality of life of the Basin’s residents. 
8. Secondly, the idea of clean-up of the Matanza-Riachuelo River is not new in Argentina and 
there is a history of failed attempts to do so. This has created a feeling of mistrust and lack of 
confidence towards any initiative that promises a clean river. To manage this perception, the 
communications team in ACUMAR will base the communication actions on facts rather than on 
promises. The strategy is to slowly gain credibility among the beneficiaries and other key 
audiences by promoting the concept that sustainable development of the basin is being achieved 
through concrete actions and works of reducing contamination from domestic sources thereby 
improving the quality of the water in the river, as well as reducing industrial contamination and 
improving solid waste management in the basin. 
9. Thirdly, in view of the reality of high contamination affecting the quality of life of the 
basin’s residents, the project will promote participation mechanisms to include the different key 
stakeholders in the sustainable management of the basin. 
10. In terms of implementation, the communication strategy for the project has initially 
identified 4 clear stages that will take place throughout the project’s life. These stages will be 
further adjusted once the Communications Director for ACUMAR is hired. 
a. Re-positioning. In this initial phase, the priority is given to comply with the ruling of the 
Supreme Court, to organize the communication department of ACUMAR and to execute 
the initial phase of the communication strategy for the period July 2009 - February, 2010. 
b. Positioning. Start activities through three communication axes: 
0 Improve quality of life in the basin 
Promote environmental clean up through sustainable development, 
Promote participation in sustainable management of the basin 
c. Leadership. Profile ACUMAR as leading the implementation of the Project. Define 
communication strategy for year the 20 10. 
177 
<<PAGE 187>>
d. Consolidation of Leadership - after the main works have been initiated and the project 
is delivering on its promises, from the communication perspective it is time to launch the 
vision of a cleaner basin and a strong commitment to promoting the environment and 
sustainable development. It is in this phase when the project will take the leadership in 
terms of communication, to generate a vision of the clean basin which will include active 
participation of the neighbors and more massive ways of communications will be used.

Annex 11E: Letter of Development Program 
ARGENTINA: Matanza-Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development Project 
(Translation) 
Buenos Aires, 16 April 2009 
Reference: Matanza Riachuelo Basin 
Sustainable Development Project (P105680) 
Mr. President 
International Bank for Reconstruction and Development 
Robert Zoellik 
I write to you with regards to the loan operation referenced (above) that has been negotiated over 
the past year and a half between the technical teams of the Bank and our government, so that it 
might contribute to the implementation of policies that the Argentine Nation is undertaking. 
One of these policies is the recovery of the Matanza Riachuelo Basin, through the Integrated 
Environmental Clean-up Plan (Plan Integral de Saneamiento Ambiental, PISA) which. requires 
prompt implementation. 
For this reason, our President, Ms. Cristina Fernandez de Kirchner, held a meeting in November 
2008 with the World Bank’s Vice-president, Ms. Pamela Cox, in which she shared this concern 
and expressed her decision to undertake such actions, elaborating in detail the policies that have 
been instrumental for the sector. 
It is worth remembering that the Argentine Republic has achieved one of the goals imposed by 
this government: grow in order to honor our obligations. 
It is worth mentioning that over the last four years, the Argentine Republic has paid to the 
International Bank for Reconstruction and Development, an amount of approximately US$5,13 1 
million in interest and capital, for the operations with this organization, such that today there are 
no arrears of any kind. 
In addition, it is worth mentioning that over the last five years Argentina achieved a fiscal 
surplus as well as a surplus in its balance of payments, thereby reaching a unique milestone in 
the modern history of our country. 
With regard to the loan request presented by the National Government to the Bank for the 
financing of the “Matanza Riachuelo Basin Sustainable Development” Program, overall 
responsibility for which will lie with the Secretariat of Environment and Sustainable 
Development (Secretaria de Ambiente y Desarrollo Sustentable, SAyDS) of the Chief of Cabinet 
Office (Jefatura de Gabinete de Ministros), we would like to inform you of the guiding 
principles that will allow the expected results to be achieved. 
179 
<<PAGE 189>>
First of all, we inform you that the National Government enacted Law No. 26.168 that created 
the Matanza Riachuelo Basin Authority (ACUMAR) as an overarching interjurisdictional body 
that brings institutionality and a new management tool to the area of the Basin. In the articles of 
this Law, there is a special emphasis on the creation of a Social Participation Commission 
(Comisibn de Participacibn Social) with consultative functions under the auspices of the 
Matanza Riachuelo Basin Authority. This commission will be comprised of representatives of 
organizations with interests in the area. 
Among other things, this Basin Authority has the mission of implementing the Integrated 
Environmental Clean-up Plan (PISA), that was developed by the Secretariat of Environment and 
Sustainable Development jointly with the Province of Buenos Aires and the Autonomous City of 
Buenos Aires. The program, to which this letter refers, will be partially financed by the Bank and 
has been conceived within this framework. 
The main objective of this program is to improve the quality of life of the population as well as 
the environmental recovery of the Matanza Riachuelo Basin. At the same time, the program will 
offer a cost-effective solution that is sustainable over time for the safe disposal of sewage from a 
large part of the Buenos Aires Metropolitan Area which today lacks this basic service. 
The National Government considers this program a high priority since it will directly benefit 3.5 
million inhabitants of the Basin and - more generally -- the entire population of Greater Buenos 
Aires. 
To finance the program, it was decided to use the APL (Adaptable Program Lending) tool since 
this allows for flexibility to carry out the investments in the short term and to prepare the 
conditions for future investments in the medium term. By virtue of this, we request US$840 
million from the Bank for the first stage APL-1. 
The first stage of the program will contribute directly to the improvement of sewerage services in 
the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin, the reduction in untreated industrial discharges to surface water 
bodies, the improvement of the urban drainage system, land use in the basin, and the institutional 
strengthening of ACUMAR, given its fundamental role as basin authority and as an organization 
that coordinates the actions necessary to improve the basin in fundamental ways with the 
objective of increasing the social inclusion of its inhabitants and of restoring the environment. 
Technically, for the environmental recovery of the Matanza-Riachuelo Basin, the strategy 
consists of the removal of pollutant loads and the progressive withdrawal of identified point 
source pollution discharges, as well as the industrial discharges with the largest relative 
contribution of pollutants, in support of a final objective of reaching dissolved oxygen levels 
higher than 2 mg/L along the length of the Matanza-Riachuelo River. 
The program, as we mentioned before, will be under the responsibility of the Secretariat of 
Environment and Sustainable Development (Secretaria de Ambiente y Desarrollo Sustentable, 
SAyDS) of the Chief of Cabinet Office (Jefatura de Gabinete de Ministros), and divided into two 
Macro-components. Macro-component 1 , with only one component titled component 1 - 
180 
<<PAGE 190>>
Sanitation; and Macro-component 2, composed of three components identified as component 
2 - Industrial pollution abatement, component 3 - Environmental territorial management, and 
finally component 4 - Institutional Strengthening. Macro-component 1 will implemented entirely 
by AySA and Macro-component 2 by SAyDS and ACUMAR as the interjurisdictional entity 
responsible for implementing PISA which will vet actions that will be developed under the 
program, determining in this way the legal, political and institutional framework for the same. 
It is important to highlight that during the implementation of APL-1, part of the key structural 
works necessary for the transport, treatment and final disposal of the pollutant flows that are 
today channeled to the Matanza Riachuelo River will be built. These works include the Left 
Bank collector, the Costanera Bypass collector, the Riachuelo preliminary treatment plant (with 
its pumping stations), the Riachuelo outfall, the industrial collector; the Right Bank collector (or 
a possible technical alternative that could be agreed in its place). The above mentioned works 
will have the technical consent of ACUMAR and SAyDS and will be sent for consideration to 
the Bank in order to determine their ultimate feasibility. 
During the second stage of the APL, these works are expected to complemented by others, also 
structural, that will allow the recovery of water quality on the shores of La Plata River (Baja 
Costanera Expansion Collector) and to complement the final disposal system (pumping station 
and Berazategui sub-aquatic outfall). In this second stage, it is planned to extend significantly the 
primary and secondary sewerage networks in AySA’s operating area within the Matanza 
Riachuelo Basin. 
Regarding component 2, the intervention strategy is based on the reduction of pollutant loads 
produced by industrial discharges to the basin, through the development and implementation of 
the Industrial Conversion Plans (Planes de Reconversidn Industrial) for those industries that will 
be declared contaminating agents (agentes contarninantes). 
Component 3 aims to improve public policies related to environmental territorial management of 
the basin and will be implemented through the design of a territorial master plan agreed with 
corresponding jurisdictions and direct actions on the ground. 
Component 4, ACUMAR institutional strengthening, is fundamental to the development of the 
entire program, since it will allow for the implementation of institutional strengthening activities 
that structure the organization in a way that allows it to reach the objectives set-out in the PISA 
with efficacy and efficiency. 
It is worth highlighting the importance the National Government attaches to having AySA carry 
out component 1 in its capacity as the water supply and sanitation utility for the Buenos Aires 
Metropolitan area and the City of Buenos Aires, where most human activity in the Matanza 
Riachuelo Basin are located. It is worth noting that 70% of all water supply and sewerage 
investments under AySA’s master plan (Plan Director) are to be implemented in the Matanza 
Riachuelo basin. 
It is the policy of the National Government to continue actions conducive to achieving AySA’s 
operational sustainability, as well as, as you well know, to take on the program of works 
181 
<<PAGE 191>>
necessary to connect the remaining population that today lacks water supply and sewerage 
connections -- the central objective recommended by all International Organizations, especially 
the United Nations. 
In this context, the Government will ensure that AySA has the necessary resources for financing 
its operations and maintenance through its own revenues or through national government 
transfers. 
Finally, we would like to draw attention to the support provided by the Inter-American 
Development Bank for the sanitation policies carried out by the National Government through 
AySA, given that, on November 5th its Board of Directors unanimously approved a credit of 250 
million Dollars as part of a total loan of 900 million Dollars. 
With nothing further to add, kind regards, 
Dr. Homero Maximo Bibiloni 
Secretary of Environment and Sustainable Development
